The monotonicity of the Franz-Parisi potential is equivalent with Low-degree MMSE lower bounds
Konstantinos Tsirkas, Leda Wang, Ilias Zadik

Abstract
Over the last decades, two distinct approaches have been instrumental to our understanding of the computational complexity of statistical estimation. The statistical physics literature predicts algorithmic hardness through local stability and monotonicity properties of the Franz–Parisi (FP) potential [ 12 , 13 ] , while the mathematically rigorous literature characterizes hardness via the limitations of restricted algorithmic classes, most notably low-degree polynomial estimators [ 15 ] . For many inference models, these two perspectives yield strikingly consistent predictions, giving rise to a long-standing open problem of establishing a precise mathematical relationship between them. Recent works [ 5 , 8 ] addressed this question in the setting of detection, showing that for broad classes of models the success of low-degree polynomials is governed by an area criterion involving the annealed FP potential. While this provides a rigorous bridge between the two frameworks, the resulting criterion differs from the monotonicity-based conditions traditionally emphasized in the physics literature, reflecting fundamental distinctions between detection and estimation tasks. In this work, we show that for estimation problems the power of low-degree polynomials is equivalent to the monotonicity of the annealed FP potential for a broad family of Gaussian additive models (GAMs) with signal-to-noise ratio λ \lambda . In particular, subject to a low-degree conjecture for GAMs, our results imply that the polynomial-time limits of these models are directly implied by the monotonicity of the annealed FP potential, in conceptual agreement with predictions from the physics literature dating back to the 1990s. Specifically, letting ℱ ann , λ \mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ann},\lambda} denote the annealed FP potential, we prove that the optimal degree- D D correlation satisfies, for all D = O ⁡ ( poly ⁡ ( log ⁡ n ) ) D=O(\mathrm{poly}(\log n)) , the approximate fixed point equation, Corr P 0 ≤ D ​ ( λ + d d ​ q ​ F ann , λ | q = q ⁡ ( D ) ) 2 ≈ q ⁡ ( D ) , \mathrm{Corr}^{\leq D}_{P_{0}}\!\left(\lambda+\left.\frac{d}{dq}F_{\mathrm{ann},\lambda}\right|_{q=q(D)}\right)^{2}\approx q(D), where q ⁡ ( D ) q(D) denotes the e − D e^{-D} -quantile of the overlap between two independent draws from the prior. In particular, the condition d d ​ q ​ F ann , λ | q = q ⁡ ( D ) ≥ 0 \left.\frac{d}{dq}F_{\mathrm{ann},\lambda}\right|_{q=q(D)}\geq 0 is equivalent to all degree- D D polynomial estimators achieving correlation at most q ⁡ ( D ) q(D) at signal-to-noise ratio λ \lambda . This establishes the first rigorous equivalence between a physics-inspired monotonicity criterion and low-degree estimation lower bounds, and may be viewed as a low-degree analogue of the classical I–MMSE relationship [ 14 ] .



1
Introduction

Over the last decades, there has been a strong effort to understand the computational complexity of Bayesian statistical estimation tasks. Due to the average-case nature of such tasks, it appears well beyond our current mathematical abilities to characterize their hardness phases based on standard complexity theory assumptions (such as
\mathcal{P}\neq\mathcal{NP}
) and for this reason researchers from various backgrounds have produced different approaches to the questions.

MMSE lower bounds against low-degree polynomials

One prominent direction in theoretical statistics and theoretical computer science studies the limitations of restricted classes of estimators, often referred to as unconditional lower bounds. Among these frameworks, low-degree polynomial estimators have played a particularly central role in predicting computational limits. While challenging to study in the context of estimation, the pioneering work of
[
30
]
provided some key techniques on how to bound the minimum mean squared error (MMSE) among all low-degree polynomials for some classes of Gaussian additive models (GAM), where for some prior
P_{0}
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
one observes for some signal-to-noise ratio
\lambda
,

Y=\sqrt{\lambda}X+Z,

(1.1)

for the signal
X\sim P_{0}
and independent noise
Z\sim N(0,I_{N})
. Following this, more works have provided MMSE lower bounds for other families of GAMs or Bernoulli models, e.g.,
[
31
,
21
,
20
,
10
]
. It is important to highlight that the belief that low-degree polynomials are powerful in statistical tasks has culimanted with the an influential “low-degree conjecture” in the similar detection (hypothesis testing) setting
[
15
]
, which posits that for many sufficiently “nice” detection problems, degree-
D=O(\log N)
polynomial tests match the performance of the optimal polynomial-time algorithm. Although no direct analogue of this conjecture has yet been formally articulated for statistical estimation, it is widely expected that a corresponding conjecture will be posed soon, at least for GAMs.

Monotonicity of the Franz-Parisi potential

Another highly influential perspective on the computational complexity of statistical inference comes from the statistical physics community (see e.g.,
[
36
]
for a survey). From this physics viewpoint, since the optimal estimator that achieves the minimum mean-squared error (MMSE) can be obtained by sampling from the Bayesian posterior, the optimal time-efficient algorithm should correspond to a physically natural reversible dynamics on the parameter space—such as Glauber or Langevin dynamics—whose stationary distribution is the posterior itself. Understanding the computational limits of this task is therefore reduced to analyzing whether such dynamics can efficiently sample from the posterior or become trapped in bottlenecks (or “metastable” states).

In a seminal work, Franz and Parisi
[
12
,
13
]
introduced the Franz–Parisi (FP) potential, parameterized by an overlap variable, as a tool for predicting the behavior of such dynamics. The FP potential captures the local geometry of the posterior landscape around configurations of a given overlap, and its shape is used to predict whether local dynamics will rapidly mix or instead become trapped. In particular, the physics prediction is that when the FP potential ceases to be decreasing, the dynamics become trapped in a metastable state, and the resulting estimator achieves correlation with the signal equal to the overlap at which the FP potential attains its first local minimum.

For a GAM the FP potential takes the following form (see
[
5
]
for details),

\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}(q):=-\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\mu,Z\sim N(0,I_{N}),Y=\sqrt{\lambda}X+Z}\log\mathbb{E}_{X^{\prime}\sim\mu}1_{\langle X,X^{\prime}\rangle=q}\exp\left(-\|Y-\sqrt{\lambda}X^{\prime}\|^{2}_{2}/2\right),q\in[-1,1]

Due to its complicated form, physicists typically study tractable approximations of the FP potential to make concrete predictions. Two approximations play a particularly important role: the replica-symmetric (RS) approximation and the annealed approximation. The RS approximation has been shown to accurately characterize the behavior of the Approximate Message Passing (AMP) algorithm in some spiked matrix models, in the sense that the asymptotic correlation of AMP iterates converges to the location of the first local minimum of the RS potential (see, e.g.,
[
25
, Theorem 1.1.]
and references therein). The annealed approximation, obtained by applying Jensen’s inequality to the FP potential and exchanging the logarithm and expectation, leads to a significantly simpler quantity known as the annealed FP potential (see
[
5
, Proposition 2.3]
), given by

\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ann},\lambda}(q):=-\log\mathbb{E}_{X,X^{\prime}\sim\mu,X\perp\!\!\!\perp X^{\prime}}1_{\langle X,X^{\prime}\rangle=q}\exp\left(\lambda\langle X,X^{\prime}\rangle\right),q\in[-1,1].

A mathematical relation? Obstacles and prior results

Strikingly, for many estimation tasks, the computational hardness predictions obtained from low-degree polynomial methods agree with those arising from the physics perspective based on the monotonicity of the Franz–Parisi (FP) potential. This empirical alignment naturally leads to the central question motivating this work, which has remained an open puzzle in the field:

Is there a precise mathematical connection between

the monotonicity criterion of the FP potential

and the MMSE achieved by low-degree polynomial estimators?

Unfortunately, pursuing such a connection using the original (or quenched) FP potential, defined in (
1
), turns out to be highly delicate and, in general, invalid without substantial restrictions. The underlying issue is that there also exist canonical estimation problems—even within the class of Gaussian additive models—for which the quenched FP prediction is provably incorrect. A notable example is Bernoulli sparse tensor PCA, where
X=x^{\otimes t},
for some
t\geq 2,
with
x
drawn uniformly from
\{v\in\{0,1/\sqrt{k}\}^{N}:\|v\|_{0}=k\}
(see also section
A
)
1
1

1

It is widely believed that even simple tensor PCA with spherical prior is a counterexample, but only low or high temperature variants of the quenched FP prediction appears to be rigorously proven to fail so far
[
2
]
.
. In this setting, the quenched FP potential predicts computational hardness deep inside a regime where the estimation task is known to be computationally easy, whereas the low-degree polynomial prediction is conjectured to be optimal (this is an implication of the bottleneck proven in
[
9
, Section 3.3]
). Yet, on a positive light, a motivating result appeared a few years ago in
[
25
]
, showing that, for the (biased, i.i.d.) spiked matrix model within the class of GAMs, the MMSE performance of Approximate Message Passing (AMP) coincides with that of
O(1)
-degree polynomial estimators. Since the asymptotic MMSE of AMP is characterized by the replica-symmetric (RS) approximation of the FP potential, this result raised the hope that a broader and more systematic theory connecting FP-based predictions and low-degree estimation limits might exist. However, despite its conceptual appeal and exact nature, this correspondence is currently limited to biased i.i.d. rank-1 spiked matrix GAMs.

An equivalence for detection settings by another FP criterion

An important step in this direction was taken by Bandeira et al.
[
5
]
, who proved that for the detection variant of GAM—where the task is to distinguish between pure noise and a GAM—the power of low-degree polynomial tests is characterized by an area criterion involving the annealed FP potential defined in (
1
)
2
2

2

Specifically, the criterion asks whether the area under the exponential of the annealed FP potential near the origin diverges.
. Notably, the use of the annealed FP potential is essential: for problems such as sparse tensor PCA, the corresponding area criterion applied to the quenched FP potential again fails to match the predictions of low-degree methods. More recently,
[
8
]
significantly extended this connection beyond the class of GAMs.

While these results provide a partial resolution of our motivating question in the setting of detection, they leave open whether the area-based criterion aligns with the monotonicity-based intuition emphasized in the physics literature. It turns out that the monotonicity of the annealed FP curve is, in fact, not the right criterion for detection hardness and that happens for a fundamental reason. Many GAMs—including again sparse tensor PCA—exhibit a detection–estimation gap, in which detection is possible in polynomial time while estimation is conjectured to be computationally hard for low-degree polynomial methods. In such regimes, routine calculations can prove that the monotonicity criterion correctly predicts hardness for estimation but incorrectly predicts hardness for detection, whereas the area-based criterion correctly captures the detection low-degree hardness threshold. It is therefore suggestive that if one wants to create a relevant theory connecting the monotonicity of the FP potential with low-degree polynomials one needs to restrict themselves in the context of estimation.

1.1
Contributions

The main contribution of this work is the establishment of an exact quantitative relationship between the MMSE achievable by low-degree polynomial estimators and the monotonicity of the annealed Franz–Parisi (FP) potential for a broad class of Gaussian additive models (GAMs). We name the class of GAMs our results apply
“low-order cumulant-nonnegative”
GAMs. This result yields the first rigorous equivalence between two a priori distinct hardness predictions: those arising from the monotonicity criterion of the annealed FP potential in statistical physics, and those obtained from low-degree MMSE lower bounds in theoretical statistics and computer science. In particular, our results provide the first formal setting in which the physics monotonicity criterion is shown to characterize low-degree estimation limits.

To state our results, recall that for any GAM with signal-to-noise ratio
\lambda>0
, prior
P_{0}
, and degree parameter
D\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
, the degree-
D
MMSE and degree-
D
correlation are defined as

\mathrm{MMSE}^{\leq D}_{P_{0}}(\lambda):=\inf_{f_{1},\dots,f_{n}\in\mathbb{R}[Y]_{\leq D}}\mathbb{E}\!\left[\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(f_{i}(Y)-X_{i}\big)^{2}\right],\mathrm{Corr}^{\leq D}_{P_{0}}(\lambda):=\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}g_{i}\in\mathbb{R}[Y]_{\leq D}\\
\mathbb{E}[g_{i}(Y)^{2}]=1\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}\!\left[g_{i}(Y)X_{i}\right]^{2}},

which are linked by the identity (see Lemma
6.8
),

\mathrm{MMSE}^{\leq D}_{P_{0}}(\lambda)=\mathbb{E}_{X\sim P_{0}}[\|X\|^{2}]-\big(\mathrm{Corr}^{\leq D}_{P_{0}}(\lambda)\big)^{2}.

Our main result can be informally summarized as follows. For simplicity, we state it under the assumption that the overlap
\langle X,X^{\prime}\rangle
, where
X,X^{\prime}
are i.i.d. from
P_{0}
, admits a continuous distribution with a differentiable PDF, so that derivatives of the annealed FP potential are well-defined.

Theorem 1.1

(Informal, see Theorems
2.7
,
2.9
.)
.

For any low-order cumulant-nonnegative GAM with prior
P_{0}
, for any degree

0<D\leq\mathrm{poly}(\log n)
and any SNR
\lambda>0
, the optimal degree-
D
correlation satisfies

\big(\mathrm{Corr}^{\leq D}_{P_{0}}\big)^{2}\!\left(\lambda+\left.\frac{d}{dq}\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ann},\lambda}(q)\right|_{q=q(D)}\right)\approx q(D),

where the approximation hides only polylogarithmic factors in
n
, and
q(D)
denotes the
e^{-D}
-quantile of the overlap
|\langle X,X^{\prime}\rangle|
(see Definition
2.1
).

A direct corollary provides the precise equivalence between the physics monotonicity criterion and low-degree estimation hardness.

Corollary 1.2

(Informal)
.

For any “low-order cumulant-nonnegative” GAM with prior
P_{0}
and SNR
\lambda
, and any
0<D\leq\mathrm{poly}(\log n)
, the following are equivalent up to polylogarithmic factors in
n
:

\left.\frac{d}{dq}\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ann},\lambda}(q)\right|_{q=q(D)}\geq 0\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\big(\mathrm{Corr}^{\leq D}_{P_{0}}(\lambda)\big)^{2}\leq q(D).

We briefly describe the correspondence at a conceptual level. In the classical physics picture based on the Franz–Parisi (FP) potential, the prediction is that a time-efficient algorithm—modeled by local or reversible dynamics—becomes trapped at the overlap value
q_{1}
corresponding to the first local minimum of the FP potential, while an algorithm with unbounded computational resources eventually reaches the global minimum at overlap
q_{2}
.

Our results
prove
a precise quantitative analogue of this picture is correct in the low-degree framework (see also Figure
1
(a)). Suppose we quantify computational power by the maximum degree
D=1,2,\ldots
of polynomial estimators one can use. For any SNR
\lambda
, we examine the monotonicity of the annealed FP potential
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ann},\lambda}
at the overlap values
q(D)
.

If

\left.\frac{d}{dq}\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ann},\lambda}(q)\right|_{q=q(D)}\leq 0

then the physics interpretation is that the dynamics
descends a hill
and hence, this is locally an “easy” phase. From Corollary
1.2
, this phase corresponds exactly to

\big(\mathrm{Corr}^{\leq D}_{P_{0}}(\lambda)\big)^{2}\geq q(D),

meaning that degree-
D
polynomials are powerful enough to achieve squared correlation at least
q(D)
.

Conversely, if

\left.\frac{d}{dq}\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ann},\lambda}(q)\right|_{q=q(D)}\geq 0,

the physics prediction is that the dynamics must
climb a hill
and therefore becomes trapped, signaling computational “hardness”. In exact correspondence, from Corollary
1.2
, this phase maps to
\big(\mathrm{Corr}^{\leq D}_{P_{0}}(\lambda)\big)^{2}\leq q(D)
,
which expresses the inability of degree-
D
polynomials to achieve squared correlation
q(D)
.

In this sense, the physics monotonicity prediction at overlap
q(D)
admits a complete and rigorous interpretation in the language of low-degree estimation. Moreover, the correspondence is bidirectional: the behavior of the low-degree correlation function as a function of
D
can be translated back into precise monotonicity properties of the annealed FP potential (see Figure
1
(b)).

(a)
This is a pictorial representation of the annealed potential
q\mapsto F_{\mathrm{ann},\lambda}(q)
as a function of the overlap parameter
q
(for fixed SNR
\lambda
).
Based on Theorem
1.1
, and choosing points
q=q(D_{i}),i=1,2
where the curve is locally decreasing/increasing, we can conclude that
(\mathrm{Corr}_{P_{0}}^{\leq D_{i}})^{2}(\lambda)
is (roughly) more/less than
q(D_{i})

(b)
This is a pictorial representation of
(\mathrm{Corr}_{P_{0}}^{\leq D})^{2}(\lambda)
and
q(D)
as functions of
D
(for fixed SNR
\lambda
).
Based on Theorem
1.1
, we can use the relative position of these two curves to conclude the sign of the derivative of
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ann}}
.

Figure 1
:
Pictorial representations of our equivalence.

Remark 1.3
.

A key technical feature of our results is the role played by the
e^{-D}
-quantiles
q(D)
of the overlap
|\langle X,X^{\prime}\rangle|
. As mentioned above, these quantiles provide the exact parametrization required to map the overlap value at which the annealed FP potential is increasing/decreasing to the corresponding low-degree MMSE upper/lower bound. We emphasize that the importance of overlap quantiles in the annealed FP framework was first identified by
[
5
]
, where it was shown—roughly speaking—that boundedness of the area under
\exp(-\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ann},\lambda}(q))
on the interval
[-q(D),q(D)]
characterizes low-degree detection hardness.

Remark 1.4
.

While Theorem
1.1
is stated for continuous priors, we also establish an analogous result for discrete priors by replacing derivatives of the annealed FP potential with appropriate discrete difference operators.

We defer the precise set of technical assumptions on the GAMs, and specifically the assumption on low-order cumulant non-negativity, to the main body of the paper. We note here, however, that these assumptions are satisfied by several canonical GAMs studied in the recent literature. As an illustration, we apply our main theorem to recover existing and prove new state-of-the-art low-degree MMSE lower bounds via direct, black-box applications of our framework. In all cases, the proofs reduce to bounding the overlap quantiles
q(D)
of
|\langle X,X^{\prime}\rangle|
.

In particular, we obtain from our method tight
O(\log n)
-degree MMSE hardness results below the conjectured algorithmic threshold for:
(a) tensor PCA with prior
X=x^{\otimes r}
, where the coordinates of
x
are either i.i.d. Gaussian (Section
3.1.1
) or i.i.d.
\mathrm{Rad}(k/n)
with
\widetilde{\omega}(\sqrt{n})=k=o(n)
(Section
3.1.2
); and
(b) the sparse clustering model with
X=\xi\mu^{\top}
, where
\xi
has i.i.d.
\mathrm{Rad}(1/2)
entries and
\mu
has i.i.d. coordinates distributed as the product of a standard Gaussian and a
\mathrm{Ber}(s/p)
random variable with
\widetilde{\omega}(\sqrt{p})=s=o(p)
(Section
3.2.1
).

Finally, we address the natural question of whether our results could be extended to the
quenched
FP potential; such an inquiry is well-motivated, because as we mentioned above the annealed FP potential is often treated in physics as a mere tractable proxy for its quenched counterpart (often the quenched FP potential is challenging to calculate). While such an extension is likely possible for many of the GAMs our work applies to, we must emphasize that there exist GAMs for which our equivalence holds, yet the monotonicity of the quenched FP potential is
not in agreement
with the behavior of the low-degree MMSE. More specifically, there are cases for which the monotonicity of the annealed FP potential successfully captures the low-degree MMSE behavior while the monotonicity of the quenched FP potential does not. We present and analyze a specific such counterexample in Section
A
(a truncated version of Rademacher sparse 3-tensor PCA). We believe that this unexpected “computational success” of the annealed FP potential—where it outperforms the quenched potential in predicting algorithmic hardness—represents a significant open question for future work.

2
Main Result: Equivalence between FP monotonicity and low-degree MMSE

As mentioned in the Introduction, of crucial importance to our work is the quantiles of the overlap between two i.i.d. copies from the prior
P_{0}
of a GAM. To present our results, we start by defining the quantile function of the absolute value of the overlap
\langle X,X^{\prime}\rangle
between two i.i.d. draws of the prior.

Definition 2.1

(Quantile function)
.

Let
X,X^{\prime}
i.i.d. draws from the prior
P_{0}
. For any
D>0
, we define the quantile
q(D)
by

q(D)\coloneqq\inf\left\{y\in\mathbb{R}\mid\mathbb{P}(|\langle X,X^{\prime}\rangle|\leq y)\geq 1-e^{-D}\right\}.

For clarity reasons, we first present our equivalence results for the case
P_{0}
admits a continuous and differentiable PDF in
\mathbb{R}^{n}.
We then describe in the last subsection, Section
2.3
, the almost identical conclusions (via, in fact, almost identical proofs) in the case
P_{0}
is a discrete distribution.

2.1
Low Degree correlation upper bound from decreasing FP

We prove an upper bound on the low-degree correlation from the monotonicity of annealed FP. We start by describing exactly the set of assumptions we make for the GAMs. We first need the following notion from probability theory.

Definition 2.2

(Sub-Weibull
(\phi)
)
.

[See e.g., Theorem 2.1 from
[
34
]
]
Fix
\phi>0
. A real-valued random variable
X
is called sub-Weibull of order
\phi
if there exist constants
K>0
and
C_{\phi}\in(0,\infty)
such that
\big(\mathbb{E}|X|^{p}\big)^{1/p}\ \leq\ C_{\phi}Kp^{1/\phi}
,
for all
p\geq 1
.

Remark 2.3

(Examples of sub-Weibull random variables)
.

The class of sub-Weibull random variables includes many familiar distributions. For instance:
(i) any bounded random variable is sub-Weibull
(\phi)
for every
\phi>0
,
(ii) any sub-Gaussian random variable is sub-Weibull
(2)
, and
(iii) any sub-exponential random variable is sub-Weibull
(1)
.

We study Gaussian additive models (GAMs) whose priors satisfy a
low-order cumulant-nonnegative
property. This condition constitutes our main structural assumption, supplemented by several mild regularity assumptions.

Assumption 2.4

(Low-order cumulant-nonnegatively GAMs)
.

Fix a universal constant
C>0
. Let
(D_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of positive integers satisfying
D_{n}\leq n^{C}
, and let
(A_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of positive reals.

A GAM with prior
P_{0}
satisfies our assumption with parameters
(D_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
,
(A_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, and
C>0
the following conditions hold.

(1)

(
D_{n}
-order cumulant-nonnegative
)
For every multi-index
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
with
|\alpha|\leq D_{n}
, the joint cumulant satisfies

\kappa_{\alpha}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})\geq 0.

(2)

(
Polynomial growth of marginal moments
)
There exists a universal constant
\phi>0
such that for all
i\in[n]
, the marginal
X_{i}
is sub-Weibull
(\phi)
.

(3)

(
Controlled decay of overlap quantiles
)
There exists a universal constant
C_{1}>0
such that for all
n
,

q\!\left(D_{n}(\log n)^{2}\right)\;\geq\;\max\!\left\{(\log n)^{-D_{n}/2},\,n^{-C_{1}}\right\}.

(4)

(
Quantile stability
)
The overlap distribution satisfies (here
\mathbb{P}(\langle X,X^{\prime}\rangle=t)
should be understood as the PDF of
\langle X,X^{\prime}\rangle
),

\left.\frac{d}{dt}\log\mathbb{P}(\langle X,X^{\prime}\rangle=t)\right|_{t=q(D_{n})}\;\geq\;-\frac{A_{n}}{q(D_{n}(\log n)^{2})\log n}.

(2.1)

Assumptions (1)–(4) should be interpreted as consisting of one principal structural condition, Assumption (1), and three auxiliary regularity conditions that are satisfied by most canonical priors considered in the GAM literature.

Remark 2.5

(On Assumption (1))
.

Assumption (1) is the main technical condition underpinning our equivalence, and in particular our low-degree correlation upper bounds. Theorem
2.7
(the low-degree correlation lower bounds per Theorem
2.9
apply without it).

Many natural priors satisfy this condition. For instance, the tensor prior
X=x^{\otimes p}
satisfies Assumption (1), (a) for all
D>0
when
x
has i.i.d. Gaussian entries, and (b) for
D=O(\log n)
when
x
has i.i.d. Bernoulli–Rademacher entries with sparsity
k/n
. In Section
8
, we show that a substantially broader class of priors satisfies this condition, including for example the prior used for Gaussian sparse clustering
[
10
]
.

Assumption (2) is standard and mild; see Remark
2.3
.
Assumption (3) is also weak: for
D_{n}=\omega(\log n/\log\log n)
, it reduces to the condition
q(D_{n})\geq n^{-C}
for some universal constant
C>0
, which holds for most priors of interest.

Remark 2.6

(On Assumption (4))
.

Although Assumption (4) may appear technical, it is mild in most relevant settings. In many GAMs, the overlap
\langle X,X^{\prime}\rangle
is centered and composed of weakly dependent terms—for example, in tensor PCA of any finite order with i.i.d. coordinates, or in sparse tensor PCA.

In such cases, after appropriate normalization, the overlap converges in distribution to a polynomial of a standard Gaussian:
\langle X,X^{\prime}\rangle/a_{n}\xrightarrow{d}Z^{p},\ Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1),
for some fixed
p\in\mathbb{N}
.
Consequently, for any
D_{n}=\mathrm{polylog}(n)
, the quantiles behave as follows (explaining the name “quantile stability” for the assumption)

q(D_{n})=\Theta(a_{n}D_{n}^{p/2}).

Moreover, via a similar local central limit theorem argument, one naturally expects also
-\log\mathbb{P}(\langle X,X^{\prime}\rangle=t)
to be close to
(t/a_{n})^{2/p}/2
, which together suggest

-\left.\frac{d}{dt}\log\mathbb{P}(\langle X,X^{\prime}\rangle=t)\right|_{t=q(D_{n})}=\Theta\left(\frac{D_{n}}{q(D_{n})}\right),

which is consistent with Assumption (4) (where some additional slack
A_{n}
, which should be treated as polylogarithmic, and other slack polylogarithmic factors are allowed).

We are now in a position to state and prove our first one-sided equivalence result

Theorem 2.7

(Increasing FP potential implies Low-Degree hard)
.

Suppose we have a Gaussian Additive Model with prior
P_{0}
that satisfies Assumption
2.4
and let for each
n
,
D_{n},D^{\prime}_{n}
positive integers with
D_{n}^{\prime}=(1+o(1))D_{n}\log^{2}n
.

For any
u>0
satisfying
u+\frac{d}{dq}\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ann},u}\bigg|_{q=q(D_{n})}>0
we have

\mathrm{Corr}^{\leq D_{n}}_{P_{0}}\left(\frac{1}{A_{n}}\cdot\left(u+\frac{\textup{d}}{\textup{d}q}\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ann},u}|_{q=q(D_{n})}\right)\right)^{2}\leq 2q(D^{\prime}_{n}).

(2.2)

In particular, for any SNR
\lambda=\lambda_{n}>0
, if
n
is large enough, we have that
\frac{d}{dq}\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ann},\lambda}|_{q=q(D_{n})}\geq 0
implies
\mathrm{Corr}^{\leq D_{n}}_{P_{0}}\left(\frac{1}{A_{n}}\lambda\right)^{2}\leq 2q(D^{\prime}_{n}).

The proof of the theorem is deferred to Section
6
; see also Section
2.4
for a proof sketch.

2.2
Low Degree correlation lower bound from increasing FP

We now turn to the other side of the equivalence. This time, this works under only mild assumptions for the GAM, and in particular, no assumption on cumulant-nonnegativity is needed here.

Before we proceed, we first state the assumptions that will be used in this section.

Assumption 2.8
.

Fix constants
c,c_{1},c_{2},C,\kappa>0
, a sequence of positive integers
(D_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
D_{n}=\omega(1)
and a sequence of positive numbers
(B_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
B_{n}=\omega(\max\{n^{-C},e^{-CD_{n}}\})
. A GAM with prior
P_{0}
satisfies the assumption for parameters
(D_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}},(B_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
c,c_{1},c_{2},C,\kappa>0
if the following conditions hold.

(1)

(Fixed quantile order) For all
D>0,

c_{1}B_{n}D^{\kappa}\leq q(D)\leq c_{2}B_{n}D^{\kappa}
.

(2)

(Quantile stability) The PDF of the overlap satisfies,

\left.\frac{d}{dt}\log\mathbb{P}(\langle X,X^{\prime}\rangle=t)\right|_{t=q(D_{n})}\leq-\frac{c}{q(D_{n})}.

Both the assumptions (1), (2) are rather mild and satisfied by most GAMs in the literature. The motivation behind them in fact lies entirely on what is described in Remark
2.6
on Assumption (4) from Assumption
2.4
and we direct the reader there for the details. Interestingly, notice that for this section we need to assume the other inequality direction from Assumption (4).

We are now ready to state the main result of this direction.

Theorem 2.9

(Decreasing FP potential implies Low-Degree easy)
.

For any GAM satisfying Assumption
2.8
for sequence
(B_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
,
(D_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and constants
c,c_{1},c_{2},C,\kappa>0
, there exists a constant
C^{\prime}>0
, such that the following holds. For any
u>0
satisfying
u+\frac{d}{dq}\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ann},u}\bigg|_{q=q(D_{n})}>0
we have

(\mathrm{Corr}^{\leq D_{n}}_{P_{0}})^{2}\left({u+\frac{d}{dq}\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ann},u}\bigg|_{q=q(D_{n})}}\right)\geq\frac{C^{\prime}}{D_{n}^{2\kappa}}{q(D_{n})}.

In particular, for any SNR
\lambda>0
,
(\mathrm{Corr}^{\leq D_{n}}_{P_{0}})^{2}(\lambda)\leq\frac{C^{\prime}}{D_{n}^{2\kappa}}{q(D_{n})}
implies
\frac{d}{dq}\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ann},\lambda}^{\prime}\bigg|_{q=q(D_{n})}\geq 0.

The proof of the theorem is deferred to Section
7
; see also Section
2.4
for a proof sketch.

2.3
The equivalence for discrete priors

Here we state the equivalence results for discrete prior
P_{0}
. The sole difference is that the derivative of
\log(\Pr(\langle X,X^{\prime}\rangle=q))
is not well-defined anymore, where now
\Pr(\langle X,X^{\prime}\rangle=q)
is a probability mass function (PMF) and instead consider a discrete derivative/difference operator. Importantly, modulo this difference, all statements, assumptions, and proofs of this section are identical to the ones in Section
2
. More specifically, the sole distinction in the proofs is that to apply these Theorems, one needs to check the assumptions in terms of this discrete now derivative operator.

Let us denote for simplicity
f_{\mathrm{ov}}(q)=\Pr(\langle X,X^{\prime}\rangle=q)
the PMF of the overlap for two independent draws from our prior. To define the discrete derivative operator we need to be careful as
f_{\mathrm{ov}}(q)
is supported only on a set of discrete values of
q
. Let
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{ov}}=\{q\in\mathbb{R}:f_{\mathrm{ov}}(q)>0\}
. Now, let any sequence of functions
a_{n}:\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{ov}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{>0},n\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all
q\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{ov}}
,
q+a_{n}(q)\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{ov}}
. Given this choice of
a_{n},n\in\mathbb{N}
we define the discrete derivative operator
\mathbf{\Delta}_{a_{n}}
on functions
f:\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{ov}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
such that

\mathbf{\Delta}_{a_{n}}(f)(q)=\frac{f(q+\alpha_{n}(q))-f(q)}{a_{n}(q)},q\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{ov}}.

2.3.1
Low Degree correlation upper bound from decreasing FP (discrete case)

Similar to the continuous case to prove this result we need some assumptions, which is identical to Assumption
2.4
by switching the derivative with the discrete derivative operator.

Assumption 2.10

(Low-order cumulant-nonnegatively GAMs (discrete))
.

Fix a universal constant
C>0
. Let
(D_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of positive integers satisfying
D_{n}\leq n^{C}
, let
(A_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of positive reals, and let any sequence of “speed” functions
a_{n}:\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{ov}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{>0},n\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all
q\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{ov}}
,
q+a_{n}(q)\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{ov}}
.

A GAM with prior
P_{0}
is assumed to satisfy with parameters
(D_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
,
(A_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
,
(a_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
C>0
the following conditions hold.

(1)

(
D_{n}
-order cumulant-nonnegative
)
For every multi-index
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
with
|\alpha|\leq D_{n}
, the joint cumulant satisfies

\kappa_{\alpha}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})\geq 0.

(2.3)

(2)

(
Polynomial growth of marginal moments
)
There exists a universal constant
\phi>0
such that for all
i\in[n]
, the marginal
X_{i}
is sub-Weibull
(\phi)
.

(3)

(
Controlled decay of overlap quantiles
)
There exists a universal constant
C_{1}>0
such that for all
n
,

q\!\left(D_{n}(\log n)^{2}\right)\;\geq\;\max\!\left\{(\log n)^{-D_{n}/2},\,n^{-C_{1}}\right\}.

(4)

(
Quantile stability
)
The overlap distribution satisfies (here
\mathbb{P}(\langle X,X^{\prime}\rangle=t)
should be understood as the PMF of
\langle X,X^{\prime}\rangle
),

\left.\mathbf{\Delta}_{a_{n}}\log\mathbb{P}(\langle X,X^{\prime}\rangle=t)\right|_{t=q(D_{n})}\;\geq\;-\frac{A_{n}}{q(D_{n}(\log n)^{2})\log n}.

(2.4)

Given this assumption, we obtain the following result in the discrete case.

Theorem 2.11

(Increasing FP potential implies Low-Degree hard (discrete))
.

Suppose we have a Gaussian Additive Model with prior
P_{0}
that satisfies Assumption
2.10
and let for each
n
,
D_{n},D^{\prime}_{n}
positive integers with
D_{n}^{\prime}=(1+o(1))D_{n}\log^{2}n
.

For any
u>0
satisfying
u+\mathbf{\Delta}_{a_{n}}\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ann},u}\bigg|_{q=q(D_{n})}>0
we have

\mathrm{Corr}^{\leq D_{n}}_{P_{0}}\left(\frac{1}{A_{n}}\cdot\left(u+\mathbf{\Delta}_{a_{n}}\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ann},u}|_{q=q(D_{n})}\right)\right)^{2}\leq 2q(D^{\prime}_{n}).

(2.5)

In particular, for any SNR
\lambda=\lambda_{n}>0
, if
n
is large enough, we have that
\mathbf{\Delta}_{a_{n}}\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ann},\lambda}|_{q=q(D_{n})}\geq 0
implies
\mathrm{Corr}^{\leq D_{n}}_{P_{0}}\left(\frac{1}{A_{n}}\lambda\right)^{2}\leq 2q(D^{\prime}_{n}).

The proof is identical to the proof of Theorem
2.7
with swapping the derivative operator and the discrete derivative operator, and is omitted.

2.3.2
Low Degree correlation lower bound from increasing FP (discrete case)

Now the assumption for the positive result is changed as follows.

Assumption 2.12
.

Fix constants
c,c_{1},c_{2},C,\kappa>0
, a sequence of positive integers
(D_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
D_{n}=\omega(1)
, a sequence of positive numbers
(B_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
B_{n}=\omega(\max\{n^{-C},e^{-CD_{n}}\})
, and let any sequence of “speed” functions
a_{n}:\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{ov}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{>0},n\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all
q\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{ov}}
,
q+a_{n}(q)\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{ov}}
. A GAM with prior
P_{0}
is assumed to satisfy for parameters
(D_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}},(B_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}},(a_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
c,c_{1},c_{2},C,\kappa>0
if the following conditions hold.

(1)

(Fixed quantile order) For all
D>0,

c_{1}B_{n}D^{\kappa}\leq q(D)\leq c_{2}B_{n}D^{\kappa}
.

(2)

(Quantile stability) The PMF of the overlap satisfies,

\left.\mathbf{\Delta}_{a_{n}}\log\mathbb{P}(\langle X,X^{\prime}\rangle=t)\right|_{t=q(D_{n})}\leq-\frac{c}{q(D_{n})}.

We are now ready to state the main result of this direction in the discrete case as well.

Theorem 2.13

(Decreasing FP potential implies Low-Degree easy (discrete))
.

For any GAM satisfying Assumption
2.12
for sequence
(B_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
,
(D_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
,
(a_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and constants
c,c_{1},c_{2},C,\kappa>0
, there exists a constant
C^{\prime}>0
, such that the following holds. For any
u>0
satisfying
u+\mathbf{\Delta}_{a_{n}}\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ann},u}\bigg|_{q=q(D_{n})}>0
we have

(\mathrm{Corr}^{\leq D_{n}}_{P_{0}})^{2}\left({u+\mathbf{\Delta}_{a_{n}}\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ann},u}\bigg|_{q=q(D_{n})}}\right)\geq\frac{C^{\prime}}{D_{n}^{2\kappa}}{q(D_{n})}.

In particular, for any SNR
\lambda>0
,
(\mathrm{Corr}^{\leq D_{n}}_{P_{0}})^{2}(\lambda)\leq\frac{C^{\prime}}{D_{n}^{2\kappa}}{q(D_{n})}
implies
\mathbf{\Delta}_{a_{n}}\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ann},\lambda}^{\prime}\bigg|_{q=q(D_{n})}\geq 0.

Similar to above, the proof is identical to the proof of Theorem
2.9
with swapping the derivative operator and the discrete derivative operator, and is omitted.

2.4
Proof ideas

While the proofs of the equivalence Theorems are deferred to later sections, we briefly outline here the main ideas underlying them, assuming for simplicity that the overlap distribution admits a continuous and differentiable density.

A key starting point is the following elementary identity, which significantly clarifies the role of the annealed FP potential in GAMs: for any SNR
\lambda>0
and all
q\in[-1,1]
, we have that

\lambda q+\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ann},\lambda}(q)=-\log\mathbb{P}(\langle X,X^{\prime}\rangle=q).

In particular, differentiating at
q=q(D)
yields

\lambda+\left.\frac{d}{dq}\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ann},\lambda}(q)\right|_{q=q(D)}=-\left.\frac{d}{dq}\log\mathbb{P}(\langle X,X^{\prime}\rangle=q)\right|_{q=q(D)}.

(2.6)

Under Assumption
2.4
together with Assumption
2.8
, and for degrees
D=\mathrm{polylog}(n)
, the right-hand side of (
2.6
) can be shown to satisfy
-\left.\frac{d}{dq}\log\mathbb{P}(\langle X,X^{\prime}\rangle=q)\right|_{q=q(D)}\;\approx\;\frac{1}{q(D)}.

Combining the last two steps, establishing our main theorems in fact interestingly reduces to showing that, for all such GAMs of interest and degrees
D=\mathrm{polylog}(n)
,
\big(\mathrm{Corr}_{P_{0}}^{\leq D}\big)^{2}\!\left(\frac{1}{q(D)}\right)\;\approx\;q(D),

that is, that the low-degree correlation satisfies an approximate fixed-point relation at the scale
q(D)
.

We prove the two inequalities separately. For the upper bound

\big(\mathrm{Corr}_{P_{0}}^{\leq D}\big)^{2}\!\left(\frac{1}{q(D)}\right)\leq q(D)
,
we rely on a recent result of
[
30
]
, often referred to as
Jensen’s trick
, which allows one to upper bound the low-degree correlation in terms of the cumulants of the prior
P_{0}
. This reduces the problem to verifying an inequality relating cumulants of
P_{0}
to quantiles of the overlap
\langle X,X^{\prime}\rangle
. The proof exploits the fact that overlap quantiles can be controlled via the log-moment generating function, which in turn admits a Taylor expansion in terms of cumulants. The details are rather delicate and the whole proof is provided in Section
6
.

For the matching lower bound,

\left(\mathrm{Corr}_{P_{0}}^{\leq D}\right)^{2}\left(\frac{1}{q(D)}\right)\geq q(D),

we explicitly construct a degree-
D
estimator achieving a squared correlation of at least
q(D)
under mild conditions on the GAM. A natural approach is to analyze the optimal degree-
D
polynomial, which corresponds to the projection of the posterior mean onto the linear space of degree-
D
polynomials under the marginal measure of
Y
.

Since
Y
does not follow a product measure, and therefore no canonical basis of orthonormal polynomials is known, the analysis of this projection is considered often in the literature as “technically prohibitive”—even for simple GAMs—which is why researchers frequently analyze the low-degree MMSE via alternative means. In this work, we design a close proxy to this optimal low-degree projection of the posterior mean which is tractable to define and analyze
for all GAMs
we consider in this work, and could be of independent interest. This analysis yields the desired tight lower bound on the low-degree correlation.

The construction is based on an importance-sampling scheme: we draw
M
independent samples
X_{1},\ldots,X_{M}
from the prior
P_{0}
and consider a polynomial estimator of the form

p(Y)=\sum_{i=1}^{M}W(Y\mid X_{i})X_{i},

(2.7)

where the weights
W(Y\mid X_{i})=W_{D}(Y\mid X_{i})
are carefully chosen degree-
D
polynomials designed to maximize correlation with the signal. The motivation behind this choice of weights is for
p(Y)
to approximate the low-degree projection of the posterior mean. The key insight is to treat the Hermite polynomials as if they form an orthogonal basis under the measure of
Y
and project onto them accordingly. This leads to a canonical choice of the weights
W=W_{D}
(see (
7.12
)) and enables a tractable analysis that establishes the desired lower bound, completing the proof of the approximate fixed-point relation. We note that the idea of using the Hermite/Fourier-Walsh basis as almost orthonormal basis under the “planted”
Y
for certain models has recently appeared in the low-degree MMSE literature
[
7
]
(in
[
7
]
the focus is not on GAMs but on some random graph models). The technical details of this construction are subtle, also in this case, and are provided in Section
7
.

3
Applications

To present the applicability of our equivalence we prove that our results can prove some old and new state-of-the-art MMSE lower bounds in the recent literature by (1) directly calculating the quantiles of the overlap of the prior, (2) proving the derivative of the annealed FP potential is nonnegative (hence, “physics-hard”), and (3) applying Theorem
2.7
(or, Theorem
2.11
in the discrete case) to conclude its low-degree MMSE hard.

We highlight that in these proof the technical work lies on checking Assumption
2.4
for Theorem
2.7
. Among them, all parts are relatively easy to check with the sole exception of Assumption (4) which sometimes is more challenging. To verify Assumption (4), we prove several local CLT theorems for the density of the overlap which can be seen as a technical contribution of potential independent interest. All proofs are deferred to Section
10
.

3.1
Tensor PCA models

In this model, the prior
P_{0}
is of the form
X=\mathrm{vec}(v^{\otimes r})
for some constant
r>0
, some distribution on
v\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
and
n=N^{r}
. In particular, notice that under such prior for any
X=\mathrm{vec}(v^{\otimes r})
,
X^{\prime}=\mathrm{vec}((v^{\prime})^{\otimes r})
, it holds
\langle X,X^{\prime}\rangle=\langle\mathrm{vec}(v^{\otimes r}),\mathrm{vec}(v^{\prime\otimes r})\rangle=\langle v,v^{\prime}\rangle^{r}.

3.1.1
Tensor PCA (Gaussian prior)

In this model
v\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
has i.i.d.
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
entries. The statistical threshold for this model is
\lambda_{\mathrm{IT}}=\widetilde{\Theta}(n^{1-r})

[
24
]
and the algorithmic threshold is conjectured to be
\lambda_{\mathrm{ALG}}=\widetilde{\Theta}(n^{-r/2})
(see e.g.,
[
16
]
and references therein).

It turns out that Theorem
2.7
applies for the tensor PCA model for any
r\geq 1
, as the prior satisfies Assumption
2.4
for any
D_{n}=\widetilde{o}(\sqrt{n})
, Weibull constant
\phi=2
and
A_{n}=\Theta(D_{n}^{3/2}\log^{r+1}n)
. In particular, Theorem
2.7
implies the following result.

Theorem 3.1
.

Fix
r\geq 2
. For the Gaussian prior in the rank one tensor spike model we prove that for all
\lambda>0
and for any sequence of integers
D_{n}=o(\sqrt{n}/\log^{2}n)
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{MMSE}^{\leq D_{n}}_{X}\left(\frac{1}{D_{n}^{3/2}\log^{r+1}n}\big(\lambda+\frac{\textup{d}}{\textup{d}q}\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ann},\lambda))}\bigg|_{q=q(D_{n})}\big)\right)=n^{r}-\widetilde{O}\big((D_{n}n)^{r/2}\big)=(1+o(1))\mathrm{MMSE}^{\mathrm{trivial}}_{X}.

In particular, for some
\lambda=\widetilde{\Theta}(n^{-r/2})=\widetilde{\Theta}(\lambda_{\mathrm{ALG}}),
we have
\frac{\textup{d}}{\textup{d}q}\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ann},\lambda}\bigg|_{q(D_{n})}\geq 0
, and therefore

\mathrm{MMSE}^{\leq D_{n}}_{X}\left(\widetilde{\Theta}\Big(\frac{\lambda_{\mathrm{ALG}}}{D_{n}^{r/2}}\Big)\right)\geq(1+o(1))\mathrm{MMSE}^{\mathrm{trivial}}_{X}.

The proof of the theorem is deferred to Section
10.2
.

A low-degree MMSE lower bound for the tensor PCA setting (with i.i.d. “dense” prior) has also been attained in
[
17
]
for the Rademacher prior, using techniques from free probability theory, but only for
r
odd. Our approach is able to prove the low-degree MMSE lower bound for all
r
for the Gaussian prior.

3.1.2
Sparse Tensor PCA (Rademacher sparse prior)

We now move to another Tensor PCA setting with a very well-studied discrete prior. Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
with
k=n^{\beta+o(1)}
for some
\beta\in(1/2,1)
. Then, the sparse (Rademacher) tensor PCA model, is the tensor PCA model where
v\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
has i.i.d. entries
\mathrm{Rad}(k/n),
meaning that for all
i=1,\ldots,N
,
v_{i}=1
with probability
k/(2n)
,
v_{i}=-1
with probability
k/(2n)
and
v_{i}=0
, otherwise. The statistical threshold for this model is
\lambda_{\mathrm{IT}}=\widetilde{\Theta}(k^{1-r})

[
21
]
and the algorithmic threshold of this model is widely conjectured to be
\lambda_{\mathrm{ALG}}=\widetilde{\Theta}(\min\{1,n^{r/2}/k^{r}\})

[
21
]
.

It turns out that the discrete version of Theorem
2.7
(Theorem
2.11
in the Appendix) applies for the sparse tensor PCA model for any
r\geq 1,\beta\in(0,1/2)\cup(1/2,1)
, as the sparse Rademacher prior satisfies the discrete version of Assumption
2.4
(specifically, Assumption
2.10
) for
D_{n}=\lfloor(1-\beta)\log n\rfloor
, and
A_{n}=\Theta(D_{n}^{2-r}\log^{(3r+2)/2}n)
. In particular, the discrete version of Theorem
2.7
implies the following result (below
\mathbf{\Delta}_{a_{n}}
corresponds to the discrete derivative operator, see Section
2.3
for details).

Theorem 3.2
.

Fix
r\geq 2
. For the sparse tensor PCA model for all SNR
\lambda>0
and
D_{n}=\lfloor(1-\beta)\log n\rfloor
, we have the following.

If
\beta\in(\frac{1}{2},1)
, then

\displaystyle\mathrm{MMSE}^{\leq D_{n}}\left(\frac{1}{D_{n}^{r-2}\log^{(3r+2)/2}n}\cdot\big(\lambda+\mathbf{\Delta}_{a_{n}}\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ann},\lambda}\bigg|_{q=q(D_{n})}\big)\right)

\displaystyle=k^{r}-\widetilde{O}\bigg(\frac{k^{r}}{n^{r/2}}\bigg)=(1+o(1))\mathrm{MMSE}^{\mathrm{trivial}}.

In particular, since for some
\lambda=\widetilde{\Theta}(n^{r/2}/k^{r})=\widetilde{\Theta}(\lambda_{\mathrm{ALG}}),

\mathbf{\Delta}_{a_{n}}\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ann},\lambda}\bigg|_{q(D_{n})}\geq 0
, we conclude

\mathrm{MMSE}^{\leq D_{n}}\left(\widetilde{\Theta}(\lambda_{\mathrm{ALG}})\right)\geq(1+o(1))\mathrm{MMSE}^{\mathrm{trivial}}.

If
\beta\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
, then

\displaystyle\mathrm{MMSE}^{\leq D_{n}}\left(\frac{1}{\log^{3}n}\cdot\big(\lambda+\mathbf{\Delta}_{a_{n}}\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ann},\lambda}\bigg|_{q=q(D_{n})}\big)\right)

\displaystyle=k^{r}-\widetilde{O}(1)=(1+o(1))\mathrm{MMSE}^{\mathrm{trivial}}.

In particular, since for some
\lambda=\widetilde{\Theta}(1)=\widetilde{\Theta}(\lambda_{\mathrm{ALG}}),

\mathbf{\Delta}_{a_{n}}\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ann},\lambda}\bigg|_{q(D_{n})}\geq 0
, we conclude

\mathrm{MMSE}^{\leq D_{n}}\left(\widetilde{\Theta}(\lambda_{\mathrm{ALG}})\right)\geq(1+o(1))\mathrm{MMSE}^{\mathrm{trivial}}.

The proof of the theorem deferred to Section
10.1.1
.

To the best of our knowledge, this is the first tight low-degree MMSE for this setting. For this model, there exists some estimation lower bounds against the support recovery question
[
21
]
which concerns the recovery of the support of
v,
but (a) this appears not applicable to recovering the signed vector
x
and (b) it is coupled with a reduction-based hardness argument rather than a direct low-degree MMSE lower bound.

3.2
Gaussian-Mixture model

In the Gaussian Mixture Model with two centers we observe
n
data points in
\mathbb{R}^{p}
each one of which is either a draw from
\mathcal{N}(\mu_{1},\sigma^{2}I_{p})
or
\mathcal{N}(\mu_{2},\sigma^{2}I_{p})
with equal probability, where
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
are the two “centers”. We collect these
n
observations as the rows of a matrix

Y\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times p}
. The canonical parameter for this model
\Delta^{2}
is proportional to the minimum separation between the centers, i.e.,
\Delta^{2}:=\frac{\|\mu_{1}-\mu_{2}\|^{2}_{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}.

We focus on the balanced case, where the two centers are
\mu
and
-\mu
, for some
\mu\in\mathbb{R}^{p},
and without loss of generality assume
\sigma=1
. Equivalently,
let latent labels
\xi_{i}\in\{+1,-1\}
be i.i.d. with
\mathbb{P}(\xi_{i}=+1)=\mathbb{P}(\xi_{i}=-1)=1/2
,
and assume
Y_{i}=\xi_{i}\,\mu+\,z_{i}
, for
z_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i.i.d.}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,I_{p})
. In that case this maps exactly to the GAM setting in
n\times p
-dimensions
Y=X+Z
setting for
\xi=(\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n})^{\top}
and
X=\mathrm{vec}(\xi\mu^{\top})
and

Y
is the vectorized
n\times p
matrix with rows
Y_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{p},i=1,\ldots,n
. Moreover, in this case
\Delta^{2}=2\|\mu\|^{2}_{2}.

3.2.1
Sparse Clustering

In sparse clustering, the mean
\mu
is assumed to be sparse. Specifically,
there exists an unknown set
J\subseteq[p]
with
|J|\leq s
such that

\mu_{j}=0
for all
j\notin J
. We will focus on the case
s=p^{\alpha+o(1)}
, where
\alpha\in(1/2,1).
In the regime we are interested in the model is conjectured to have an algorithmic threshold, in our scaling, at
\Delta^{2}=\Delta^{2}_{\mathrm{ALG}}=\Theta(p/n)

[
19
]
.

We now prove low-degree MMSE lower bound near the predicted algorithmic threshold. Consider the following prior on our centers
\mu
and
-\mu
, borrowed from
[
11
]
.
Let
\Delta>0
and i.i.d.
\ b_{j}\sim\mathrm{Ber}(s/p),j=1,\ldots,p
and i.i.d.
g_{j}\sim N(0,\frac{\Delta}{s}),j=1,\ldots,n
for which we set
\mu_{j}:=b_{j}g_{j},j=1,\ldots,p.
Note that for this choice of the mean of
\|\mu\|^{2}_{2}
equals
\Theta(\Delta^{2})
with high probability as
n
grows, so we would like a low-degree MMSE lower bound at
\Delta^{2}=\widetilde{\Theta}(p/n).
We rescale the GAM so that the prior satisfies our assumptions to
Y=\sqrt{\lambda}X^{\prime}+Z,
for
X^{\prime}=X/\sqrt{\Delta/s}
and
\lambda=\Delta/s.
In this scaling,
\lambda_{\mathrm{ALG}}=\widetilde{\Theta}(\sqrt{p/(s^{2}n)})
and our goal then is to prove low-degree MMSE hardness at
\lambda=\widetilde{\Theta}(\lambda_{\mathrm{ALG}}).

It turns out that Theorem
2.7
applies for this Sparse Clustering model, as this prior also satisfies Assumption
2.4
, this time for
D_{n}=\lfloor(1-\alpha)\log n\rfloor
, Weibull constant
\phi=2
and
A_{n}=\Theta(\log^{4}n)
. In particular, Theorem
2.7
implies the following result.

Theorem 3.3
.

Let
p,n
be positive integers such that
p=n^{c+o(1)}
for some constant
c>0
. For the Sparse Clustering model and the prior discussed above we prove that for
\lambda=\Delta/s
,
s=p^{a+o(1)}
where
a\in(1/2,1)
and for
D_{n}=\lfloor(1-\alpha)\log n\rfloor
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{MMSE}^{\leq D_{n}}_{X}\left(\frac{1}{\log^{4}n}\cdot\big(\lambda+\frac{\textup{d}}{\textup{d}q}\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ann},\lambda}\bigg|_{q=q(D_{n})}\big)\right)=ns-\widetilde{O}\left(s\sqrt{n/p}\right)=(1+o(1))\mathrm{MMSE}^{\mathrm{trivial}}_{X}.

In particular, for
\lambda=\widetilde{\Theta}(\sqrt{p/(s^{2}n)})=\widetilde{\Theta}(\lambda_{\mathrm{ALG}}),
we have
\frac{\textup{d}}{\textup{d}q}\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ann},\lambda}\bigg|_{q=q(D_{n})}\geq 0
, and therefore

\mathrm{MMSE}^{\leq D_{n}}_{X}\left(\widetilde{\Theta}(\lambda_{\mathrm{ALG}})\right)\geq(1+o(1))\mathrm{MMSE}^{\mathrm{trivial}}_{X}.

The proof is deferred to Section
10.3
.

This tight low-degree lower bound for estimation in this model has also been established very recently in
[
11
]
. In fact, that work proves a slightly stronger result than what follows from our general equivalence, as by exploiting the specific latent-variable structure of the clustering problem they obtain the
D
-degree MMSE lower bound to degrees
D_{n}
that grow slightly faster than logarithmic.

4
Conclusion

In this work, we establish a rigorous mathematical equivalence between the monotonicity of the annealed Franz-Parisi (FP) potential and low-degree MMSE lower bounds for a broad class of Gaussian Additive Models (GAMs). We show that the sign of the derivative of the annealed FP potential is equivalent to the low-degree MMSE surpassing a specific benchmark defined by the quantiles of the overlap of two i.i.d. draws from the prior. Our results bridge these two seemingly distinct formulations of computational hardness, which are distinct both in terms of their scientific origins and in terms of their mathematical formulation. Furthermore, our findings open several promising avenues for future research:

•

Annealed vs. Quenched Potential:
Perhaps the most significant departure from classical statistical physics methodology in our work is the focus on the annealed, rather than quenched, FP potential. As discussed in Section
A
, this appears to some extent fundamental; for certain GAMs, the quenched potential remains increasing (indicating a “physics-hard” regime) even deep into the “low-degree easy” regime. In contrast, the monotonicity of the annealed potential tightly captures the low-degree MMSE phase transition from trivial to non-trivial. This suggests that the annealed FP potential may be more naturally suited for characterizing computational hardness in statistical settings, raising the fundamental question of whether this phenomenon has a deeper interpretation in statistical physics.

•

Link between Potentials and Algorithmic classes:
It is somewhat folklore in estimation tasks that the quenched FP potential relates to the performance of MCMC methods, see for example
[
2
,
3
]
where the non-monotonicity of the quenched FP potential termed as a free energy barrier is proven to imply MCMC lower bounds. Moreover, the replica-symmetric FP potential is known to relate to the performance of Approximate Message Passing (AMP) in spiked rank-1 models (see e.g.,
[
25
]
for a discussion). Our work (alongside
[
5
,
8
]
for detection) establishes a new connection; the annealed FP potential characterizes the performance of low-degree polynomials. Deepening our understanding of this mapping between physics potentials and classes of algorithms is a very interesting direction for future study.

•

Obtaining more Low-Degree Information from the Annealed FP Potential:
We prove that the monotonicity of the annealed FP potential determines whether the low-degree MMSE outperforms a specific benchmark. In most applications, this benchmark is of the order of the “trivial MMSE,” meaning an increasing annealed FP potential implies the failure of low-degree polynomials to beat the trivial performance. While we utilize a lot this “one-sided” implication of our results in our applications, a compelling open question is whether the annealed potential can provide a more refined “success” result for the low-degree MMSE—specifically, whether it can indicate exactly when low-degree polynomials achieve low-degree MMSE that beats
in order
the trivial MMSE. Our current results suggest that the sign of the derivative alone is not sufficient to find this information.

Low-degree I-MMSE relation:
A compelling way to frame the success of our technique is as follows. One of the most fundamental identities in information theory is the I-MMSE relation
[
14
]
, which links for any GAM the derivative of the mutual information (i.e., in physics jargon, the derivative of the “free energy” of the system) to the MMSE. This identity has been instrumental in characterizing the asymptotic MMSE for numerous high-dimensional models. Specifically, it has served as a key step in proving the “replica-symmetric” MMSE formulas for compressed sensing
[
6
,
28
]
and low-rank matrix estimation
[
18
]
, as well as establishing “all-or-nothing” phase transitions in various settings
[
29
,
27
]
. The technical advantage of this approach rather than analyzing the MMSE directly is that researchers can instead study the more tractable free energy and simply differentiate it to extract the MMSE.

Given the recent surge of interest in the low-degree MMSE—which is typically analyzed via direct combinatorial methods and “cumulant” bounds—it is natural to ask whether a
low-degree I-MMSE relation
exists. Such a relation would ideally reduce the low-degree MMSE analysis to the study of a (hopefully simpler) “low-degree free energy.” Our main result (Theorem
1.1
) establishes exactly such an approximate identity for a large class of GAMs: we prove that for these models the derivative of the annealed FP potential is linked to the low-degree MMSE. As the annealed FP potential is a much easier object to compute, this leads to relatively easier analysis of the low-degree MMSE of a series of interesting models.

Since the classical I-MMSE relation holds for all GAMs and extends to other settings such as Poisson
[
4
]
and Bernoulli channels
[
26
]
, generalizing our key identity beyond the Gaussian framework is a natural and promising direction for future research.

5
Additional notation for the proofs

We write
\mathbb{N}=\{1,2,\dots\}
and
[N]=\{1,\dots,N\}
.
All asymptotic notation (
O,o,\Omega,\omega,\Theta
) is for
n\to\infty
unless stated otherwise, and

\widetilde{O}(\cdot),\widetilde{\Omega}(\cdot),\widetilde{\Theta}(\cdot)
hide
\text{polylog}(n)=(\log n)^{O(1)}
factors.
For an event
A
,
\mathbf{1}_{A}
denotes its indicator. All logarithms are natural.

For
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{N}
, define
|\alpha|=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\alpha_{i}
,
\alpha!=\prod_{i=1}^{N}\alpha_{i}!
, and for
X\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
,

X^{\alpha}=\prod_{i=1}^{N}X_{i}^{\alpha_{i}}
. We write
\alpha\geq\beta
for coordinate wise inequality, and define

\binom{\alpha}{\beta}=\prod_{i=1}^{N}\binom{\alpha_{i}}{\beta_{i}}
when
\alpha\geq\beta
.
We write
\beta\prec\alpha
to mean
\beta\leq\alpha
and
\beta\neq\alpha
. Also,
{\rm supp}(\alpha)=\{i\in[N]:\alpha_{i}\neq 0\}
.

For
v\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
r\geq 1
,
v^{\otimes r}\in\mathbb{R}^{n^{r}}
denotes the
r
-fold tensor power with

(v^{\otimes r})_{i_{1}\ldots i_{r}}=v_{i_{1}}\cdots v_{i_{r}}
.

For two random variables
X,Y
, we write
X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}Y
for equality in distribution,
X\perp\!\!\!\perp Y
for independence and we write
X\sim P
to mean that the random variable
X
has distribution
P
.

Lastly, for any
D\in\mathbb{N}
, we define
\exp_{\leq D}(v)=\sum_{k=0}^{D}\frac{v^{k}}{k!}
and we use
\Gamma(t)
to denote the Gamma function, which is define as
\Gamma(t)=\int_{0}^{\infty}x^{t-1}e^{-x}\textup{d}x
,
t>0
.

6
Proof that increasing FP implies MMSE lower bounds (Proof of Theorem
2.7
)

6.1
Cumulants background

We give some background on cumulants together with some identities that they satisfy.

Definition 6.1

(Cumulants)
.

Let
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
be jointly-distributed random variables. Their cumulant generating function (CGF) is the function

K(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})=\log\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}t_{i}X_{i}\right)\right]\right),

for all
t_{1},\dots,t_{n}\in\mathbb{R}
such that the expectation is finite. The joint cumulants are defined as

\kappa(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})=\left.\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t_{i}}K(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})\right|_{t_{1}=\ldots=t_{n}=0}.

Using the Taylor-Lagrange multivariate theorem, we have the following expansion into a series for all
t=(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
for which the following Taylor series converges,

K(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}}^{+\infty}\frac{\left.\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t_{i}^{\alpha_{i}}}K(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})\right|_{t_{1}=\ldots=t_{n}=0}}{\alpha!}\prod_{i=1}^{n}t_{i}^{\alpha_{i}}.

The proof for the following Proposition is standard, see e.g.
[
30
]
, Appendix D.

Proposition 6.2

(Vanishing under independence)
.

Let
a,b\geq 1
, and let
X_{1},\dots,X_{a},Y_{1},\dots,Y_{b}
be random variables such that

\{X_{i}\}_{i=1}^{a}
is independent of
\{Y_{j}\}_{j=1}^{b}
. Then

\kappa(X_{1},\dots,X_{a},Y_{1},\dots,Y_{b})=0.

The proof of the following Proposition is deferred to Section
B
.

Proposition 6.3

(Linearity in one argument)
.

Fix integers
n\geq 1
,
n\geq t\geq 1
and let
X,Y
,

Z_{1},\dots,Z_{t-1},Z_{t+1},\dots,Z_{n}
be jointly distributed random variables.
Then for any scalars
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\kappa\big(Z_{1},\dots,Z_{t-1},

\displaystyle\,aX+bY,\,Z_{t+1},\dots,Z_{n}\big)

\displaystyle=a\,\kappa\big(Z_{1},\dots,Z_{t-1},X,Z_{t+1},\dots,Z_{n}\big)+b\,\kappa\big(Z_{1},\dots,Z_{t-1},Y,Z_{t+1},\dots,Z_{n}\big).

The following is a standard formula for cumulants. For example, it can be found in
[
23
]
, Eq.
(2.9)
.

Proposition 6.4

(Moment-cumulant partition formulas)
.

Let
\mathcal{P}([n])
denote the set of all set partitions of
[n]:=\{1,\dots,n\}
.
For
\pi\in\mathcal{P}([n])
, write
|\pi|
for the number of blocks of
\pi
.
Assume
\mathbb{E}|X_{B}|<\infty
for every nonempty
B\subseteq[n]
. The joint cumulant
\kappa(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
satisfies the partition formula

\kappa(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})=\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{P}([n])}(|\pi|-1)!\,(-1)^{|\pi|-1}\,\prod_{B\in\pi}\mathbb{E}\Big[\prod_{i\in B}X_{i}\Big].

(6.1)

Moreover, the moments satisfy the moment - cumulant relation:

\mathbb{E}\Big[\prod_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}\Big]=\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{P}([n])}\ \prod_{B\in\pi}\kappa(X_{i}:i\in B).

(6.2)

The proof for the following Proposition can be found in
[
30
, Appendix D]
.

Proposition 6.5

(Cumulant recursion by splitting)
.

Let
Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n}
be random variables with
\mathbb{E}\big[\prod_{i\in T}|Y_{i}|\big]<\infty

for every
T\subseteq[n]
. Write
\kappa(Y_{i}:i\in T)
for the joint cumulant of the subfamily

\{Y_{i}\}_{i\in T}
, and use the convention
\prod_{i\in\varnothing}Y_{i}=1
.
Then

\kappa(Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n})=\mathbb{E}\Big[\prod_{i=1}^{n}Y_{i}\Big]-\sum_{\emptyset\neq S\subseteq[n]\setminus\{1\}}\kappa\big(Y_{i}:i\notin S\big)\,\mathbb{E}\Big[\prod_{i\in S}Y_{i}\Big].

(6.3)

Lemma 6.6

(Diagonal slice identity)
.

Let
X=\sum_{i=1}^{N}X_{i}
and let
\kappa_{m}(X)
be its
m
-th cumulant. Then

\frac{\kappa_{m}(X)}{m!}=\sum_{|\gamma|=m}\frac{\kappa_{\gamma}(X_{1},\dots X_{N})}{\gamma!}.

(6.4)

Proof.

By definition,

\kappa_{m}(X):=\kappa(\underbrace{X,\dots,X}_{m\ \text{times}}).

Using multilinearity of cumulants, i.e. Proposition
6.3
, and the definition
X=\sum_{i=1}^{N}X_{i}
,

\displaystyle\kappa_{m}(X)=\kappa\Big(\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{N}X_{i_{1}},\dots,\sum_{i_{m}=1}^{N}X_{i_{m}}\Big)=\sum_{i_{1},\dots,i_{m}=1}^{N}\kappa\big(X_{i_{1}},\dots,X_{i_{m}}\big).

For each
m
-tuple
(i_{1},\dots,i_{m})
, define its multiplicity vector

\gamma=(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{N}),\qquad\gamma_{k}:=\#\{j:i_{j}=k\}.

Then
|\gamma|=\sum_{k=1}^{N}\gamma_{k}=m
. For all
m
-tuples with the same multiplicity vector
\gamma
, the joint cumulant

\kappa(X_{i_{1}},\dots,X_{i_{m}})

is the same by symmetry and equals
\kappa_{\gamma}
by definition. The number of such
m
-tuples is the number of permutations of a multiset with
\gamma_{k}
copies of
k
, namely

\frac{m!}{\gamma!},
where
\gamma!:=\prod_{k=1}^{N}(\gamma_{k})!.
Hence,

\kappa_{m}(X)=\sum_{|\gamma|=m}\frac{m!}{\gamma!}\kappa_{\gamma},

which, dividing by
m!
, is (
6.4
).
∎

6.1.1
Key Lemmas

Some key lemmas are needed for us to prove the low-degree correlation upper bound, also describing the logic of the proof along the way. The proofs of these Lemmas are deferred to Section
6.3

First, we will make use of an easy lemma, which follows from an important result upper bounding the low-degree correlation from
[
30
]
via the cumulants of the prior. In particular, to produce our low-degree correlation upper bounds it suffices to bound the resulting weighted sum of the squared cumulants.

Lemma 6.7

(Correlation bound via cumulants)
.

For any GAM with SNR
\lambda
,

(\mathrm{Corr}_{P_{0}}^{\leq D})^{2}(\lambda)\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{0\leq|\alpha|\leq D}\frac{\kappa_{\alpha}^{2}(X_{i},\sqrt{\lambda}X_{1},\sqrt{\lambda}X_{2},\ldots,\sqrt{\lambda}X_{n})}{\alpha!},

where the quantity
\kappa_{\alpha}
is equal, for fixed
i\in[n]
, to the joint cumulant of the following collection of dependent random
variables: one instance of
X_{i}
, and
\alpha_{j}
copies of
X_{j}
for each
j\in[n]
.

We will also make use of this Lemma that gives two decompositions of the low degree MMSE for Gaussian Additive Models which we use interchangably. We start with some notation. Fix an integer
D\geq 0
and let

\mathbb{R}[Y]_{\leq D}

denote the space of polynomials in
Y
of total degree at most
D
. Define the degree-
D
MMSE of the vector
X
to be:

\mathrm{MMSE}^{\leq D}_{X}:=\inf_{f_{1},\dots,f_{n}\in\mathbb{R}[Y]_{\leq D}}\mathbb{E}\Big[\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(f_{i}(Y)-X_{i}\big)^{2}\Big].

We also define the degree-
D
coordinatewise correlations for all
i\in[n]
and the correlation for the vector
X
to be respectively:

\mathrm{Corr}^{\leq D}_{P_{0},i}:=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}g\in\mathbb{R}[Y]_{\leq D}\\
\mathbb{E}[g(Y)^{2}]=1\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}\big[g(Y)X_{i}\big],\qquad\mathrm{Corr}^{\leq D}_{P_{0}}:=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}f_{1},\dots,f_{n}\in\mathbb{R}[Y]_{\leq D}\\
\mathbb{E}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{i}(Y)^{2}=1\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}\Big[\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{i}(Y)X_{i}\Big].

Then, we have the following convenient formulas. The proof of this Lemma is deferred to Section
B
.

Lemma 6.8

(low-degree MMSE formula for the vector
X
)
.

Suppose we have a Gaussian Additive Model as described above with
X=(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Then:

\displaystyle\mathrm{MMSE}^{\leq D}_{X}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}[X_{i}^{2}]-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(\mathrm{Corr}^{\leq D}_{P_{0},i}\big)^{2}

(6.5)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\|X\|^{2}-\big(\mathrm{Corr}^{\leq D}_{P_{0}}\big)^{2}.

(6.6)

Next, we make use of the following easy result regarding the derivative of the log-MGF of a bounded random variable. This will be a key first step to bound the sum of the squared cumulants.

Lemma 6.9

(Bounded derivative of a truncated log-MGF)
.

Let
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
be a probability space and
Y_{n}
be
a real-valued random variable such that
|Y_{n}|\leq M_{n}
, almost surely, where
M_{n}>0
.
If the log-MGF of
Y_{n}
,

\phi_{n}(\theta):=\log\mathbb{E}\big[e^{\theta Y_{n}}\big]

exists on an open interval
I\subset\mathbb{R}
,
Then
\phi_{n}(\theta)
is differentiable on
I
and its derivative satisfies

(\phi_{n})^{\prime}(\theta)\leq M_{n},\qquad\text{for all }\theta\in I.

To leverage the Lemma above, we need the following slightly more involved proposition regarding the log-MGF Taylor expansion of a random variable around
0
and its relation with its cumulants. This proposition will now be crucial as combined with the previous Lemma allows us in the proof to obtain bounds on a weighted sum of some
similar-looking
cumulants.

Proposition 6.10

(Taylor expansion of log-MGF)
.

Let
T=\sum_{i=1}^{n}T_{i}
be the sum of
n
random variables
T_{i}
such that
|T|\leq M_{n}
, almost surely, for some
M_{n}>0
. If

\phi_{n}(\theta)
denotes the log MGF of
T
then the Taylor series of
\phi_{n}(\theta)
at
\theta=0
is

\phi_{n}(t)=\sum_{m\geq 1}\frac{\kappa_{m}(T)}{m!}t^{m}=\sum_{m\geq 1}t^{m}\sum_{|\gamma|=m}\frac{\kappa_{n,\gamma}(T_{1},\dots,T_{n})}{\gamma!},

and this series has radius of convergence at least

R_{n}\geq 1/eM_{n}.

Furthermore, for
|t|<1/(eM_{n})
the degree-
m
slice satisfies

\left|\sum_{|\gamma|=m}\frac{\kappa_{n,\gamma}}{\gamma!}t^{|\gamma|}\right|\leq\big(eM_{n}|t|\big)^{m}.

(6.7)

Lastly, our next two lemmas allows to translate our cumulant bounds from the previous two results, to the desired sum of the squared cumulants. To do this an important step would be to compare the cumulants of truncated random variables to the cumulants of original random variables. The following Lemma guarantees such a bound for sub-Weibull random variables.

Lemma 6.11
.

Fix
m\geq 1
, indices
i_{1},\dots,i_{m}\in\{1,\dots,n\}
and
M_{n}>0
.
Let
T_{i},i\in[n]
be random variables such that for some
\phi>0
,
T_{i}
are sub-Weibull
(\phi)
distributions for all
i\in[n]
.
If
T=\sum_{i=1}^{n}T_{i}
,
T_{i}^{\mathrm{tr}}=T_{i}1_{A_{n}}
and
\varepsilon_{n}=\mathbb{P}(|T|\geq M_{n})

then,

\big|\kappa_{m}(T_{i_{1}}^{\mathrm{tr}},\dots,T_{i_{m}}^{\mathrm{tr}})-\kappa_{m}(T_{i_{1}},\dots,T_{i_{m}})\big|\leq m^{C(\phi)m}\sqrt{\varepsilon_{n}}.

(6.8)

Finally, the last step would be to compare the cumulants produced by our application of the Taylor expansion as described in Proposition
6.10
with the squared cumulants obtained in
[
30
]
.

We introduce some notation. Let
X=(X_{1},\ldots,X_{N})\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
be a random vector, and let
X^{\prime}=(X_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,X_{N}^{\prime})

be an independent copy of
X
. For a multi-index
\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{N})\in\mathbb{N}^{N}
, we define

\widetilde{\kappa}_{\alpha}(X_{1},\dots,X_{N})\;:=\;\kappa_{\alpha}\big(X_{1}X_{1}^{\prime},\,X_{2}X_{2}^{\prime},\,\ldots,\,X_{N}X_{N}^{\prime}\big).

(6.9)

Lemma 6.12
.

Fix
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{N}
. Suppose that for some random variables
X_{1},\dots,X_{N}
it holds that for any multi-index
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{N}
with cardinality
|\alpha|\leq D_{n}
it holds that:

\kappa_{\alpha}(X_{1},\dots,X_{N})\geq 0.

(6.10)

Then, we can prove the following: for all
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{N}
such that
|\alpha|\leq D_{n}
:

\widetilde{\kappa}_{\alpha}(X_{1},\dots,X_{N})\geq\kappa_{\alpha}^{2}(X_{1},\dots,X_{N}).

6.2
Proof of the upper bound

Proof of Theorem
2.7
.

We denote by
\phi_{n}
the log-moment generating function (log-MGF) of
\langle X,X^{\prime}\rangle
, where
X,X^{\prime}
are two i.i.d. draws from
P_{0}
. We also denote by
\phi_{n}^{\mathrm{tr}}
the log MGF of the “truncated” random variable
\langle X,X^{\prime}\rangle 1_{(|\langle X,X^{\prime}\rangle|\leq q(D_{n}^{\prime}))}
.

Then, notice that using Lemma
6.9
applied for
M_{n}=q(D_{n}^{\prime})
we get that for all
\theta\in I:=\{t\in\mathbb{R}:\phi_{n}^{\mathrm{tr}}(t)<\infty\}
, it holds that:

\displaystyle(\phi_{n}^{\mathrm{tr}})^{\prime}(\theta)\leq q(D_{n}^{\prime}).

(6.11)

Let
R_{n}
be the Taylor expansion radius of
\phi_{n}^{\mathrm{tr}}
around
0
. By Proposition
6.10
,
(q(D_{n}^{\prime}))^{-1}\leq R_{n}
and therefore by the same Proposition we get that for all
|\theta|\leq(q(D_{n}^{\prime}))^{-1}
:

\phi_{n}^{tr}(\theta)=\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\theta^{m}\sum_{|\gamma|=m}\frac{\kappa_{n,\gamma}^{\mathrm{tr}}(X_{1}X_{1}^{\prime},\dots,X_{n}X_{n}^{\prime})}{\gamma!}.

Taking derivatives

(\phi_{n}^{tr})^{\prime}(\theta)=\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}m\theta^{m-1}\sum_{|\gamma|=m}\frac{\kappa_{n,\gamma}^{tr}(X_{1}X_{1}^{\prime},\dots,X_{n}X_{n}^{\prime})}{\gamma!}.

Now, again using Proposition
6.10
, if
r_{n}=\log n
, we get that for
|\theta|\leq(q(D_{n}^{\prime})r_{n})^{-1}
, we have
q:=q(D_{n}^{\prime})|\theta|\leq 1/r_{n}
, and the tail beyond degree
D_{n}+1
satisfies:

\left|\sum_{m\geq D_{n}+2}mt^{m-1}\sum_{|\gamma|=m}\frac{\kappa^{tr}_{n,\gamma}}{\gamma!}\right|\leq\sum_{m\geq D_{n}+2}(q(D_{n}^{\prime})|\theta|)^{m}\leq\frac{q^{D_{n}+2}}{1-q}\leq\frac{(1/r_{n})^{D_{n}+2}}{1-1/r_{n}}.

Therefore, for all
|\theta|\leq(q(D_{n}^{\prime})r_{n})^{-1}
we conclude,

\left|(\phi_{n}^{tr})^{\prime}(\theta)-\sum_{m=1}^{D_{n}+1}m\theta^{m-1}\sum_{|\gamma|=m}\frac{\kappa^{tr}_{n,\gamma}(X_{1}X_{1}^{\prime},\dots,X_{n}X_{n}^{\prime})}{\gamma!}\right|\leq\frac{(1/r_{n})^{D_{n}+2}}{1-1/r_{n}}.

(6.12)

which combined with (
6.11
) implies

\sum_{m=1}^{D_{n}+1}m\theta^{m-1}\sum_{|\gamma|=m}\frac{\kappa^{tr}_{n,\gamma}(X_{1}X_{1}^{\prime},\dots,X_{n}X_{n}^{\prime})}{\gamma!}-\frac{(1/r_{n})^{D_{n}+2}}{1-1/r_{n}}\leq q(D_{n}^{\prime}).

(6.13)

But, using Lemma
6.11
we get that for all
\gamma
such that
|\gamma|\leq D_{n}

\big|\kappa_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{tr}}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})-\kappa_{\gamma}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})\big|\leq|\gamma|^{C(\phi)|\gamma|}e^{-D_{n}^{\prime}}\leq e^{C(\phi)D_{n}\log D_{n}-D_{n}^{\prime}},

(6.14)

for some
C(\phi)>0
depending on the sub-Weibull constant
\phi>0
from Item 3 in Assumption
2.4
.
Finally, notice that there are
n^{D_{n}+1}
such multi-indices
\gamma
such that
|\gamma|\leq D_{n}+1
. Applying (
6.14
) to each term we get by direct algebra,

\displaystyle\sum_{|\gamma|\leq D_{n}+1}

\displaystyle\frac{\kappa^{tr}_{n,\gamma}(X_{1}X_{1}^{\prime},\dots,X_{n}X_{n}^{\prime})-\kappa_{n,\gamma}(X_{1}X_{1}^{\prime},\dots,X_{n}X_{n}^{\prime})}{\gamma!}|\gamma|\theta^{|\gamma|-1}

\displaystyle\geq-e^{C(\phi)D_{n}\log D_{n}-D_{n}^{\prime}}\cdot\sum_{|\gamma|\leq D_{n}+1}|\gamma|\theta^{|\gamma|-1}(\gamma!)^{-1}

\displaystyle\geq-e^{C(\phi)D_{n}\log D_{n}-D_{n}^{\prime}}\cdot\sum_{0<|\gamma|\leq D_{n}+1}\theta^{|\gamma|-1}

\displaystyle\geq-e^{C(\phi)D_{n}\log D_{n}+D_{n}\log n-D_{n}^{\prime}}\cdot\max\{\theta,\theta^{D_{n}}\}.

Assuming furthermore that
0<\theta<n^{C_{1}}
, for some positive universal constant
C_{1}>0
this allows to conclude for all
|\theta|\leq\min\{(q(D_{n}^{\prime})r_{n})^{-1},n^{C_{1}}\},

\sum_{|\gamma|\leq D_{n}+1}\frac{\kappa^{tr}_{n,\gamma}(X_{1}X_{1}^{\prime},\dots,X_{n}X_{n}^{\prime})-\kappa_{n,\gamma}(X_{1}X_{1}^{\prime},\dots,X_{n}X_{n}^{\prime})}{\gamma!}|\gamma|\theta^{|\gamma|-1}\geq-e^{C(\phi)D_{n}\log D_{n}+(C_{1}+1)D_{n}\log n-D_{n}^{\prime}}

(6.15)

Hence, (
6.13
) together with (
6.15
) imply for all
|\theta|\leq\min\{(q(D_{n}^{\prime})r_{n})^{-1},n^{C_{1}}\},

\sum_{m=1}^{D_{n}+1}m\theta^{m-1}\sum_{|\gamma|=m}\frac{\kappa_{n,\gamma}(X_{1}X_{1}^{\prime},\dots,X_{n}X_{n}^{\prime})}{\gamma!}-\frac{(1/r_{n})^{D_{n}+2}}{1-1/r_{n}}-e^{C(\phi)D_{n}\log D_{n}+(C_{1}+1)D_{n}\log n-D_{n}^{\prime}}\leq q(D_{n}^{\prime}).

(6.16)

But now, notice that our prior on the signal
X
satisfies Item
2
from Assumption
2.4
and therefore Lemma
6.12
together with elementary algebraic manipulations imply,

\displaystyle\sum_{m=1}^{D_{n}+1}m\theta^{m-1}\sum_{|\gamma|=m}\frac{\kappa_{n,\gamma}(X_{1}X_{1}^{\prime},\dots,X_{n}X_{n}^{\prime})}{\gamma!}

\displaystyle=\sum_{|\gamma|\leq D_{n}+1}\frac{\kappa_{n,\gamma}(X_{1}X_{1}^{\prime},\dots,X_{n}X_{n}^{\prime})}{\gamma!}|\gamma|\theta^{|\gamma|-1}

(6.17)

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{|\gamma|\leq D_{n}+1}\frac{\kappa^{2}_{n,\gamma}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})}{\gamma!}|\gamma|\theta^{|\gamma|-1}

(6.18)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{|\gamma|\leq D_{n}}\frac{\kappa^{2}_{n,\gamma}(X_{i},\sqrt{\theta}X_{1}\dots,\sqrt{\theta}X_{n})}{(\gamma+e_{i})!}(|\gamma|+1)

(6.19)

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{|\gamma|\leq D_{n}}\frac{\kappa^{2}_{n,\gamma}(X_{i},\sqrt{\theta}X_{1}\dots,\sqrt{\theta}X_{n})}{\gamma!},

(6.20)

where for the last inequality we used that
\gamma!(|\gamma|+1)\geq(\gamma+e_{i})!
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
.

Now by Theorem
6.7
we have for each
i=1,\ldots,n
,

(\mathrm{Corr}_{P_{0},i}^{\leq D})^{2}(\theta)_{\leq D_{n}}\leq\sum_{|\gamma|\leq D_{n}}\frac{\kappa^{2}_{n,\gamma}(X_{i},\sqrt{\theta}X_{1}\dots,\sqrt{\theta}X_{n})}{\gamma!}.

Summing then over
i
, and combining the last displayed inequality with (
6.20
) and with (
6.16
) we conclude for all
|\theta|\leq\min\{(q(D_{n}^{\prime})r_{n})^{-1},n^{C_{1}}\},

\displaystyle(\mathrm{Corr}^{\leq D}_{P_{0}})^{2}(\theta)-\frac{(1/r_{n})^{D_{n}+2}}{1-1/r_{n}}-e^{C(\phi)D_{n}\log D_{n}+(C_{1}+1)D_{n}\log n-D_{n}^{\prime}}\leq q(D_{n}^{\prime}).

(6.21)

Using Item 3 from Assumption
2.4
, substituing
r_{n}=\log n
and for
n
large enough we know that:

\displaystyle e^{(C(\phi)+C+1)D_{n}\log(nD_{n})-D_{n}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\leq e^{-D_{n}^{\prime}/2}\leq n^{-\frac{D_{n}}{2}\log n}\leq(\log n)^{-D_{n}/2}=o(q(D_{n}^{\prime}))

\displaystyle\frac{(1/r_{n})^{D_{n}+2}}{1-1/r_{n}}

\displaystyle\leq 2(\log n)^{-D_{n}}=o(q(D_{n}^{\prime})).

and therefore for all
|\theta|\leq\min\{(q(D_{n}^{\prime})r_{n})^{-1},n^{C_{1}}\},
if
n
is large enough,

\displaystyle(\mathrm{Corr}_{P_{0}}^{\leq D})^{2}(\theta)\leq 2q(D_{n}^{\prime}).

(6.22)

But, now observe the identity that for all
u>0,

u+\frac{d}{dq}\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ann},u}|_{q=q(D_{n})}=-\frac{d}{dq}\log\mathbb{P}(\langle X,X^{\prime}\rangle=q)|_{q=q(D_{n})}.

which combined with Condition 2 from Assumption
2.4
implies

\frac{1}{A_{n}}\left(u+\frac{d}{dq}\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ann},u}|_{q=q(D_{n})}\right)\leq(q(D_{n}^{\prime})r_{n})^{-1}.

Notice next that using Item 3 from Assumption
2.4
this quantity is upper bounded by
n^{C}/\log n
for some universal constant
C>0
.
Hence, we can pick
C_{1}=C
and then plug in
\theta=\frac{1}{A_{n}}\left(u+\frac{d}{dq}\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ann},u}|_{q=q(D_{n})}\right)\leq n^{C_{1}}
to (
6.22
) concluding the proof.
∎

6.3
Proofs of Key Lemmas

6.3.1
Proof of Lemma
6.9

Since
Y_{n}
is bounded by
M_{n}
,

|Y_{n}(\omega)|\leq M_{n}\qquad\text{for all }\omega\in\Omega.

Next,
\phi_{n}(\theta)
is finite on
I
and
Y_{n}
is bounded, by the dominated convergence theorem,

\phi_{n}

is differentiable on
I
and for each
\theta\in I
we have

(\phi_{n})^{\prime}(\theta)=\frac{\textup{d}}{\textup{d}\theta}\log\mathbb{E}\big[e^{\theta Y_{n}}\big]=\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\big[Y_{n}\big],

where
\mathbb{E}_{\theta}
denotes expectation with respect to the
tilted
measure

\mathbb{P}_{\theta}
defined by

\frac{d\mathbb{P}_{\theta}}{d\mathbb{P}}=\frac{e^{\theta Y_{n}^{\mathrm{tr}}}}{\mathbb{E}\big[e^{\theta Y_{n}^{\mathrm{tr}}}\big]}.

Using the pointwise bound on
Y_{n}
and the fact that
\mathbb{P}_{\theta}

is a probability measure, we obtain

\big|(\phi_{n})^{\prime}(\theta)\big|=\big|\mathbb{E}_{\theta}[Y_{n}]\big|\leq\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\big[|Y_{n}|\big]\leq M_{n},

for all
\theta\in I
, as claimed.

6.3.2
Proof of Proposition
6.10

Before moving to the proof we state the following Lemma the proof of which is deferred to Section
6.4
.

Lemma 6.13

(Cumulant bound for bounded variables)
.

Let
Z
be a real-valued random variable with
|Z|\leq B
almost surely for some
B>0
. Let
\kappa_{m}(Z)
denote its
m
-th cumulant for
m\geq 1
. Then

|\kappa_{m}(Z)|\leq m!(eB)^{m},\qquad m=1,2,\dots

(6.23)

We now combine Lemma
6.6
and Lemma
6.13
to prove Proposition
6.10
.

Proof of Proposition
6.10
.

Since
|T|\leq M_{n}
, the moment generating function (MGF)

\Phi_{n}(t):=\mathbb{E}e^{tT}

is an analytic function for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
. Moreover,
\Phi_{n}(0)=1
, so
\Phi_{n}(t)\neq 0
for
|t|
sufficiently small, and hence the log-MGF of
T

\phi_{n}(t):=\log\Phi_{n}(t)

is analytic in some neighborhood of the origin. Its Taylor expansion at
0
is

\phi_{n}(t)=\sum_{m\geq 1}\frac{\phi_{n}^{(m)}(0)}{m!}t^{m}=\sum_{m\geq 1}\frac{\kappa_{m}(T)}{m!}t^{m},

where
\phi_{n}^{(m)}(0)
denotes the
m
-th derivative and equals the
m
-th cumulant of
T
. By Lemma
6.13
applied for
Z=Y
and
B=M_{n}
, we have

\big|\kappa_{m}(T)\big|\leq m!\ (eM_{n})^{m}.

Hence the Taylor coefficients
a_{m}:=\kappa_{m}(T)/m!
satisfy

|a_{m}|\leq(eM_{n})^{m}.

By the root test,

\limsup_{m\to\infty}|a_{m}|^{1/m}\leq eM_{n},

and therefore the radius of convergence
R_{n}
of the Taylor series is bounded below by

R_{n}\geq\frac{1}{eM_{n}}.

In particular, the series converges absolutely for all
|t|<1/(eM_{n})
.
We now consider the following series

\sum_{\gamma\neq 0}\frac{\kappa_{n,\gamma}(T_{1},\dots,T_{n})}{\gamma!}t^{|\gamma|}=\sum_{m\geq 1}t^{m}\sum_{|\gamma|=m}\frac{\kappa_{n,\gamma}(T_{1},\dots,T_{n})}{\gamma!}.

By Lemma
6.6
, the coefficient of
t^{m}
equals

\sum_{|\gamma|=m}\frac{\kappa_{n,\gamma}}{\gamma!}=\frac{\kappa_{m}(T)}{m!}.

Therefore

\phi_{n}(t)=\sum_{m\geq 1}\frac{\kappa_{m}(T)}{m!}t^{m}=\sum_{m\geq 1}t^{m}\sum_{|\gamma|=m}\frac{\kappa_{n,\gamma}}{\gamma!}.

Moreover, by Lemma
6.13
,

\left|\sum_{|\gamma|=m}\frac{\kappa_{n,\gamma}}{\gamma!}t^{|\gamma|}\right|=\left|\frac{\kappa_{m}(T)}{m!}t^{m}\right|\leq(eM_{n})^{m}|t|^{m}=(eM_{n}|t|)^{m},

which is (
6.7
) completing the proof.
∎

6.3.3
Proof of Lemma
6.11

In this section we aim to prove Lemma
6.11
. To do this we start with some notation and by proving two results (Lemmas
6.14
and
6.15
) that will help us. Fix an integer
n\geq 1
, let
T_{1},\dots,T_{n}
be any real-valued random variables defined on a common probability space and define
