T:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}T_{i}.
Fix
m\geq 1
and indices
i_{1},\dots,i_{m}\in\{1,\dots,N\}
. We are interested in the
m
-th joint cumulant of the random variables

T_{i_{1}},\dots,T_{i_{m}}\quad\text{and}\quad T_{i_{1}}^{\mathrm{tr}},\dots,T_{i_{m}}^{\mathrm{tr}},\qquad\textrm{for all}\qquad i_{1},\dots,i_{m}\in[N]

and in bounding their difference. For convenience define

L:=\max_{1\leq t\leq m}\big(\mathbb{E}|T_{i_{t}}|^{m}\big)^{1/m},\qquad U:=\max_{1\leq t\leq m}\big(\mathbb{E}|T_{i_{t}}|^{2m}\big)^{1/(2m)}.

(6.24)

To simplify notation we set

Z_{t}:=T_{i_{t}},\ \ Z_{t}^{\mathrm{tr}}:=T_{i_{t}}^{\mathrm{tr}}=T_{i_{t}}1_{A_{n}},\ \ t=1,\dots,m,

so that

\kappa_{m}(T_{i_{1}},\dots,T_{i_{m}})=\kappa_{m}(Z_{1},\dots,Z_{m}),\qquad\text{and}\qquad\kappa_{m}(T_{i_{1}}^{\mathrm{tr}},\dots,T_{i_{m}}^{\mathrm{tr}})=\kappa_{m}(Z_{1}^{\mathrm{tr}},\dots,Z_{m}^{\mathrm{tr}}).

Also,
L,U
will now be:

L:=\max_{1\leq t\leq m}\big(\mathbb{E}|Z_{t}|^{m}\big)^{1/m},\qquad U:=\max_{1\leq t\leq m}\big(\mathbb{E}|Z_{t}|^{2m}\big)^{1/(2m)},

(6.25)

assuming that
\mathbb{E}|Z_{t}|^{2m}<\infty
for all
t
so that everything is well defined. Next, given a block
B\subseteq\{1,\dots,m\}
, define

Z_{B}:=\prod_{t\in B}Z_{t},\qquad s:=|B|.

We will use the following Lemma the proof of which is deferred to Sextion
6.4
.

Lemma 6.14

(Block bounds under truncation by
A_{n}
)
.

Let
B\subseteq\{1,\dots,m\}
be a block of size
s=|B|
.
With
L,U
as in (
6.24
) and
\varepsilon_{n}=\mathbb{P}(A_{n}^{c})
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big|Z_{B}\big|\leq L^{s}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathbb{E}\big[|Z_{B}|1_{A_{n}^{c}}\big]\leq U^{s}\sqrt{\varepsilon_{n}}.

(6.26)

Using this Lemma we are ready to prove the following result that relates the multivariate cumulants of truncated random variables back to the original ones.

Lemma 6.15

(Cumulants under truncation)
.

Fix
m\geq 1
and indices
i_{1},\dots,i_{m}\in\{1,\dots,N\}
.
Assume that
\mathbb{E}|T_{i_{t}}|^{2m}<\infty
for all
t
.
Let
L,U
be given by (
6.24
) and
\varepsilon_{n}=\mathbb{P}(|Y|\geq M_{n})
as above.
Then,

\big|\kappa_{m}(Z_{1}^{\mathrm{tr}},\dots,Z_{m}^{\mathrm{tr}})-\kappa_{m}(Z_{1},\dots,Z_{m})\big|\leq(m-1)!\,m^{m}\,2^{m}\,(U\vee L)^{m}\,\sqrt{\varepsilon_{n}}.

(6.27)

Proof.

Recall the partition formula (
6.1
) applied to the truncated variables

Z_{t}^{\mathrm{tr}}=X_{i_{t}}^{\mathrm{tr}}
and the original ones
Z_{t}=X_{i_{t}}
:

\kappa_{m}(Z_{1}^{\mathrm{tr}},\dots,Z_{m}^{\mathrm{tr}})-\kappa_{m}(Z_{1},\dots,Z_{m})=\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{m}}(|\pi|-1)!(-1)^{|\pi|-1}\Delta_{\pi},

where

\Delta_{\pi}:=\prod_{B\in\pi}\mathbb{E}\Big[\prod_{t\in B}Z_{t}^{\mathrm{tr}}\Big]-\prod_{B\in\pi}\mathbb{E}\Big[\prod_{t\in B}Z_{t}\Big].

Fix
\pi\in\Pi_{m}
. For a block
B\in\pi
, we have denoted
Z_{B}:=\prod_{t\in B}Z_{t}
.
Then

\mathbb{E}\Big[\prod_{t\in B}Z_{t}^{\mathrm{tr}}\Big]=\mathbb{E}\big[Z_{B}1_{A_{n}}\big]=\mathbb{E}[Z_{B}]-\mathbb{E}\big[Z_{B}1_{A_{n}^{c}}\big].

Thus,

\prod_{B\in\pi}\mathbb{E}\Big[\prod_{t\in B}Z_{t}^{\mathrm{tr}}\Big]=\prod_{B\in\pi}\Big(\mathbb{E}[Z_{B}]-\mathbb{E}\big[Z_{B}1_{A_{n}^{c}}\big]\Big).

Subtracting
\prod_{B\in\pi}\mathbb{E}[Z_{B}]
and expanding the product, we obtain

\Delta_{\pi}=\sum_{\emptyset\neq S\subseteq\pi}(-1)^{|S|}\prod_{B\in S}\mathbb{E}\big[Z_{B}1_{A_{n}^{c}}\big]\prod_{B\notin S}\mathbb{E}[Z_{B}].

Taking absolute values and using Lemma
6.14
gives, for every nonempty

S\subseteq\pi
,

\prod_{B\in S}\big|\mathbb{E}[Z_{B}1_{A_{n}^{c}}]\big|\,\prod_{B\notin S}\big|\mathbb{E}[Z_{B}]\big|\leq\prod_{B\in S}\mathbb{E}\big[|Z_{B}|1_{A_{n}^{c}}\big]\,\prod_{B\notin S}\mathbb{E}|Z_{B}|\leq\prod_{B\in S}U^{|B|}\sqrt{\varepsilon_{n}}\;\prod_{B\notin S}L^{|B|}.

Write
r(S):=\sum_{B\in S}|B|
and note that
\sum_{B\in\pi}|B|=m
. Then

\prod_{B\in S}U^{|B|}\sqrt{\varepsilon_{n}}\;\prod_{B\notin S}L^{|B|}=(\sqrt{\varepsilon_{n}})^{|S|}\,U^{r(S)}\,L^{m-r(S)}\leq\sqrt{\varepsilon_{n}}\,(U\vee L)^{m},

since
|S|\geq 1
implies
(\sqrt{\varepsilon_{n}})^{|S|}\leq\sqrt{\varepsilon_{n}}

(we always have
\varepsilon_{n}\leq 1
) and
U^{r}L^{m-r}\leq(U\vee L)^{m}
.
Therefore,

|\Delta_{\pi}|\leq\sum_{\emptyset\neq S\subseteq\pi}\sqrt{\varepsilon_{n}}\,(U\vee L)^{m}=(2^{|\pi|}-1)\,(U\vee L)^{m}\,\sqrt{\varepsilon_{n}}\leq 2^{m}\,(U\vee L)^{m}\,\sqrt{\varepsilon_{n}},

using
|\pi|\leq m
for any partition
\pi
of
[m]
. Plugging this estimate into the partition formula,

\big|\kappa_{m}(Z_{1}^{\mathrm{tr}},\dots,Z_{m}^{\mathrm{tr}})-\kappa_{m}(Z_{1},\dots,Z_{m})\big|\leq\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{m}}(|\pi|-1)!\,|\Delta_{\pi}|\leq 2^{m}(U\vee L)^{m}\sqrt{\varepsilon_{n}}\,\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{m}}(|\pi|-1)!,

which almost yields our first inequality. To get (
6.27
), observe that there are at most
m^{m}
set partitions of
\{1,\dots,m\}
,
and
(|\pi|-1)!\leq(m-1)!
for all
\pi\in\Pi_{m}
, so

\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{m}}(|\pi|-1)!\leq(m-1)!m^{m}.

\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{m}}(|\pi|-1)!\leq(m-1)!\,m^{m}
, hence

\big|\kappa_{m}(Z_{1}^{\mathrm{tr}},\dots,Z_{m}^{\mathrm{tr}})-\kappa_{m}(Z_{1},\dots,Z_{m})\big|\leq(m-1)!\,m^{m}\,2^{m}\,(U\vee L)^{m}\,\sqrt{\varepsilon_{n}}.

(6.28)

which is our desired inequality and the proof is complete.
∎

If we want a bound that is uniform in the choice of indices
(i_{1},\dots,i_{m})
, we may simply replace (
6.24
) by

L_{n}:=\max_{1\leq i\leq n}\big(\mathbb{E}|T_{i}|^{m}\big)^{1/m},\qquad U_{n}:=\max_{1\leq i\leq n}\big(\mathbb{E}|T_{i}|^{2m}\big)^{1/(2m)}.

(6.29)

Then
L\leq L_{n}
and
U\leq U_{n}
for all
(i_{1},\dots,i_{m})
, and Lemma
6.15
holds with
L,U
replaced by
L_{n},U_{n}
.

Now we are ready to prove Lemma
6.11
that we need for our Main Theorem.

Proof of Lemma
6.11
.

Note that
2^{m}m!\leq m^{m\log 2}
and
\sqrt{\varepsilon}\leq 1
trivially. Therefore, for our result to hold it suffices to prove that it holds that

\max\{L_{n},U_{n}\}\leq m^{c_{1}(\phi)m},

for all sufficiently large
n
and for a constant
c_{1}(\phi)>0
where
\phi
is the sub-Weibul constant of
X_{i}
. We will show that this is true for all priors that satisfy Assumption
2.4
. Indeed, item 3 from Assumption
2.4
implies that all coordinates
(T_{i})_{i=1}^{N}
are uniformly sub-Weibull
(\phi/2)
(since
T_{i}=X_{i}X_{i}^{\prime}
). The fact that
T_{i}
are sub-Weibull
(\phi/2)
for some
\phi>0
is equivalent, from Definition
2.2
, with the following:
for a constant
K
independent of
i
and
n
:

(\mathbb{E}|T_{i}|^{p})^{1/p}\leq C_{\phi}Kp^{2/\phi}\qquad\text{for all }p\geq 1,

and therefore

L\leq C_{\phi}Km^{2/\phi},\qquad U\leq C_{\phi}K(2m)^{2/\phi}.

Hence
\max\{U_{n},L_{n}\}^{m}\leq(2^{1/\phi}C_{\phi}K)^{m}m^{2m/\phi}

and therefore our proof is complete.

∎

6.3.4
Proof of Lemma
6.12

In this section we aim to prove Lemma
6.12
. We start with some notation and a definition. For
N
random variables
X_{1},\dots,X_{N}
remember we denoted

\widetilde{\kappa}_{\alpha}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n}):=\kappa_{\alpha}(X_{1}X_{1}^{\prime},\dots,X_{n}X_{n}^{\prime}),

where the vector
X^{\prime}=(X_{1}^{\prime},\dots,X_{n}^{\prime})
is an i.i.d. copy of
X=(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
. In this subsection we give a closed form for the cumulants
\widetilde{\kappa}_{\alpha}
that we defined above. Our results are based on an important result from
[
23
]
. We start with the following definition:

Definition 6.16
.

For any partition
\pi\in\mathcal{P}([n])
we denote by
E(\pi)
to be the “induced” graph from the partition
\pi
, i.e., the graph
G
on the vertices
[n],
which is the union of
|\pi|
cliques
(C_{S})_{S\in\pi}
, i.e.,
G=\bigcup_{S\in\pi}C_{S},
where for each part of the partition
S\in\pi
we denote by
C_{S}
the clique on the vertices
i\in S
.

Theorem 6.17

(Eq.
(3.3)
from
[
23
]
)
.

Suppose we have
n
random variables
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
. Then, for any partition
\pi\in\mathcal{P}([n])
, with parts
S_{1},\dots,S_{|\pi|}
, the following holds:

\displaystyle\kappa(\prod_{i\in S_{1}}X_{i},\dots,\prod_{i\in S_{|\pi|}}X_{i})=\sum_{\pi^{\prime}\in G}\prod_{S^{\prime}\in\pi^{\prime}}\kappa(S^{\prime}).

(6.30)

where
G:=\{\pi^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}([n])\ \text{s.t.}\ E(\pi)\cup E(\pi^{\prime})\text{ is a connected graph }\}
.

Using this result we can prove for the cumulants
\widetilde{\kappa}
that we are interested in the following result. Define, for any
N\in\mathbb{N}
, the partition
\pi_{\mathrm{pair}}^{N}=\{\{1,2\},\{3,4\},\dots,\{2N-1,2N\}\}
.

Lemma 6.18
.

Consider
X_{1},\dots,X_{N}
be a sequence of identically distributed but not necessarily independent random variables. Fix any
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{N}
and let
X^{\prime}=(X_{1}^{\prime},\dots,X_{N}^{\prime})
be an i.i.d. copy of
X=(X_{1},\dots,X_{N})
. Denote
\widetilde{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{2|\alpha|}
the vector defined as

\widetilde{X}:=(\underbrace{X_{1},X_{1}^{\prime}\dots,X_{1},X_{1}^{\prime}}_{\alpha_{1}-\text{times}},\dots,\underbrace{X_{N},X_{N}^{\prime}\dots,X_{N},X_{N}^{\prime}}_{\alpha_{N}-\text{times}}).

Then, their joint cumulant
\widetilde{\kappa}_{\alpha}:=\kappa_{\alpha}(X_{1}X_{1}^{\prime},\dots,X_{N}X_{N}^{\prime})
, satisfies,

\displaystyle\widetilde{\kappa}_{\alpha}:=\kappa_{\alpha}(X_{1}X_{1}^{\prime},\dots,X_{N}X_{N}^{\prime})=\kappa(\widetilde{X}_{1}\widetilde{X}_{2},\dots,\widetilde{X}_{2|\alpha|-1}\widetilde{X}_{2|\alpha|})=\sum_{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})\in J}\prod_{S\in\pi_{1},\ S\in\pi_{2}}\kappa(S)\kappa(S^{\prime})

(6.31)

where
J:=\{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})\in\mathcal{P}(\{1,3,\dots,2|\alpha|-1\})\times\mathcal{P}(\{2,,4,\dots,2|\alpha|\})\ \text{s.t.}\ E(\pi_{1})\cup E(\pi_{2})\cup E(\pi_{\mathrm{pair}}^{|\alpha|})\text{ is a connected graph}\}
.

Proof.

Applying Theorem
6.17
for the
\pi_{\mathrm{pair}}^{|\alpha|}=\{\{1,2\},\{3,4\},\dots,\{2|\alpha|-1,2|\alpha|\}\}
partition for the coordinates of
\widetilde{X}
we have

\widetilde{\kappa}_{\alpha}:=\kappa_{\alpha}(X_{1}X_{1}^{\prime},\dots,X_{N}X_{N}^{\prime})=\sum_{\pi^{\prime}\in G}\prod_{S^{\prime}\in\pi^{\prime}}\kappa(S^{\prime})

where
G:=\{\pi^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}([2|\alpha|])\ \text{s.t.}\ E(\pi_{\mathrm{pair}}^{|\alpha|})\cup E(\pi^{\prime})\text{ is a connected graph}\}
. In words, the first summation is over all partitions
\pi^{\prime}
such that when combined with the partition

\pi_{\mathrm{pair}}^{|\alpha|}

they produce a connected graph.

Our proof readily follows by establishing the following two steps. First, we show that every partition pair in
J
appears in the summation defined by
G
. Second we argue that no other partition contributes a non-zero value to our summation.

For the first part, let
(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})\in J
. Since
\pi_{1}
partitions the odd indices (from the vector
X
) and
\pi_{2}
partitions the even indices (from the vector
X^{\prime}
), their union
\pi=\pi_{1}\cup\pi_{2}
is a partition on
[2|\alpha|]
. Moreover, since
E(\pi)=E(\pi_{1})\cup E(\pi_{2})
,
\pi
belongs to
G
, completing this step.

For the second step, consider a partition
\pi^{*}
that is not in
J
but in
G
. Then
\pi^{*}
cannot be decomposed into two separate partitions of the
X
variables and the
X^{\prime}
variables. Then we argue
\prod_{S^{\prime}\in\pi^{*}}\kappa(S^{\prime})=0
. Indeed, in this case
\pi^{*}
contains a block
S^{*}
, and indices
i,j
such that
X_{i}\in S^{*}
and
X^{\prime}_{j}\in S^{*}
. But the vectors
X,X^{\prime}
are independent and therefore
\kappa(S^{*})=0
, from Proposition
6.2
, concluding the second step.

∎

We are now ready to prove Lemma
6.12
.

Proof of Lemma
6.12
.

We use formula (
6.31
) for
\widetilde{\kappa}
and notice two things about it. First, if
\psi=\{1,2,\dots,|\alpha|\}
then
(\psi,\psi)\in J
. Therefore,

\widetilde{\kappa}_{\alpha}(X_{1},\dots,X_{N})=\kappa_{\alpha}^{2}(X_{1},\dots,X_{N})+\sum_{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})\in J\setminus\{(\psi,\psi)\}}\prod_{S\in\pi_{1},\ S\in\pi_{2}}\kappa(S)\kappa(S^{\prime}).

Moreover, any other term in the remaining sum is a product that only contains term which joint cumulants of the random variables
X_{1},\dots,X_{N}
, possibly repeated (recall
X^{\prime}
is i.i.d. with
X
). But all such cumulants are nonnegative according to the assumption of our Corollary, and therefore,

\widetilde{\kappa}_{\alpha}(X_{1},\dots,X_{N})\geq\kappa_{\alpha}^{2}(X_{1},\dots,X_{N}).

∎

6.4
Proofs of Auxiliary Lemmas

In this Section we prove Lemmas
6.13
and
6.14
.

Proof of Lemma
6.13
.

Recall the moment - cumulant formula for a single variable
6.4
:

\kappa_{m}(Z)=\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{P}([m])}(|\pi|-1)!(-1)^{|\pi|-1}\prod_{B\in\pi}\mathbb{E}\Big[Z^{|B|}\Big].

Taking absolute values and using
|Z|\leq B
,

|\kappa_{m}(Z)|\leq\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{P}([m])}(|\pi|-1)!\prod_{B\in\pi}\mathbb{E}|Z|^{|B|}\leq\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{P}([m])}(|\pi|-1)!\prod_{B\in\pi}B^{|B|}=B^{m}\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{P}([m])}(|\pi|-1)!.

Let
|\pi|=k
be the number of blocks. If
S(m,k)
is the number of ways to partition a set with
m
objects into
k
non-empty subsets, then

\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{P}([m])}(|\pi|-1)!=\sum_{k=1}^{m}(k-1)!S(m,k).

Using the standard bound
S(m,k)\leq k^{m}/k!
, we obtain

(k-1)!S(m,k)\leq(k-1)!\frac{k^{m}}{k!}=k^{m-1},

and hence

\sum_{k=1}^{m}(k-1)!S(m,k)\leq\sum_{k=1}^{m}k^{m-1}\leq m^{m}.

Finally, the crude bound
m^{m}\leq e^{m}m!
yields

\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{P}([m])}(|\pi|-1)!\leq e^{m}m!,

so altogether

|\kappa_{m}(Z)|\leq B^{m}e^{m}m!=m!(eB)^{m}.

This proves (
6.23
).

∎

Proof of Lemma
6.14
.

We use Hölder’s inequality with equal exponents.
For (
6.26
), we apply Hölder with weights
p_{t}
for every
t\in B
where we choose
p_{t}=s
for all
t\in B
. Then,
\sum_{t\in B}1/p_{t}=s\cdot(1/s)=1
, so

\mathbb{E}\big|Z_{B}\big|=\mathbb{E}\prod_{t\in B}|Z_{t}|\leq\prod_{t\in B}\big(\mathbb{E}|Z_{t}|^{s}\big)^{1/s}.

Since
s\leq m
and
L^{p}
-norms are nondecreasing in
p
,

\big(\mathbb{E}|Z_{t}|^{s}\big)^{1/s}\leq\big(\mathbb{E}|Z_{t}|^{m}\big)^{1/m}\leq L

for each
t
. Hence

\mathbb{E}|Z_{B}|\leq\prod_{t\in B}L=L^{s}.

For the second equation, apply Cauchy–Schwarz inequality:

\mathbb{E}\big[|Z_{B}|1_{A_{n}^{c}}\big]\leq\big(\mathbb{E}|Z_{B}|^{2}\big)^{1/2}\sqrt{\varepsilon_{n}}.

Now
|Z_{B}|^{2}=\prod_{t\in B}|Z_{t}|^{2}
, so applying Hölder again with exponents
s
,

\mathbb{E}|Z_{B}|^{2}=\mathbb{E}\prod_{t\in B}|Z_{t}|^{2}\leq\prod_{t\in B}\big(\mathbb{E}|Z_{t}|^{2s}\big)^{1/s}.

Since
2s\leq 2m
,

\big(\mathbb{E}|Z_{t}|^{2s}\big)^{1/(2s)}\leq\big(\mathbb{E}|Z_{t}|^{2m}\big)^{1/(2m)}\leq U,

and therefore

\big(\mathbb{E}|Z_{t}|^{2s}\big)^{1/s}=\Big(\big(\mathbb{E}|Z_{t}|^{2s}\big)^{1/(2s)}\Big)^{2}\leq U^{2}.

Thus

\mathbb{E}|Z_{B}|^{2}\leq\prod_{t\in B}U^{2}=U^{2s},

and hence

\mathbb{E}\big[|Z_{B}|1_{A_{n}^{c}}\big]\leq(U^{2s})^{1/2}\sqrt{\varepsilon_{n}}=U^{s}\sqrt{\varepsilon_{n}}.

∎

7
Proof that decreasing FP implies MMSE upper bounds (Proof of Theorem
2.9
)

In this section and only by we study the quantiles of random variables beyond the overlap between two draws from the prior. For this reason, we provide a more general definition to Definition
2.1
.

Definition 7.1

(Quantile function)
.

Let
Y_{n}
be a real-valued random variable. For any
D>0
, we define the quantile
q(D)
by

q(D)\coloneqq\inf\left\{y\in\mathbb{R}\mid\mathbb{P}(|Y_{n}|\leq y)\geq 1-e^{-D}\right\}.

We also make a note of the following remark.

Remark 7.2
.

Notice that another way to define the quantile function
q(D)
, which is equivalent to Definition
7.1
, is as the time-rescaled version of the generalized inverse function of the cumulative distribution function of
|Y_{n}|
, i.e,
q(D)=F_{|Y_{n}|}^{-1}(1-e^{-D})
(we direct the reader to e.g.,
[
32
, Chap 21.1]
for more details).

7.1
Hermite Background

We consider the
probabilist’s Hermite polynomials
on
\mathbb{R}^{N}
. For a multi-index
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{N}
and a vector
x\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
, the multivariate Hermite polynomial
H_{\alpha}(x)
is defined via the tensor product:

H_{\alpha}(x)=\prod_{j=1}^{N}h_{\alpha_{j}}(x_{j}),

(7.1)

where
h_{k}(z)=(-1)^{k}e^{z^{2}/2}\frac{d^{k}}{dz^{k}}e^{-z^{2}/2}
denotes the univariate Hermite polynomial of degree
k
.

These polynomials form an orthogonal basis for the Hilbert space
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{N},\gamma)
, where
\gamma
is the standard Gaussian measure. The inner product is defined as
\langle f,g\rangle_{L^{2}}=\mathbb{E}_{Z}[f(Z)g(Z)]
with
Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{N})
. The orthogonality relation is given by:

\mathbb{E}_{Z}[H_{\alpha}(Z)H_{\beta}(Z)]=\alpha!\delta_{\alpha,\beta},

(7.2)

where
\delta_{\alpha,\beta}
is the Kronecker delta and
\alpha!=\prod_{j=1}^{d}\alpha_{j}!
.

Similarly, for a multi-index
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{N}
and two vectors
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
, abusing notation, we denote

x^{\alpha}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}^{\alpha_{i}}\quad\text{ and }\quad(xy)^{\alpha}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}(x_{i}y_{i})^{\alpha_{i}}.

To derive the properties required for the proof, we rely on the following fundamental Translation Identity given by Proposition
7.3
, which expresses a shifted Hermite polynomial in terms of the basis centered at the origin. We give the proof for completeness in Section
B
.

Proposition 7.3

(Translation Identity)
.

For any
z,\mu\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
and
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{N}
:

H_{\alpha}(z+\mu)=\sum_{\gamma\leq\alpha}\binom{\alpha}{\gamma}\mu^{\alpha-\gamma}H_{\gamma}(z).

(7.3)

Using this expansion, we prove the following lemmas.

Lemma 7.4

(Gaussian Mean Shift)
.

Let
Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{N})
and
\mu\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
. Then:

\mathbb{E}_{Z}[H_{\alpha}(\mu+Z)]=\mu^{\alpha}.

(7.4)

Proof.

Applying Proposition
7.3
to
H_{\alpha}(Z+\mu)
, we have:

\mathbb{E}_{Z}[H_{\alpha}(Z+\mu)]=\sum_{\gamma\leq\alpha}\binom{\alpha}{\gamma}\mu^{\alpha-\gamma}\mathbb{E}_{Z}[H_{\gamma}(Z)].

By the orthogonality of Hermite polynomials,
\mathbb{E}[H_{\gamma}(Z)]=\langle H_{\gamma},H_{0}\rangle_{L^{2}}=0
for all
\gamma\neq 0
. The only non-vanishing term corresponds to
\gamma=0
(where
H_{0}\equiv 1
). Thus:

\mathbb{E}_{Z}[H_{\alpha}(Z+\mu)]=\binom{\alpha}{0}\mu^{\alpha}\cdot 1=\mu^{\alpha}.

∎

Lemma 7.5

(Expectation of Shifted Products)
.

Let
Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{N})
and
\mu\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
. Then:

\mathbb{E}_{Z}\left[H_{\alpha}(\mu+Z)H_{\beta}(\mu+Z)\right]=\sum_{r\leq\min(\alpha,\beta)}r!\binom{\alpha}{r}\binom{\beta}{r}\mu^{\alpha+\beta-2r}.

(7.5)

Proof.

We expand both polynomials using the Translation Identity (Proposition
7.3
):

\mathbb{E}_{Z}\left[H_{\alpha}(Z+\mu)H_{\beta}(Z+\mu)\right]=\mathbb{E}_{Z}\left[\left(\sum_{\gamma\leq\alpha}\binom{\alpha}{\gamma}\mu^{\alpha-\gamma}H_{\gamma}(Z)\right)\left(\sum_{\delta\leq\beta}\binom{\beta}{\delta}\mu^{\beta-\delta}H_{\delta}(Z)\right)\right].

Using the linearity of expectation, we can interchange the sum and expectation. Due to the orthogonality relation (
7.2
), the cross-terms where
\gamma\neq\delta
vanish. We sum only over indices
r
common to both expansions (
r\leq\alpha
and
r\leq\beta
):

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{Z}\left[H_{\alpha}(\mu+Z)H_{\beta}(\mu+Z)\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{r\leq\min(\alpha,\beta)}\binom{\alpha}{r}\mu^{\alpha-r}\binom{\beta}{r}\mu^{\beta-r}\mathbb{E}_{Z}[H_{r}(Z)^{2}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{r\leq\min(\alpha,\beta)}\binom{\alpha}{r}\mu^{\alpha-r}\binom{\beta}{r}\mu^{\beta-r}(r!)

\displaystyle=\sum_{r\leq\min(\alpha,\beta)}r!\binom{\alpha}{r}\binom{\beta}{r}\mu^{\alpha+\beta-2r}.

∎

7.2
Key Lemmas

To prove our low-degree correlation lower bound, we construct and analyze a nearly-optimal low-degree polynomial. To do this we need a few key lemmas which we state here and defer their proofs to Section
7.4
. First, one simple observation is made by the following:

Lemma 7.6

(Correlation is non-decreasing)
.

\mathrm{Corr}^{\leq D_{n}}_{P_{0}}(h)
is a non-decreasing function in
h>0
.

Then we need a quantitative error bound between the degree-
D
Taylor expansion of the exponential function and the exponential function restricted near zero. This lemma is a fundamental tool behind our construction of the optimal low-degree polynomial, and in particular how the quantiles of the overlap of the prior appear in its analysis.

Lemma 7.7

(Quantitative Error Bound for Truncated Exponential Moments)
.

Let
V
be a random variable with finite moments up to order
2D_{n}+2
, where
D_{n}\in\mathbb{N}
is a degree,
C>0
be a constant,
(q(D^{\prime}))_{D^{\prime}>0}
the quantiles of
|V|
defined in Definition
7.1
.
Then,

\left|\mathbb{E}[V\exp_{\leq D_{n}}(V)]-\mathbb{E}[Ve^{V}1_{|V|\leq q(CD_{n})}]\right|\leq\frac{e^{q(CD_{n})}\left\lVert V\right\rVert_{D_{n}+2}^{D_{n}+2}}{(D_{n}+1)!}+e^{-CD_{n}/2}\sum_{k=0}^{D_{n}}\frac{\left\lVert V\right\rVert_{2k+2}^{k+1}}{k!}

(7.6)

where
\left\lVert V\right\rVert_{p}=(\mathbb{E}[|V|^{p}])^{1/p}
denotes the
L_{p}
-norm of
V
. Furthermore, if there exists constants
C_{1},C_{2}>0
, such that
\sup_{p\in[2D_{n}+2]}\left\lVert V\right\rVert_{p}\leq C_{1}
and
q(CD_{n})\leq C_{2}
, then

\left|\mathbb{E}[V\exp_{\leq D_{n}}(V)]-\mathbb{E}[Ve^{V}1_{|V|\leq q(CD_{n})}]\right|=o(e^{-CD_{n}/2}).

(7.7)

Next, we need a lemma comparing the quantiles of the sum of three identically distributed random variables to the quantiles of each one of them.

Lemma 7.8

(Quantile Domination)
.

Let
X,X^{\prime},X^{\prime\prime}
be three independent samples from the prior
P_{0}
,
\widetilde{q}(D)
be the quantile function of the sum
S=|\langle X^{\prime},X^{\prime\prime}\rangle|+|\langle X,X^{\prime}\rangle|+|\langle X,X^{\prime\prime}\rangle|
using Definition
7.1
, and
q(D)
be the quantile function of
\langle X,X^{\prime}\rangle
accordingly. There exists a universal constant
C\geq 1
such that for all
D\geq\ln 3
,

q(D)\leq\widetilde{q}(D)\leq Cq(D).

(7.8)

We also use an interesting easy fact that one can express the expectation of any function against any probability measure, with respect to the quantiles of the measure.

Lemma 7.9

(Change of variable)
.

Suppose a random variable
X\geq 0
, and its quantile function
q
is defined as in Definition
7.1
. Then for any integrable function
g
, that is,
\mathbb{E}|g(X)|<\infty
, we have

\mathbb{E}g(X)=\int_{0}^{\infty}g(q(t))e^{-t}\textup{d}t.

(7.9)

Finally, we will also need a general
L_{p}
norm control of a random variable using the growth rate of its quantiles.

Lemma 7.10

(Moment Growth Bound)
.

Let
V
be a non-negative random variable. Assume its quantile function
q(t)
satisfies the upper growth condition
q(t)\leq CB_{n}t^{\kappa}
for all
t\geq t_{0}
. Then, there exists a constant
K
, such that for any
p\in\mathbb{N}
, the
L_{p}
norm is bounded by:

\left\lVert V\right\rVert_{p}\leq KB_{n}p^{\kappa}

(7.10)

for some constant
K
depending only on
C
and
\kappa
.

7.3
Proof of the lower bound

Proof of Theorem
2.9
.

According to Lemma
7.6
,
\mathrm{Corr}^{\leq D_{n}}_{P_{0}}(h)
is a monotonically non-decreasing function in
h>0
,
and by (
2.6
) together with Assumption
2.8
,

\lambda+\frac{d}{dq}\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ann},\lambda}\bigg|_{q=q(D_{n})}=-\left.\frac{d}{dq}\log\mathbb{P}(\langle X,X^{\prime}\rangle=t)\right|_{q=q(D_{n})}\geq\frac{c}{q(D_{n})},

it suffices to prove

\displaystyle\mathrm{Corr}^{\leq D_{n}}_{P_{0}}\left(\frac{c}{{q(D_{n})}}\right)\geq\frac{C}{D_{n}^{\kappa}}\sqrt{q(D_{n})}.

(7.11)

In order to show this, we use the probabilistic method and construct a polynomial estimator based on a reference set drawn from the prior, which will satisfy with high probability (
7.11
).

Let
\alpha
denote a multi-index element in
\mathbb{N}^{n}
and
\lambda=\sqrt{c/q(D_{n})}
in the following. We define the function
W(Y|X)
via a truncated Hermite expansion of degree
D_{n}
:

W(Y|X)=\sum_{|\alpha|\leq D_{n}}\frac{1}{\alpha!}X^{\alpha}H_{\alpha}(Y).

(7.12)

Let
M\in\mathbb{N}
be a sufficiently large number which we will choose later and
\mathcal{S}=\{X_{1},\dots,X_{M}\}
be a set of
M
independent samples from the prior distribution
P_{0}
. Consider the degree-
D_{n}
polynomial estimator defined by

p(Y)=\frac{1}{M}\sum_{k=1}^{M}W(Y|\sqrt{\lambda}X_{k})\sqrt{\lambda}X_{k},

Conditional on
X
,
we utilize Proposition
7.3
to get

\mathbb{E}_{Z}[H_{\alpha}(Y)|X]=\mathbb{E}_{Z}[H_{\alpha}(\sqrt{\lambda}X+Z)|X]=(\sqrt{\lambda}X)^{\alpha}.

Then direct algebra gives,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda}}\mathbb{E}\langle p(Y),X\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{M}\mathbb{E}_{Z,X}\left[\sum_{k=1}^{M}W(Y|\sqrt{\lambda}X_{k})\langle X_{k},X\rangle\right]=\frac{1}{M}\sum_{k=1}^{M}\sum_{|\alpha|\leq D_{n}}\frac{1}{\alpha!}(\sqrt{\lambda}X_{k})^{\alpha}\mathbb{E}_{Z,X}[\langle X_{k},X\rangle H_{\alpha}(Y)]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{M}\sum_{k=1}^{M}\sum_{|\alpha|\leq D_{n}}\frac{1}{\alpha!}\mathbb{E}_{X}[\langle X_{k},X\rangle(\sqrt{\lambda}X_{k})^{\alpha}(\sqrt{\lambda}X)^{\alpha}]=\frac{1}{M}\sum_{k=1}^{M}\sum_{|\alpha|\leq D_{n}}\frac{1}{\alpha!}\mathbb{E}_{X}[\langle X_{k},X\rangle(\lambda X_{k}X)^{\alpha}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{M}\sum_{k=1}^{M}\mathbb{E}_{X}[\langle X_{k},X\rangle\exp_{\leq D_{n}}(\lambda\langle X_{k},X\rangle)].

Since
X_{k},k\in[M]
is independently sampled from the same prior
P_{0}
, by the strong law of large numbers, with high probability, as long as
|\mathbb{E}_{X,X^{\prime}}[\langle X,X^{\prime}\rangle\exp_{\leq D_{n}}(\lambda\langle X,X^{\prime}\rangle)]|<+\infty
,

\left|\frac{1}{M}\sum_{k=1}^{M}\mathbb{E}_{X}[\langle X_{k},X\rangle\exp_{\leq D_{n}}(\lambda\langle X_{k},X\rangle)]-\mathbb{E}_{X,X^{\prime}}[\langle X,X^{\prime}\rangle\exp_{\leq D_{n}}(\lambda\langle X,X^{\prime}\rangle)]\right|=o_{M}(1).

(7.13)

Combining the above, we conclude,

\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda}}\mathbb{E}\langle p(Y),X\rangle=\mathbb{E}[\langle X^{\prime},X\rangle\exp_{\leq D_{n}}{(\lambda\langle X^{\prime},X\rangle)}]+o_{M}(1).

(7.14)

We now invoke the identity given in Lemma
7.5
to get

\mathbb{E}_{Z}[H_{\alpha}(\sqrt{\lambda}X+Z)H_{\beta}(\sqrt{\lambda}X+Z)]|X=\sum_{r\leq\min(\alpha,\beta)}r!\binom{\alpha}{r}\binom{\beta}{r}(\sqrt{\lambda}X)^{\alpha+\beta-2r}.

Therefore, by direct algebra

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\lambda}{\mathbb{E}[\|p(Y)\|^{2}]}

\displaystyle={\frac{1}{M^{2}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{M}\mathbb{E}_{X,Z}[W(Y|\sqrt{\lambda}X_{i})W(Y|\sqrt{\lambda}X_{j})\langle X_{i},X_{j}\rangle]}

\displaystyle={\frac{1}{M^{2}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{M}\langle X_{i},X_{j}\rangle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\alpha|\leq D_{n},\,|\beta|\leq D_{n}\end{subarray}}\frac{(\sqrt{\lambda}X_{i})^{\alpha}(\sqrt{\lambda}X_{j})^{\beta}}{\alpha!\beta!}\mathbb{E}_{X,Z}[H_{\alpha}(Y)H_{\beta}(Y)]}

\displaystyle={\frac{1}{M^{2}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{M}\langle X_{i},X_{j}\rangle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha,\beta,r\\
|\alpha|\leq D_{n},|\beta|\leq D_{n}\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{r!(\alpha-r)!(\beta-r)!}(\sqrt{\lambda}X_{i})^{\alpha}(\sqrt{\lambda}X_{j})^{\beta}\mathbb{E}_{X}[(\sqrt{\lambda}X)^{\alpha+\beta-2r}]}

\displaystyle={\frac{1}{M^{2}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{M}\langle X_{i},X_{j}\rangle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha,\beta,r\\
|\alpha|\leq D_{n},|\beta|\leq D_{n}\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{r!(\alpha-r)!(\beta-r)!}\mathbb{E}_{X}[(\lambda X_{i}X)^{\alpha-r}(\lambda X_{j}X)^{\beta-r}(\lambda X_{i}X_{j})^{r}]}

\displaystyle={\frac{1}{M^{2}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{M}\langle X_{i},X_{j}\rangle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|r|\leq D_{n}\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{r!}\mathbb{E}_{X}[\exp_{\leq D_{n}-|r|}(\lambda\langle X_{i},X\rangle)\exp_{\leq D_{n}-|r|}(\lambda\langle X_{j},X\rangle)](\lambda X_{i}X_{j})^{r}}

\displaystyle={\frac{1}{M^{2}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{M}\langle X_{i},X_{j}\rangle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\leq D_{n}\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{k!}\mathbb{E}_{X}[\exp_{\leq D_{n}-k}(\lambda\langle X_{i},X\rangle)\exp_{\leq D_{n}-k}(\lambda\langle X_{j},X\rangle)](\lambda\langle X_{i},X_{j}\rangle)^{k}}.

We use three observations: for a given
D\in\mathbb{N}
,
\exp_{\leq D}(x)
is monotone increasing for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
; for all
x\geq 0
,
\exp_{\leq D}(x)
is monotone increasing for all
D\in\mathbb{N}
; and for any
x,y\geq 0
, any
D_{1},D_{2}\in\mathbb{N}
,
\exp_{\leq D_{1}}(x)\exp_{\leq D_{2}}(y)\leq\exp_{\leq D_{1}+D_{2}}(x+y)
. Define

h(x,y)=|\langle x,y\rangle|\mathbb{E}_{X}[\exp_{\leq 3D_{n}}(\lambda(|\langle x,X\rangle|+|\langle y,X\rangle|+|\langle x,y\rangle|))],

then the above quantity can be bounded as follows,

\displaystyle{\frac{1}{M^{2}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{M}|\langle X_{i},X_{j}\rangle|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\leq D_{n}\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{k!}\mathbb{E}_{X}[\exp_{\leq D_{n}-k}(\lambda|\langle X_{i},X\rangle|)\exp_{\leq D_{n}-k}(\lambda|\langle X_{j},X\rangle|)]|\lambda\langle X_{i},X_{j}\rangle|^{k}}

\displaystyle\leq{\frac{1}{M^{2}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{M}|\langle X_{i},X_{j}\rangle|\mathbb{E}_{X}[\exp_{\leq D_{n}}(\lambda|\langle X_{i},X\rangle|)\exp_{\leq D_{n}}(\lambda|\langle X_{j},X\rangle|)]\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\leq D_{n}\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{k!}|\lambda\langle X_{i},X_{j}\rangle|^{k}}

\displaystyle={\frac{1}{M^{2}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{M}|\langle X_{i},X_{j}\rangle|\exp_{\leq D_{n}}(|\lambda\langle X_{i},X_{j}\rangle|)\mathbb{E}_{X}[\exp_{\leq D_{n}}(\lambda|\langle X_{i},X\rangle|)\exp_{\leq D_{n}}(\lambda|\langle X_{j},X\rangle|)]}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{M^{2}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{M}|\langle X_{i},X_{j}\rangle|\mathbb{E}_{X}[\exp_{\leq 3D_{n}}(\lambda(|\langle X_{i},X\rangle|+|\langle X_{j},X\rangle|+|\langle X_{i},X_{j}\rangle|))]=\frac{1}{M^{2}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{M}h(X_{i},X_{j}).

For the same reason, since
X_{k},k\in[M]
is independently sampled from the same prior
P_{0}
, applying the strong law of large numbers for U-statistics
[
32
, Problem 12.15]
, as long as
|\mathbb{E}_{X^{\prime},X^{\prime\prime}}[h(X^{\prime},X^{\prime\prime})]|<+\infty
,

\left|{\frac{1}{M^{2}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{M}h(X_{i},X_{j})}-{\mathbb{E}_{X^{\prime},X^{\prime\prime}}[h(X^{\prime},X^{\prime\prime})]}\right|=o_{M}(1).

(7.15)

In short, we established that with high probability,

\frac{1}{\lambda}{\mathbb{E}[\|p(Y)\|^{2}]}\leq\mathbb{E}_{X,X^{\prime},X^{\prime\prime}}[|\langle X^{\prime},X^{\prime\prime}\rangle|\exp_{\leq 3D_{n}}\left({\lambda(|\langle X^{\prime},X^{\prime\prime}\rangle|+|\langle X,X^{\prime}\rangle|+|\langle X,X^{\prime\prime}\rangle|)}\right)]+o_{M}(1).

Denoting the sum
S=|\langle X^{\prime},X^{\prime\prime}\rangle|+|\langle X,X^{\prime}\rangle|+|\langle X,X^{\prime\prime}\rangle|
, and the overlap
A=\langle X,X^{\prime}\rangle
, then the above is controlled by the degree-
3D_{n}
truncation of the exponential of the sum of inner products:

|\langle X^{\prime},X^{\prime\prime}\rangle|\exp_{\leq 3D_{n}}\left({\lambda(|\langle X^{\prime},X^{\prime\prime}\rangle|+|\langle X,X^{\prime}\rangle|+|\langle X,X^{\prime\prime}\rangle|)}\right)\leq S\exp_{\leq 3D_{n}}(\lambda S).

(7.16)

Therefore, combining with (
7.14
), there exists a sufficiently large
M
, such that the correlation ratio satisfies the following lower bound with high probability,

\mathrm{Corr}^{\leq D_{n}}_{P_{0}}(\lambda)\geq\frac{\mathbb{E}\langle p(Y),X\rangle}{\sqrt{\mathbb{E}[\|p(Y)\|^{2}]}}\geq\frac{\mathbb{E}[A\exp_{\leq D_{n}}{(\lambda A)}]}{2\sqrt{\mathbb{E}[S\exp_{\leq 3D_{n}}(\lambda S)]}}.

We apply Lemma
7.7
to the numerator
\mathbb{E}[A\exp_{\leq D_{n}}{(\lambda A)}]
, so define
\varepsilon_{1}(D_{n})>0
, such that

\left|\mathbb{E}[A\exp_{\leq D_{n}}{(\lambda A)}]-\mathbb{E}\left[Ae^{\lambda A}1_{|A|\leq q(C_{t}D_{n})}\right]\right|=\varepsilon_{1}(D_{n}).

Similarly, since
\lambda=c/{q(D_{n})}
, define
\varepsilon_{2}(D_{n})>0
such that

|\mathbb{E}[S\exp_{\leq 3D_{n}}(\lambda S)]-\mathbb{E}[Se^{\lambda S}1_{S\leq\widetilde{q}(C_{t}D_{n})}]|=\varepsilon_{2}(D_{n})

. Then applying Lemmas
7.7
and
7.8
, and according to the Assumption
2.8
(1), there exists some constant
C^{\prime}>0
, such that

\mathbb{E}[S\exp_{\leq 3D_{n}}(\lambda S)]\leq\mathbb{E}[Se^{\lambda S}1_{S\leq\widetilde{q}(C_{t}D_{n})}]+\varepsilon_{2}(D_{n})\leq e^{\lambda\widetilde{q}(C_{t}D_{n})}\widetilde{q}(C_{t}D_{n})+\varepsilon_{2}(D_{n})\leq e^{C^{\prime}}C^{\prime}\cdot q(D_{n})+\varepsilon_{2}(D_{n}).

We will verify
\varepsilon_{1}(D_{n})=\varepsilon_{2}(D_{n})=o_{n}(q(D_{n}))
such that they are negligible comparing to
q(D_{n})
for large
n
.
Starting with
\varepsilon_{1}(D_{n})
, we use the scaling
\lambda=c/{q(D_{n})}
. The scaled variable is
Z=\lambda A
.
By the Assumption
2.8
(1) and Lemma
7.10
, for any
p\in\mathbb{N}
such that
p\leq 2D_{n}+2
,

\left\lVert Z\right\rVert_{p}=\frac{c\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{p}}{q(D_{n})}\leq\frac{cKB_{n}p^{\kappa}}{cB_{n}D_{n}^{\kappa}}\leq C_{1}\left(\frac{p}{D_{n}}\right)^{\kappa}\leq C_{1}3^{\kappa}.

Therefore,
\sup_{p\in[2D_{n}+2]}\left\lVert Z\right\rVert_{p}\leq C_{1}3^{\kappa}
. Since
Z
is just a rescale of the random variable
A
, the quantile function of
|Z|
is
q_{|Z|}(t)=\lambda q(t)
, we have

q_{|Z|}(C_{t}D_{n})=\frac{cq(C_{t}D_{n})}{q(D_{n})}\leq\frac{cCB_{n}(C_{t}D_{n})^{\kappa}}{cB_{n}D_{n}^{\kappa}}={CC_{t}^{\kappa}}.

By Lemma
7.7
,
\varepsilon_{1}(D_{n})=o(e^{-C_{t}D_{n}/2})
. According to our assumption,
D_{n}=\omega(1)
,
q(D_{n})\geq cB_{n}D_{n}^{\kappa}=\omega(e^{-CD_{n}})
. Choosing the truncation constant
C_{t}
(in the definition of the integral range) such that
C_{t}=20C>0
sufficiently large, then this verifies
\varepsilon_{1}(D_{n})=o(q(D_{n}))
.

Similarly, Lemma
7.8
gives a matching upper bound on
\widetilde{q}(D)
, i.e. there exists some new constant
\widetilde{C}>\widetilde{c}>0
, such that
\widetilde{c}B_{n}t^{\kappa}\leq\widetilde{q}(t)\leq\widetilde{C}B_{n}t^{\kappa}
, where
B_{n},\kappa
are the same as given in Assumption
2.8
(1). Following the same logic,
Z=\lambda S
also satisfies
\sup_{p\in[2D_{n}+2]}\left\lVert Z\right\rVert_{p}\leq\widetilde{C}_{1}
and
q_{|Z|}(C_{t}D_{n})\leq\widetilde{C}_{2}
for some constants
\widetilde{C}_{1},\widetilde{C}_{2}>0
. By Lemma
7.7
,
\varepsilon_{2}(D_{n})=o(e^{-C_{t}D_{n}/2})=o(q{(D_{n})})
.

Since
|\mathbb{E}[A\exp_{\leq D_{n}}{(\lambda A)}]|\leq|\mathbb{E}[Ae^{\lambda A}1_{|A|\leq q(C_{t}D_{n})}]|+\varepsilon_{1}(D_{n})\leq q(C_{t}D_{n})e^{\lambda q(C_{t}D_{n})}+\varepsilon_{1}(D_{n})<+\infty
, we have verified that

|\mathbb{E}_{X,X^{\prime}}[\langle X,X^{\prime}\rangle\exp_{\leq D_{n}}(\lambda\langle X,X^{\prime}\rangle)]|<+\infty
. It is the same to verify
|\mathbb{E}_{X^{\prime},X^{\prime\prime}}[h(X^{\prime},X^{\prime\prime})]|<+\infty
. Therefore, (
7.13
) and (
7.15
) are valid.

Therefore, the lower bound simplifies to

\mathrm{Corr}^{\leq D_{n}}_{P_{0}}(\lambda)\geq c\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[Ae^{\lambda A}1_{|A|\leq q(D_{n})}\right]}{\sqrt{q(D_{n})}}-o(1).

Since
xe^{x}\geq x+\frac{1}{2}x^{2}
holds for all
x\in[-1,1]
, substituting
x=\lambda A
and truncating on the event
|x|\leq\lambda q(D_{n})\leq 1
, we have

\mathbb{E}\left[Ae^{\lambda A}1_{|A|\leq q(D_{n})}\right]\geq\mathbb{E}[A1_{|A|\leq q(D_{n})}]+\frac{1}{2}\lambda\mathbb{E}[A^{2}1_{|A|\leq q(D_{n})}]\geq\mathbb{E}[A]-|\mathbb{E}[A1_{|A|\geq q(D_{n})}]|+\frac{1}{2}\lambda\mathbb{E}[A^{2}1_{|A|\leq q(D_{n})}].

Plugging in
g(|A|)=|A|^{2}1_{|A|\leq q(D_{n})}
in Lemma
7.9
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[A^{2}1_{|A|\leq q(D_{n})}]

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}q(t)^{2}1_{q(t)\leq q(D_{n})}e^{-t}\textup{d}t=\int_{0}^{D_{n}}q(t)^{2}e^{-t}\textup{d}t.

By Assumption
2.8
(1),
c_{1}B_{n}t^{\kappa}\leq q(t)
for all
t\leq D_{n}
, there exists a new absolute constant
c^{\prime}>0
, such that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{D_{n}}q(t)^{2}e^{-t}\textup{d}t

\displaystyle\geq c_{1}B_{n}^{2}\int_{0}^{D_{n}}t^{2\kappa}e^{-t}\textup{d}t=c_{1}B_{n}^{2}\left(\Gamma(2\kappa+1)-\int_{D_{n}}^{\infty}t^{2\kappa}e^{-t}\textup{d}t\right)

\displaystyle\geq c_{1}B_{n}^{2}\left(\Gamma(2\kappa+1)-CD_{n}^{2\kappa+1}e^{-D_{n}}\right)\geq c^{\prime}B_{n}^{2}.

By our definition of
A
,
\mathbb{E}[A]=\mathbb{E}_{X,X^{\prime}\sim P_{0},X\perp X^{\prime}}[\langle X,X^{\prime}\rangle]=\|\mathbb{E}X\|^{2}\geq 0
, and using Lemma
7.10
,
|\mathbb{E}[A1_{|A|\geq q(D_{n})}]|\leq\sqrt{\mathbb{E}[A^{2}]\mathbb{P}(|A|\geq q(D_{n}))}\leq e^{-D_{n}/2}\mathbb{E}[A^{2}]\leq Ce^{-D_{n}/2}B_{n}^{2}
. To sum up, we get

\mathbb{E}\left[Ae^{\lambda A}1_{|A|\leq q(D_{n})}\right]\geq c^{\prime}B_{n}^{2}(\lambda-C^{\prime}e^{-D_{n}/2}).

Since
\lambda=c/{q(D_{n})}\geq cB_{n}^{-1}D_{n}^{-\kappa}=\omega(e^{-D_{n}/2})
, finally, there exists a constant
C>0
such that

\mathrm{Corr}^{\leq D_{n}}_{P_{0}}(\lambda)\geq\frac{c^{\prime}}{2}\cdot\frac{B_{n}D_{n}^{-\kappa}}{\sqrt{q(D_{n})}}\geq CD_{n}^{-2\kappa}\sqrt{q(D_{n})}.

∎

7.4
Proofs of Key Lemmas

Proof of Lemma
7.6
.

We will prove for any
0<h_{1}<h_{2}
,
\mathrm{Corr}^{\leq D_{n}}_{P_{0}}(h_{1}^{2})\leq\mathrm{Corr}^{\leq D_{n}}_{P_{0}}(h_{2}^{2})
.
Denote
Y_{1}=h_{1}X+Z
,
Y_{2}=h_{2}X+Z
.
Since
X\perp\!\!\!\perp Z\sim N(0,I_{N})
, then there exists a random vector
W\perp\!\!\!\perp Y_{2}
,
W\sim N(0,I_{N})
,

Y_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\frac{h_{1}}{h_{2}}Y_{2}+\sqrt{1-\frac{h_{1}^{2}}{h_{2}^{2}}}W\,.

According to
[
30
, Claim A.2]
, adding noise can only make the MMSE larger, which leads to

\mathrm{MMSE}^{\leq D}_{P_{0}}\left(Y_{1}\right)=\mathrm{MMSE}^{\leq D}_{P_{0}}\left(\frac{h_{1}}{h_{2}}Y_{2}+\sqrt{1-\frac{h_{1}^{2}}{h_{2}^{2}}}W\right)\geq\mathrm{MMSE}^{\leq D}_{P_{0}}\left(\frac{h_{1}}{h_{2}}Y_{2}\right)=\mathrm{MMSE}^{\leq D}_{P_{0}}(Y_{2}).

Therefore, simple algebra yields

(\mathrm{Corr}_{P_{0}}^{\leq D})^{2}(h_{1}^{2})=\mathbb{E}_{X\sim P_{0}}[\|X\|^{2}_{2}]-\mathrm{MMSE}^{\leq D}_{P_{0}}(Y_{1})\leq\mathbb{E}_{X\sim P_{0}}[\|X\|^{2}_{2}]-\mathrm{MMSE}^{\leq D}_{P_{0}}(Y_{2})=(\mathrm{Corr}_{P_{0}}^{\leq D})^{2}(h_{2}^{2}).

∎

Proof of Lemma
7.7
.

Let
\Delta
denote the quantity of interest:

\Delta=\left|\mathbb{E}[V\exp_{\leq D_{n}}(V)]-\mathbb{E}[Ve^{V}1_{|V|\leq q(CD_{n})}]\right|.

We decompose the expectation of the polynomial term over the event
E=\{|V|\leq q(CD_{n})\}
and its complement
E^{c}=\{|V|>q(CD_{n})\}
:

\mathbb{E}[V\exp_{\leq D_{n}}(V)]=\mathbb{E}[V\exp_{\leq D_{n}}(V)1_{E}]+\mathbb{E}[V\exp_{\leq D_{n}}(V)1_{E^{c}}].

Substituting this into
\Delta
and applying the triangle inequality:

\Delta\leq\underbrace{\mathbb{E}\left[|V|\cdot\left|\exp_{\leq D_{n}}(V)-e^{V}\right|1_{E}\right]}_{T_{1}}+\underbrace{\mathbb{E}\left[|V|\cdot\left|\exp_{\leq D_{n}}(V)\right|1_{E^{c}}\right]}_{T_{2}}.

Bounding
T_{1}
:

By Taylor’s Theorem with Lagrange remainder, for any
v
, there exists
\xi
between
0
and
v
such that
e^{v}-\exp_{\leq D_{n}}(v)=\frac{e^{\xi}}{(D_{n}+1)!}v^{D_{n}+1}
.
On the event
E=\{|V|\leq q(CD_{n})\}
, we have
|V|\leq q(CD_{n})
. Since
q(CD_{n})>0
,
\xi\leq\max(0,V)\leq q(CD_{n})
, which implies

\left|\exp_{\leq D_{n}}(V)-e^{V}\right|1_{E}\leq\frac{e^{q(CD_{n})}}{(D_{n}+1)!}|V|^{D_{n}+1}1_{E}.

Substituting this into
T_{1}
and relaxing the indicator
1_{E}\leq 1
:

T_{1}\leq\mathbb{E}\left[|V|\frac{e^{q(CD_{n})}}{(D_{n}+1)!}|V|^{D_{n}+1}\right]=\frac{e^{q(CD_{n})}}{(D_{n}+1)!}\left\lVert V\right\rVert_{D_{n}+2}^{D_{n}+2}.

Bounding
T_{2}
:

We expand the polynomial and use the triangle inequality:

T_{2}=\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\sum_{k=0}^{D_{n}}\frac{V^{k+1}}{k!}\right|1_{|V|>q(CD_{n})}\right]\leq\sum_{k=0}^{D_{n}}\frac{1}{k!}\mathbb{E}\left[|V|^{k+1}1_{|V|>q(CD_{n})}\right].

We apply the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality to the expectation term:

\mathbb{E}\left[|V|^{k+1}1_{|V|>q(CD_{n})}\right]\leq\sqrt{\mathbb{E}[|V|^{2k+2}]}\sqrt{\mathbb{E}[1_{|V|>q(CD_{n})}^{2}]}=\left\lVert V\right\rVert_{2k+2}^{k+1}\sqrt{\mathbb{P}(|V|>q(CD_{n}))}.

Using the tail assumption
\mathbb{P}(|V|>q(CD_{n}))\leq e^{-CD_{n}}
, we have
\sqrt{\mathbb{P}(|V|>q(CD_{n}))}\leq e^{-CD_{n}/2}
. Thus:

T_{2}\leq e^{-CD_{n}/2}\sum_{k=0}^{D_{n}}\frac{\left\lVert V\right\rVert_{2k+2}^{k+1}}{k!}.

Furthermore, if
\sup_{p\in[2D_{n}+2]}\left\lVert V\right\rVert_{p}\leq C_{1}
and
q(CD_{n})\leq C_{2}
, then using the bound
(D_{n}+1)!\geq(D_{n}/e)^{D_{n}}
, there exists a sufficiently large constant
C_{3}>0
,

\displaystyle T_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{e^{C_{2}}\left\lVert V\right\rVert_{D_{n}+2}^{D_{n}+2}}{(D_{n}+1)!}\leq e^{C_{2}}C_{1}^{2}\left(\frac{eC_{1}}{D_{n}}\right)^{D_{n}}=o\left(e^{-C_{3}D_{n}}\right);

\displaystyle T_{2}

\displaystyle\leq e^{-CD_{n}/2}\sum_{k=0}^{D_{n}}\frac{\left\lVert V\right\rVert_{2k+2}^{k+1}}{k!}\leq e^{-CD_{n}/2}\sum_{k=0}^{D_{n}}\frac{C_{1}^{k+1}}{k!}\leq C_{1}e^{C_{1}}e^{-CD_{n}/2}=o(e^{-CD_{n}/2}).

So in this case,
\Delta=o(e^{-CD_{n}/2})
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
7.8
.

Since
S=|\langle X^{\prime},X^{\prime\prime}\rangle|+|\langle X,X^{\prime}\rangle|+|\langle X,X^{\prime\prime}\rangle|
. Let
V_{1},V_{2},V_{3}
be these identically distributed terms, which shares the same distribution as
|\langle X,X^{\prime}\rangle|
. Using a union bound:

\mathbb{P}(S>3q(D))\leq\sum_{k=1}^{3}\mathbb{P}(V_{k}>q(D))=3e^{-D}=e^{-(D-\ln 3)}.

(7.17)

Since
q(t)
is increasing,
q(D-\ln 3)\leq q(D)
. Thus, we can choose
C
such that the event
\{S\leq Cq(D)\}
holds with probability at least
1-e^{-D}
, which implies
\widetilde{q}(D)\leq Cq(D)
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
7.9
.

By the definition of the quantile function given by Definition
7.1
, we know that
q(t)=F_{X}^{-1}(1-e^{-t})
for all
t\geq 0
. Using then
[
32
, Lemma 21.1]
,
F_{X}^{-1}(U)\sim X
for a
U\sim\mathrm{Unif}[0,1]
. Hence, for any integrable function
g
we have

\mathbb{E}g(X)=\mathbb{E}g(F_{X}^{-1}(U))=\int_{0}^{1}g(F_{X}^{-1}(u))\textup{d}u=\int_{0}^{\infty}g(F_{X}^{-1}(1-e^{-t}))e^{-t}\textup{d}t=\int_{0}^{\infty}g(q(t))e^{-t}\textup{d}t.

∎

Proof of Lemma
7.10
.

We express the
p
-th moment using the tail integral representation and apply the change of variables formula given by (
7.9
), the integral transforms to:

\mathbb{E}[V^{p}]=\int_{0}^{\infty}(q(t))^{p}e^{-t}\textup{d}t.

Since
q(t)\leq CB_{n}t^{\kappa}
and handling small
t
by a constant
M
,:

\mathbb{E}[V^{p}]\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}(CB_{n}t^{\kappa})^{p}e^{-t}\textup{d}t=(CB_{n})^{p}\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{\kappa p}e^{-t}\textup{d}t=(CB_{n})^{p}\Gamma(\kappa p+1).

(7.18)

Taking the
p
-th root, and using the upper bound for Gamma function given in
[
33
, Pg. 26]
:

(\Gamma(\kappa p+1))^{1/p}\leq\left(C(\kappa p/e)^{\kappa p}\right)^{1/p}=C^{1/p}(\kappa p/e)^{\kappa}.

(7.19)

Thus,

\left\lVert V\right\rVert_{p}=(\mathbb{E}[V^{p}])^{1/p}\leq CB_{n}(\kappa/e)^{\kappa}p^{\kappa}.

(7.20)

Setting
K=C(\kappa/e)^{\kappa}
, we obtain the desired bound
\left\lVert V\right\rVert_{p}\leq KB_{n}p^{\kappa}
.
∎

8
Two broad classes of GAMs with positive cumulants

In this section we present two key Lemmas that help us verify Item 1 from Assumption
2.4
, i.e. that the priors on the GAMs of interest have low-order nonnegative cumulants. In fact, we will establish that two broad families of GAMs satisfy the condition, which will include as particular cases the models of interest in our applications. We state them here to a degree of generality for independent interest.

More specifically, all GAMs considered in this section satisfy the following.

Fix integer
t\geq 1
. Suppose we have
v_{1},\dots,v_{n}\sim P_{1}
i.i.d. draws from some distribution
P_{1}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R})
that has finite moments and
Z_{1},\dots,Z_{t}
some i.i.d. random variables for all
j\in[t]
that they are either symmetric around
0
or satisfy
Z_{j}\geq 0,j\in[t]
a.s. Furthermore,
Z_{j}
are independent from
v_{i}
for all
i\in[n]
and
j\in[t]
.
Then we assume that for all
1\leq i\leq N
,

\displaystyle X_{i}=Z_{\alpha(i)}\cdot f_{i}(v_{1},\dots,v_{n})

(8.1)

where
f_{i}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
are monomials with respect to
v_{1},\dots,v_{n}
, i.e.,
f(v_{1},...,v_{n})=v_{1}^{k_{1}}\dots v_{n}^{k_{n}}
for some positive integers
k_{1},\dots,k_{n}
and
a(i)\in[t]
for all
i\in[n]
. Of course all tensor PCA models
[
24
]
fall into this framework.

8.1
The two GAM classes

8.1.1
P_{1}
has nonnegative and supermultiplicative moments

Consider first the following assumption on the prior
P_{1}
.

Assumption 8.1
.

Suppose that for some
c>0
the prior
P_{1}
satisfies the following assumptions.

•

(“Nonnegative moments”) For all
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
v\sim P_{1}
it holds that
\mathbb{E}_{P_{1}}{[v^{k}]}\geq 0
.

•

(“Supermultiplicative moments”) For
\rho=n^{-c}
, it holds
\mathbb{E}_{P_{1}}[v^{k}]\mathbb{E}_{P_{1}}[v^{t}]\leq\rho\cdot\mathbb{E}_{P_{1}}[v^{k+t}]
for any
k,t\in\mathbb{N}
.

For all GAMs where
P_{1}
satisfies Assumption
8.1
, we can prove that they have low-order nonnegative cumulants.

The result is as follows, and the proof of it is deferred to Section
8.2
.

Lemma 8.2
.

Suppose we observe a GAM, of the form (
8.1
),
with a prior distribution
P_{1}
satisfying Assumption
8.1

for some
c>0
. Then, for any
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
such that
1\leq|\alpha|\leq\log_{2}(1/\rho)-1
, where
\rho=n^{-c}
, we have

\kappa_{\alpha}(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})\geq 0,

Now, interestingly, Assumption
8.1
for the prior
P_{1}
is satisfied by many standard priors.

Lemma 8.3
.

Let
\rho=n^{-c}
for some
c>0
. Then consider
B_{n}\sim\text{Ber}(\rho)
and
T
any real-valued random variable that is independent of
B_{n}
and either has a symmetric law around 0 or is non-negative almost surely. Then if
P_{1}
is the law of
T\times B_{n}
,
P_{1}
satisfies Assumption
8.1
for
c>0.

In particular,
B_{n}\sim\text{Ber}(\rho)

and
R_{n}\sim\text{Rad}(\rho)
satisfy the Assumption
8.1
.

Proof.

Let
v\sim P_{1}
. In the setting of the lemma we may write

v=T_{n}:=B_{n}T,

where
B_{n}\sim\mathrm{Ber}(\rho)
is independent of
T
.

First, it’s easy to see that
\mathbb{E}[T^{m}]\geq 0
for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
under either condition on
T
. Also
B_{n}\geq 0
almost surely so
\mathbb{E}[v^{m}]\geq 0
for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
and the first condition of the assumption is satisfied.

Then, we only need to show the second condition of the Assumption. Fix any
k,t\in\mathbb{N}
and any
m\in\mathbb{N}
. Since
B_{n}\in\{0,1\}
we have

B_{n}^{m}=B_{n}
, and therefore by independence,

\mathbb{E}_{P_{1}}\left[v^{m}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[(B_{n}T)^{m}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[B_{n}^{m}\right]\mathbb{E}\left[T^{m}\right]=\mathbb{E}[B_{n}]\mathbb{E}[T^{m}]=\rho\,\mathbb{E}[T^{m}].

(8.2)

Using this we have

\mathbb{E}_{P_{1}}[v^{k}]\mathbb{E}_{P_{1}}[v^{t}]=\rho^{2}\ \mathbb{E}[T^{k}]\mathbb{E}[T^{t}],\qquad\mathbb{E}_{P_{1}}[v^{k+t}]=\rho\ \mathbb{E}[T^{k+t}].

Thus the desired inequality

\mathbb{E}_{P_{1}}[v^{k}]\mathbb{E}_{P_{1}}[v^{t}]\leq\rho\,\mathbb{E}_{P_{1}}[v^{k+t}]

is equivalent to

\mathbb{E}[T^{k}]\mathbb{E}[T^{t}]\leq\mathbb{E}[T^{k+t}].

(8.3)

If
T\geq 0
almost surely, then by Hölder’s inequality with exponents
\frac{k+t}{k}
and
\frac{k+t}{t}
,

\mathbb{E}[T^{k}]=\mathbb{E}\left[(T^{k+t})^{\frac{k}{k+t}}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}[T^{k+t}]^{\frac{k}{k+t}},\qquad\mathbb{E}[T^{t}]=\mathbb{E}\left[(T^{k+t})^{\frac{t}{k+t}}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}[T^{k+t}]^{\frac{t}{k+t}}.

Multiplying yields (
8.3
).

If
T
has a law symmetric around
0
, then whenever at least one of
k,t
is odd we have

\mathbb{E}[T^{k}]\mathbb{E}[T^{t}]=0
. Also,
\mathbb{E}[T^{k+t}]=0
if
k+t
is odd, while

\mathbb{E}[T^{k+t}]\geq 0
if
k+t
is even, since then
T^{k+t}\geq 0
almost surely. Hence
(
8.3
) holds. If
k
and
t
are both even, set
Y:=T^{2}\geq 0
. Then

\mathbb{E}[T^{k}]\mathbb{E}[T^{t}]=\mathbb{E}[Y^{k/2}]\,\mathbb{E}[Y^{t/2}]\leq\mathbb{E}[Y^{(k+t)/2}]=\mathbb{E}[T^{k+t}].

Thus (
8.3
) holds in all cases, proving the condition for
P_{1}
.

In particular,
taking
T=1
a.s. gives
v=B_{n}

and
taking
T
to be a
\mathrm{Rad}(1/2)
random variable independent of
B_{n}
gives
v=B_{n}T
,
which has law
\mathrm{Rad}(\rho)
, so
B_{n}
and
R_{n}\sim\mathrm{Rad}(\rho)
satisfy Assumption
8.1
.
∎

8.1.2
P_{1}
has nonnegative cumulants

We now turn to the second family of GAMs.

First, for a distribution
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R})
and any
r\in\mathbb{N}
we denote the
r
-cumulant of
\mu
by

\kappa_{r}(X):=\kappa(\underbrace{X,X,\dots,X}_{r-\text{times}}),

where
X\sim\mu
. Now given that the second assumption asks for all
r
-cumulants of
P_{1}
to be nonnegative.

Assumption 8.4

(Nonnegative cumulants)
.

Suppose that for a random variable
X\sim P_{1}
for any
r\in\mathbb{N}
,

\kappa_{r}(X)\geq 0.

Note that Assumption
8.4
is satisfied by multiple standard distributions, such as the Gaussian
\mathcal{N}(\mu,\sigma^{2})
with
\mu\geq 0
, the Poisson
\text{Pois}(\lambda)
for any
\lambda>0
, and the Exponential
\text{Exp}(\lambda)
for any
\lambda>0
, see e.g.
[
23
, Chap 2]
for details.

Now, suppose we have a GAM of the form (
8.1
), with
Z_{j}=1
for all
j\in[t]
, i.e.

\displaystyle X_{i}=f_{i}(v_{1},\dots,v_{n})

(8.4)

where
f_{i}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
are monomials with respect to the i.i.d. random variables
v_{1},\dots,v_{n}
. If the distribution
P_{1}
, that
v_{i},i\in[n]
follow, satisfies Assumption
8.4
then the following holds. Notice that the lemma directly implies the low-order cumulant nonnegative property, alongside a technical useful second condition.

Lemma 8.5
.

For any GAM of the form (
8.4
) with distribution
P_{1}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R})
satisfies Assumption
8.4
the following holds. For all
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{N}
,

\kappa_{\alpha}(X_{1},\dots,X_{N})\geq 0,

and
\widetilde{\kappa}_{\alpha}(X_{1},\dots,X_{N})\geq\kappa_{\alpha}^{2}(X_{1},\dots,X_{N})
.

The proof of this Lemma is deferred to Section
8.3

8.2
Proof of Lemma
8.2

For the remaining of this section we remind the reader that for convenience we have defined,

\kappa_{\alpha}(X_{1},\textbf{X})=\kappa_{\alpha}(X_{1},\underbrace{X_{1},\dots,X_{1}}_{\alpha_{1}-\text{times}},\dots,\underbrace{X_{N},\dots,X_{N}}_{\alpha_{N}-\text{times}}).

Before we move on with the proof a definition is in order.

Definition 8.6

(The dependency multigraph)
.

For the class of GAMs of interest notice that for any
\kappa_{\alpha}(X_{1},\dots,X_{N})
the multi-index
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
naturally corresponds to a “dependency” multigraph with vertices
\{1,\dots,n\}
where for any
i\in[N]
if
\alpha_{i}\neq 0
then the multigraph has a clique between the vertices
j\in[n]
s.t.
v_{j}\in\text{supp}(f_{i})
.

We now state a useful corollary for the proof which is immediate from Proposition
6.2
.

Corollary 8.7
.

For any
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
, if it’s dependency multigraph is not connected then
\kappa_{\alpha}(X_{1},\dots,X_{N})=0
.

Therefore, we may restrict our attention for multi-indices
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
for which their dependency multigraph is connected.

Now we proceed with the proof.

Proof of Lemma
8.2
.

For convenience, we assume
\lambda=1
as all conditions are homogeneous with respect to
\lambda
.

Also, note that we will prove for convenience that
\kappa_{\alpha}(X_{i},\textbf{X})\geq 0
for some fixed
i\in[n]
and for all multindices
\alpha
with cardinality
1\leq|\alpha|\leq\log_{2}(1/\rho)-1.
Combining this with the fact that
\kappa(X_{i})\geq 0
for all
i\in[n]
, since
X_{i}
are for all
i\in[n]
the product of powers of the independent random variables
v_{i},i\in[n]
that have nonnegative moments, we will have
\kappa_{\alpha}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})\geq 0
for all
\alpha
such that
1\leq|\alpha|\leq\log_{2}(1/\rho)-1
as desired.

Without loss of generality for the remaining of the proof we fix
i=1
and we show that
\kappa_{\alpha}(X_{1},\textbf{X})\geq 0
to prove the result.

Consider any GAM, of the form (
8.1
),
with a prior distribution
P_{1}
satisfying Assumption
8.1

for some
c>0
. We will start by proving via induction on
|\alpha|
the following:

\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[X_{1}X^{\alpha}]\leq\kappa_{\alpha}\leq\mathbb{E}[X_{1}X^{\alpha}].

(8.5)

Notice that for
\alpha=\textbf{0}
,
\kappa_{0}=\mathbb{E}[X_{1}]\geq 0
by Assumption
8.1
.

Now assume that the induction hypothesis holds for any
\beta
with
|\beta|<|\alpha|
. Using Proposition
6.5
we know that for all multindices
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{N}
the cumulant
\kappa_{\alpha}
satisfies the following recursive formula,

\displaystyle\kappa_{\alpha}=\mathbb{E}[X_{1}X^{\alpha}]-\sum_{0\leq\beta\prec\alpha}\kappa_{\beta}\binom{\alpha}{\beta}\mathbb{E}[X^{\alpha-\beta}].

(8.6)

The induction hypothesis, though, implies that for all
P_{1}
satisfying Assumption
8.1
it holds that
\kappa_{\beta}\geq 0
for all
\beta\prec\alpha
, since
X_{1}X^{\alpha}
can be written as a product of powers of the independent
v_{i}
’s and the moments of
v_{i}
are nonnegative. This implies that the second term is non-positive and thus we obtain
\kappa_{\alpha}\leq\mathbb{E}[X_{1}X^{\alpha}]
. To finish the induction step, we need to show that
\kappa_{\alpha}\geq\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[X_{1}X^{\alpha}]
. For the lower bound we upper bound the second term of the recursive formula for
\kappa_{\alpha}
by using Assumption
8.1
in the following way: first

\displaystyle\sum_{0\leq\beta<\alpha}\kappa_{\beta}\binom{\alpha}{\beta}\mathbb{E}[X^{\alpha-\beta}]\leq\sum_{0\leq\beta\prec\alpha}\binom{\alpha}{\beta}\mathbb{E}[X_{1}X^{\beta}]\mathbb{E}[X^{\alpha-\beta}]

where we used the induction hypothesis once again. We want to show that for every multi-index
0\leq\beta\prec\alpha
it holds that

\mathbb{E}[X_{1}X^{\beta}]\mathbb{E}[X^{\alpha-\beta}]\leq\mathbb{E}[X_{1}X^{\alpha}]\rho.

(8.7)

Note though that
X_{i}
are of the form
8.1
and
Z_{j},j\in[t]
are independent from
v_{i},i\in[n]
. Therefore, to prove our desired inequality it suffices to show that for all suh
\beta
it holds that

\mathbb{E}[Z_{i}^{k}]\mathbb{E}[Z_{i}^{t}]\leq\mathbb{E}[Z_{i}^{k+t}]\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{E}[v_{j}^{\beta_{j}}]\mathbb{E}[v_{j}^{\alpha_{j}-\beta_{j}}]\leq\rho\mathbb{E}[v_{j}^{a_{j}}]

for all
i\in[t]
and at least one
j\in[n]
(we have a product of i.i.d. random variables so we can treat each coordinate separately). For the first inequality notice that this is an implication of the fact that
Z_{j},j\in[t]
are symmetric or non-negative a.s. – we proved this also in Lemma
8.3
. For the second inequality we do the following. Since
\alpha
is a connected multi graph then for any
0<\beta<\alpha
s.t.
\kappa_{\beta}>0
it should hold that
\beta
is also connected. Furthermore,

|V(\beta)|+|V(\alpha-\beta)|\geq|V(\alpha)|+1.

(8.8)

Indeed, if that wasn’t the case then the vertices of
V(\alpha-\beta)
and
V(\alpha)
would form two components of
\alpha
that are not connected which leads to a contradiction. Equation (
8.8
) implies that there will always exist a coordinate
j\in[n]
s.t.
j\in(\{1\}\cup\text{supp}(\beta))\cap\text{supp}(\alpha)
. For this coordinate the expression

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[X_{1}X^{\beta}]\mathbb{E}[X^{\alpha-\beta}]

contains the term
\mathbb{E}[v_{j}^{\beta_{j}}]\mathbb{E}[v_{j}^{\alpha_{j}-\beta_{j}}]
with
\beta_{j}>0
. Using Assumption
8.1
we can upper bound
\mathbb{E}[v_{j}^{\beta_{j}}]\mathbb{E}[v_{j}^{\alpha_{j}-\beta_{j}}]\leq\mathbb{E}[v^{a_{j}}]\rho
and the proof of (
8.7
) is complete. Using this equation, we get the following:

\displaystyle\sum_{0\leq\beta\prec\alpha}\binom{\alpha}{\beta}\mathbb{E}[X_{1}X^{\beta}]\mathbb{E}[X^{\alpha-\beta}]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{0\leq\beta\prec\alpha}\binom{\alpha}{\beta}\mathbb{E}[X_{1}X^{\alpha}]\rho

\displaystyle\leq 2^{|\alpha|}\mathbb{E}[X_{1}X^{\alpha}]\rho

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[X_{1}X^{\alpha}].

where we used the fact that
|\alpha|\leq\log_{2}(1/\rho)-1
to obtain the last inequality. The upper bound implies
\kappa_{\alpha}\geq\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[X_{1}X^{\alpha}]
and our proof for the induction is complete.

Using now
8.5
, we know that for any prior
P_{1}
satisfying Assumption
8.1
for all
\alpha
such that
|\alpha|\leq C\log n
and for some universal constant
C:=C(\rho)
the cumulants
\kappa(X_{1},\textbf{X})
of the prior satisfy
\kappa(X_{1},\textbf{X})\geq 0
and the proof is complete.

∎

8.3
Proof of Lemma
8.5

Proof of Lemma
8.5
.

We will make use again of Theorem
6.17
.

Given any GAM of the form (
8.1
) we want to decompose any cumulant of the form

\kappa_{\alpha}(f_{1},\dots,f_{N})
, where
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{N}
.
Since
f_{i}
are for all
i\in[N],
are equal to
f_{i}(v_{1},\dots,v_{n})=v_{1}^{k^{i}_{1}}\dots v_{n}^{k^{i}_{n}}
for some positive integers
k^{i}_{1},\dots,k^{i}_{n}
we apply Theorem
6.17
with respect to the

\sum_{j=1}^{N}\alpha_{j}\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{n}k_{i}^{j}\Big)

random variables that appear in this cumulant (note that we have many of the
v_{i}
’s repeated multiple times in this family of random variables, but we yet consider them as different random variables for this argument). To explain this further consider the following: for
i=1
,
f_{1}(v_{1},\dots,v_{n})
is the product of
S_{1}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}k_{i}^{1}
random variables. Relabeling these variables to
R_{1}^{1},\dots,R_{S_{1}}^{1}
and repeating the same process for all
f_{i},i\in[n]
we can rewrite the cumulant of interest as:

\kappa_{\alpha}(f_{1},\dots,f_{n})=\kappa_{\alpha}(R_{1}^{1}\cdot\dots\cdot R_{S_{1}}^{1},\dots,R_{1}^{N}\cdot\dots\cdot R_{S_{n}}^{N})

Then, Theorem
6.17
gives a decomposition of the form

\kappa_{\alpha}(f_{1},\dots,f_{N})=\sum_{\pi^{\prime}\in G}\prod_{S^{\prime}\in\pi^{\prime}}\kappa(S^{\prime})

where importantly,
all the cumulants that appear on the right hand side take the form

\kappa(v_{i_{1}},\dots v_{i_{t}}),\qquad\text{for some}\quad i_{1},\dots,i_{t}\in[n],t\geq 1,

where
i_{1},\dots,i_{t}
are not necessarily distinct. We argue that the only terms that are non-zero are of the form
\kappa(v_{i},\dots,v_{i})
for some
i\in[n]
. Indeed, if we had a term corresponding to a part
S^{\prime}
that contained at least two
v_{i},v_{j}\in S^{\prime}
for two distinct integers
i,j\in[n]
then
\kappa(S^{\prime})=0
, since
v_{i},v_{j}
are independent. Using the notation we introduced above, this implies that the sum will only involve
r-
th cumulants of the prior distribution on
v_{i}
for
r\in\mathbb{N}
. Assumption
8.4
guarantees that these terms are all nonnegative therefore for all
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{N}

\kappa_{\alpha}(f_{1},\dots,f_{N})\geq 0.

Lastly, using Lemma
6.18
we also conclude that
\widetilde{\kappa}_{\alpha}(X_{1},\dots X_{N})\geq\kappa_{\alpha}^{2}(X_{1},\dots X_{N})
and our proof is complete.
∎

9
Background on modified Bessel functions

Throughout this appendix, let
d\geq 1
be an integer and let

G,H\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
be independent
N(0,I_{d})
vectors.
Define

W:=\langle G,H\rangle=\sum_{i=1}^{d}G_{i}H_{i},

and let
f_{d}
denote the density of
W
.
For background on modified Bessel functions, see

[
35
,
1
]
.

For
\nu\in\mathbb{R}
, the modified Bessel function of the first kind is defined by

I_{\nu}(x):=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{m!\,\Gamma(m+\nu+1)}\left(\frac{x}{2}\right)^{2m+\nu},\qquad x>0.

Especially, for
\nu=0
, we have the integral representation

I_{0}(x)=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\pi}\exp(x\cos\theta)d\theta.

The equivalence of the integral representation to the series definition for
\nu=0
follows directly from the Taylor expansion of the exponential function. Expanding
\exp(x\cos\theta)
and exchanging the sum and integral by uniform convergence yields
\frac{1}{\pi}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{x^{k}}{k!}\int_{0}^{\pi}\cos^{k}\theta d\theta
. The integral of
\cos^{k}\theta
over
[0,\pi]
vanishes by symmetry for odd
k
. For even powers
k=2m
, standard trigonometric integration yields
\int_{0}^{\pi}\cos^{2m}\theta d\theta=\pi\frac{(2m)!}{2^{2m}(m!)^{2}}
. Substituting this evaluation into the summation, the
\pi
and
(2m)!
terms cancel, leaving exactly
\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{(m!)^{2}}\left(\frac{x}{2}\right)^{2m}
. Since
\Gamma(m+1)=m!
, this perfectly recovers the series definition of
I_{0}(x)
.

9.1
Modified Bessel functions of the second kind

The modified Bessel function of the second kind is then defined, for

\nu\notin\mathbb{Z}
, by

K_{\nu}(x):=\frac{\pi}{2\sin(\pi\nu)}\bigl(I_{-\nu}(x)-I_{\nu}(x)\bigr),\qquad x>0,

with the integer-order case obtained by continuity, see

[
35
, (1.1)]
.

It also admits the integral representation

K_{\nu}(x)=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-x\cosh u}\cosh(\nu u)\,du,\qquad x>0,

see
[
35
, (1.2)]
and
[
1
, Chap. 9]
.
In particular,
K_{\nu}(x)>0
for all
x>0
, and
K_{-\nu}(x)=K_{\nu}(x)
.

We shall use the following standard identities.

Lemma 9.1

(Recurrences and derivative formulas for
K_{\nu}
)
.

For every
x>0
and every real
\nu
,

\displaystyle K_{\nu+1}(x)

\displaystyle=K_{\nu-1}(x)+\frac{2\nu}{x}K_{\nu}(x),

(9.1)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dx}\bigl(x^{\nu}K_{\nu}(x)\bigr)

\displaystyle=-x^{\nu}K_{\nu-1}(x),

(9.2)

\displaystyle K_{\nu}^{\prime}(x)

\displaystyle=-K_{\nu-1}(x)-\frac{\nu}{x}K_{\nu}(x)=-K_{\nu+1}(x)+\frac{\nu}{x}K_{\nu}(x),

(9.3)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dx}\log K_{\nu}(x)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\nu}{x}-\frac{K_{\nu-1}(x)}{K_{\nu}(x)}=\frac{\nu}{x}-\frac{K_{\nu+1}(x)}{K_{\nu}(x)}.

(9.4)

Equivalently,

\frac{d}{dx}\log\bigl(x^{\nu}K_{\nu}(x)\bigr)=-\frac{K_{\nu-1}(x)}{K_{\nu}(x)}.

(9.5)

Proof.

The recurrence (
9.1
) and derivative identity (
9.2
)
are standard, see
[
1
, (9.6.26)]
.
Expanding (
9.2
) gives the first formula in (
9.3
),
while the second follows by combining the first with (
9.1
).
Dividing by
K_{\nu}(x)
yields (
9.4
), and
(
9.5
) is just (
9.2
)
after taking logarithmic derivatives.
∎

The small and large argument asymptotics we use are the following.

Lemma 9.2

(Basic asymptotics)
.

Fix
\nu>0
. As
x\downarrow 0
,

K_{\nu}(x)=\frac{(1+o(1))}{2}\Gamma(\nu)\Bigl(\frac{x}{2}\Bigr)^{-\nu},

(9.6)

while

K_{0}(x)=-(1+o(1))\log x.
As
x\to\infty
,

K_{\nu}(x)=(1+o(1))\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2x}}e^{-x}\left(1+\frac{4\nu^{2}-1}{8x}+O_{\nu}(x^{-2})\right).

(9.7)

Consequently, for any
\nu_{1},\nu_{2}=O(1)
,

\frac{K_{\nu_{1}}(x)}{K_{\nu_{2}}(x)}\to 1\qquad\text{as }x\to\infty.

Proof.

These are standard, see
[
35
, (1.3)–(1.5)]

and
[
1
, Chap. 9]
. The ratio limit follows immediately
from the common leading term
\sqrt{\pi/(2x)}e^{-x}
.
∎

For later use we also record two ratio estimates.

Lemma 9.3

(Ratio inequalities)
.

For every
\nu\geq 0
and
x>0
,

\frac{\nu+\sqrt{x^{2}+\nu^{2}}}{x}<\frac{K_{\nu+1}(x)}{K_{\nu}(x)}<\frac{\nu+\frac{1}{2}+\sqrt{x^{2}+(\nu+\frac{1}{2})^{2}}}{x}.

(9.8)

Proof.

The inequality (
9.8
) is exactly
[
35
, (1.10)]
.
∎

Corollary 9.4

(Large-order consequence of the ratio bound)
.

Let
\nu\to\infty
and let
x=x_{\nu}>0
satisfy
x=o(\nu)
. Then

\frac{K_{\nu-1}(x)}{K_{\nu}(x)}=\frac{x}{2\nu}\bigl(1+o(1)\bigr).

(9.9)

Proof.

Apply (
9.8
) with
\nu
replaced by
\nu-1
:

\frac{\nu-1+\sqrt{x^{2}+(\nu-1)^{2}}}{x}<\frac{K_{\nu}(x)}{K_{\nu-1}(x)}<\frac{\nu-\frac{1}{2}+\sqrt{x^{2}+(\nu-\frac{1}{2})^{2}}}{x}.

Since
x=o(\nu)
,

\nu-1+\sqrt{x^{2}+(\nu-1)^{2}}=2\nu+o(\nu),\qquad\nu-\frac{1}{2}+\sqrt{x^{2}+(\nu-\frac{1}{2})^{2}}=2\nu+o(\nu).

Hence

\frac{K_{\nu}(x)}{K_{\nu-1}(x)}=\frac{2\nu}{x}\bigl(1+o(1)\bigr),

and taking reciprocals gives (
9.9
).
∎

9.2
Density of a Gaussian inner product

The distribution of
W=\langle G,H\rangle
can be written explicitly in terms of
K_{\nu}
.

Lemma 9.5

(Explicit density of a Gaussian inner product)
.

Let
W=\langle G,H\rangle=\sum_{i=1}^{d}G_{i}H_{i}.
Then
W=\frac{X-Y}{2},
where
X,Y
are independent
\chi^{2}_{d}
random variables. Moreover,
W
has density

f_{d}(x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}\,\Gamma(d/2)\,2^{\,d/2-1/2}}\,|x|^{\,d/2-1/2}\,K_{\,d/2-1/2}(|x|),\qquad x\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}.

(9.10)

For
d\geq 2
, the formula extends continuously to
x=0
. For
d=1
,

f_{1}(x)=\frac{1}{\pi}K_{0}(|x|),

and
f_{1}(x)=-\frac{(1+o(1))}{\pi}\log|x|
as
x\to 0
.

The proof of this Lemma is deferred to Section
10.4
.

10
Proofs for applications

Before we prove Theorems
3.1
,
3.2
and
3.3
we introduce state an Auxiliary Lemma the proof of which is deferred to Section
10.4
.

Lemma 10.1

(Bounds for Rademacher sum)
.

Let
S_{n}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}
with
\varepsilon_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle iid}}{{\sim}}\mathrm{Rad}(\pm 1)
and

\phi(x)=(2\pi)^{-1/2}e^{-x^{2}/2}
.
For integers
s\equiv n\pmod{2}
, the following holds.

Let
T=T_{n}\geq 1
satisfy
T^{3}=o(\sqrt{n})
. Then, uniformly for integers
s\equiv n\pmod{2}
with

|s|\leq\sqrt{n}\,T
,

\mathbb{P}(S_{n}=s)=\frac{2}{\sqrt{n}}\,\phi\Big(\frac{s}{\sqrt{n}}\Big)\,\big(1+o(1)\big).

(10.1)

Moreover, there exist absolute constants
c,C>0
and
n_{\star}\geq 2
such that for all
n\geq n_{\star}

and all integers
s\equiv n\pmod{2}
with
0\leq s\leq n/2
, it holds

\mathbb{P}(S_{n}=s)\ \geq\ \frac{c}{\sqrt{n}}\exp\Big(-\frac{s^{2}}{2n}-C\frac{s^{3}}{n^{2}}\Big).

(10.2)

10.1
Proofs for Sparse Tensor PCA

We start with some notation. Suppose
v,v^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
k=n^{\beta+o(1)},\beta\in(0,1)
have i.i.d.
\text{Rad}(k/n)
entries, meaning each entry is
1
with probability
k/n
,
-1
with probability
k/n
and
0
otherwise. Define

p:=(k/n)^{2}.

Then, if
X_{1}=v_{1}v_{1}^{\prime},\dots,X_{n}=v_{n}v_{n}^{\prime}
these random variables are i.i.d. with

\mathbb{P}(X_{i}=1)=\mathbb{P}(X_{i}=-1)=p/2,\ \mathbb{P}(X_{i}=0)=1-p.

Set
S:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}
and
T:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}1\{|X_{i}|=1\}.
We denote

\mu:=\mathbb{E}[T]=np=\frac{k^{2}}{n}.

(10.3)

Note also that

\mathrm{Var}(X_{i})=\mathbb{E}[X_{i}^{2}]=p,\qquad\mathrm{Var}(S)=n\mathrm{Var}(X_{i})=np=\mu,\qquad\sigma=\sigma_{S}=\frac{k}{\sqrt{n}}.

Based on all the above, the inner product
\langle X,X^{\prime}\rangle=\langle v^{\otimes r},v^{\prime\otimes r}\rangle
of two i.i.d. draw from the prior takes the form
S^{r}
.

Before proving the main Theorem of this section we state some Lemmas that we are going to use. For each of these models there are two key ingredients that we need. Finding the order of the derivative that appears in (
2.6
) evaluated at order
q(D_{n})
and then finding the leading order of the quantile
q(D_{n})
. In Sparse Tensor PCA we have two sparsity regimes
k=\omega(\sqrt{n})
and
k=o(\sqrt{n})
which we treat separately.

In order to analyze the algorithmic threshold in the moderate sparsity regime where
\beta\in(1/2,1)
, we first need to carefully control the scale of the overlap quantile
q(D)
. The following lemma establishes tight (up to constant) upper and lower bounds for this quantile function as
D
varies.

Lemma 10.2
.

Fix any constant
c_{1}>0
, an integer
r\geq 2
and a sparsity level
k=n^{\beta+o(1)}
where
\beta\in(1/2,1)
. Then there exist absolute constants
0<c<C<\infty
and
\mu_{0}\geq 1

(depending only on
c_{1}
) such that whenever
\mu
, defined in (
10.3
), satisfies
\mu\geq\mu_{0}
, and for all
D
satisfying
c_{1}\log\mu\leq D\leq\mu

we have

\big(c\,\sigma\sqrt{D}\big)^{r}\leq q(D)\leq\big(C\,\sigma\sqrt{D}\big)^{r}.

The proof of Lemma
10.2
is deferred to Section
10.1.2
.

Building on the quantile bounds established above, we now characterize the decay rate of the discrete derivative of the log-probability of the overlap.

Lemma 10.3
.

Fix
k=n^{\beta+o(1)}
where
\beta\in(1/2,1)
and
D_{n}\leq C\log n
, where
C>0
is any positive constant. Then, for
a_{n}(s)=(s+2)^{r}-s^{r}
,
s\in 2\mathbb{Z}
evaluated at
s=q(D_{n})
the following holds:

-\Delta_{a_{n}}\log\mathbb{P}(\langle X,X^{\prime}\rangle=q(D_{n}))=\Theta\left(\frac{n^{\frac{r}{2}}}{k^{r}}D_{n}^{2-r}\right)=\Theta\left(\lambda_{\mathrm{ALG}}\cdot D_{n}^{2-r}\right),

The proof of Lemma
10.3
is deferred to Section
10.1.3
.

We next shift our focus to the highly sparse regime, characterized by
\beta\in(0,1/2)
. In this setting, the following lemma dictates the asymptotic order of the quantile function
q(D_{n})
.

Lemma 10.4
.

Fix
\beta\in(0,1/2)
and set
k=n^{\beta+o(1)}
, so
\mu:=k^{2}/n=n^{-(1-2a)}\to 0
. Assume
D_{n}=n^{o(1)}
and
D_{n}\geq c_{0}\log n
for some fixed
c_{0}>0
.
Then there exist constants
c,C>0
(depending only on
a
and
c_{0}
) such that for all sufficiently large
n
,

c\frac{D_{n}}{\log n}\leq q(D_{n})\leq C\frac{D_{n}}{\log n}.

The proof of Lemma
10.4
is deferred to Section
10.1.4
.

The final lemma in this section estimates the discrete derivative of the log-probability for small
k
, completing the necessary technical estimates for the highly sparse regime.

Lemma 10.5
.

Fix an integer
r\geq 2
,
\beta\in(0,1/2)
and let
k=n^{\beta+o(1)}
, and
a_{n}(s):=(s+2)^{r}-s^{r},\ s\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.

Then for any

c_{0}\log n\leq D_{n}=n^{o(1)}
for some fixed
c_{0}>0
,
and
s_{n}:=q_{n}(D_{n}),
it holds

-\Delta_{a_{n}}\log\mathbb{P}(\langle X,X^{\prime}\rangle=s_{n}^{r})=\Theta\left(\frac{(\log n)^{r}}{D_{n}^{\,r-1}}\right).

The proof of Lemma
10.5
is deferred to Section
10.1.5
.

10.1.1
Proof of Theorem
3.2

We prove Theorem
3.2
by directly applying Theorem
2.7
. To do this, we first prove that the sparse Rademacher prior satisfies Assumption
2.10
. Specifically, we verify the four conditions.

We start with the case
k=n^{\beta+o(1)},\beta\in(1/2,1)
.

Condition 1:
Since this model is of the form (
8.1
) (for
Z_{j}\equiv 1
for all
j\in[t]
) and the prior
P_{0}
we have assigned satisfies Assumption
8.1
using Lemma
8.2
we know that for all multi-indices
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
with
|\alpha|\leq D_{n}
for
D_{n}=\lfloor\log_{2}(n/k)-1\rfloor
it holds that

\kappa_{\alpha}(X_{i})\geq 0
.

Condition 2:
This is trivially true since
X_{i}
are bounded by
1
.

Condition 3:
This condition is straightforward. For
D_{n}^{\prime}=D_{n}\log^{2}n
and using Lemma
10.2
for
k=n^{\beta+o(1)}
,
\beta\in(1/2,1)
,

q(D_{n}^{\prime})\geq(D_{n}^{\prime})^{r/2}(k/\sqrt{n})^{r}=\omega(\max\{\log^{-D_{n}}n,n^{-C}\}),

for any constant
C>0
.

Condition 4:
Using Lemma
10.3
we know that the derivative at
q(D_{n})
is of order

n^{\frac{r}{2}}D_{n}^{2-r}/k^{r}
for
k=\omega(\sqrt{n})
.
Therefore, Assumption 4 is equivalent to the following condition:

\frac{D_{n}^{2-r}n^{\frac{r}{2}}/k^{r}}{A_{n}}\leq\frac{1}{q(\log^{3}n)\log n}.

Using Lemma
10.2
we know that for

D_{n}^{\prime}=\log^{3}n,

(10.4)

which satisfies
\omega(\log\mu)=D_{n}^{\prime}=o(\mu),
it holds that

q(D_{n}^{\prime})\leq C_{1}\frac{k^{r}\log^{3r/2}n}{n^{\frac{r}{2}}}.

Plugging this inequality to the one above we get that Assumption 4 holds for
A_{n}=C_{1}D_{n}^{2-r}\log^{(3r+2)/2}n
, where
C_{1}>0
is a positive constant.

From all the above, combined with (
2.6
), we have shown that for the sequence
A_{n}=C_{1}D_{n}^{2-r}\log^{(3r+2)/2}n
it holds that

\mathrm{Corr}^{\leq D_{n}}_{P_{0}}\left(\frac{1}{A_{n}}\cdot\big(\lambda+\Delta_{a_{n}}\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ann},\lambda}(q(D_{n}))\big)\right)^{2}\leq q(D_{n}^{\prime})

where
D_{n}^{\prime}
was defined in (
10.4
) and
a_{n}
represents the sequence defined in Lemmas
10.3
. Now notice that

\displaystyle\mathrm{MMSE}^{\mathrm{trivial}}_{X}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\|X\|^{2}-(\mathbb{E}\|X\|)^{2}=k^{r}.

Next, combining Lemma
10.3
with (
2.6
), implies that for all
k=n^{\beta},\beta\in(1/2,1)
,
\lambda+\Delta_{a_{n}}\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ann},\lambda}(q(D_{n}))=\Theta\left(\frac{n^{\frac{r}{2}}}{k^{r}}D_{n}^{2-r}\right)
. Using this and choosing
\lambda=\frac{n^{\frac{r}{2}}}{k^{r}}D_{n}^{2-r}/\log n
we get that for this
\lambda
,
\Delta_{a_{n}}\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ann},\lambda}(q(D_{n}))\geq 0
. Therefore, using Theorem
2.7
,
\mathrm{Corr}(\lambda/A_{n})^{2}\leq 2q(D_{n}^{\prime}),
where
A_{n}=C_{1}\log^{3}n
. Equivalently, using Lemmas
10.2
and
6.8
and substituting
D_{n}=\Theta(\log n)

\mathrm{MMSE}^{\leq D_{n}}_{X}\left(\frac{\lambda}{\log^{3}n}\right)\geq k^{r}-C_{1}\frac{k^{r}\log^{r}n}{n^{\frac{r}{2}}}.

Lastly, since in this regime,
\lambda_{\mathrm{ALG}}=\widetilde{\Theta}(n^{r/2}/k^{r})
:

\mathrm{MMSE}^{\leq D_{n}}_{X}\left(\frac{\lambda_{\mathrm{ALG}}}{\log^{(3r+4)/2}n}\right)\geq k^{r}-C_{1}\frac{k^{r}\log^{r}n}{n^{\frac{r}{2}}}.

Since
k=o(n)
we have proven the desired inequality for the dense regime
k=n^{\beta+o(1)},\beta\in(1/2,1)
and all
r\geq 2
.

We know prove the result for
k=n^{\beta+o(1)},\beta\in(0,1/2)
.

Condition 1:
Again, since this model is of the form (
8.1
) (for
Z_{j}\equiv 1
for all
j\in[t]
) and the prior
P_{0}
we have assigned satisfies
8.1
using Lemma
8.2
we know that for all multi-indices
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
with
|\alpha|\leq D_{n}
for
D_{n}=\lfloor\log_{2}(n/k)-1\rfloor
it holds that

\kappa_{\alpha}(X_{i})\geq 0
.

Condition 2:
This is true since
X_{i}
are bounded by
1
.

Condition 3:

Using Lemma
10.5
for
k=n^{\beta+o(1)}
,
\beta\in(0,1/2)
,

q(D_{n}^{\prime})\geq\Theta(1)=\omega(\max\{\log^{-D_{n}}n,n^{-C}\}),

for any constant
C>0
.

Condition 4
Using Lemma
10.5
we know that the derivative at
q(D_{n})
is of order
\Theta(1)
. Therefore, Assumption
4
reduces to:

\Theta\left(\frac{1}{A_{n}}\right)\leq\frac{1}{q(\log^{3}n)\log n},

which holds for
A_{n}=C_{1}\log^{3}n
, for some positive constant
C_{1}>0
.

From all the above, combined with (
2.6
), we have shown that for the sequence
A_{n}=C_{1}D_{n}^{2-r}\log^{(3r+2)/2}n
it holds that

\mathrm{Corr}^{\leq D_{n}}_{P_{0}}\left(\frac{1}{A_{n}}\cdot\big(\lambda+\Delta_{a_{n}}\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ann},\lambda}(q(D_{n}))\big)\right)^{2}\leq q(D_{n}^{\prime})

where
a_{n}
represents the sequences defined in Lemmas
10.5
.

Next, using Lemma
10.5
and (
2.6
), we have that for all
k=n^{\beta},\beta\in(0,1/2)
,
\lambda+\Delta_{a_{n}}\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ann},\lambda}(q(D_{n}))=\Theta(\log n)
. Using this and choosing
\lambda=1
we get that for this
\lambda
,
\Delta_{a_{n}}\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ann},\lambda}(q(D_{n}))\geq 0
. Lastly, since in this regime,
\lambda_{\mathrm{ALG}}=\Theta(1)
we get, applying Theorem
2.7
:

\mathrm{MMSE}^{\leq D_{n}}_{X}\left(\frac{\lambda_{\mathrm{ALG}}}{\log^{3}n}\right)\geq k^{r}-C_{1}\log^{2}n.

Since
k=\omega(\log^{2}n)
we have proven the desired inequality for the sparse regime
k=n^{\beta+o(1)},\beta\in(0,1/2)
and all
r\geq 2
.

10.1.2
Proof of Lemma
10.2

Upper Bound:

Since
|X_{i}|\leq 1
,
\mathbb{E}X_{i}=0
and
\mathrm{Var}(S)=\mu
, Bernstein inequality yields for all
t\geq 0
,

\mathbb{P}(|S|\geq t)\leq 2\exp\Big(-\frac{t^{2}}{2(\mu+t/3)}\Big).

Taking
t=C(\sqrt{\mu D}+D)
gives
\mathbb{P}(|S|\geq t)\leq e^{-D}
for large enough absolute
C
.
Since
D\leq\mu
, then
\sqrt{\mu D}+D\leq 2\sqrt{\mu D}
, so

q(D)\leq(2C\sqrt{\mu D})^{r}=(2C\,\sigma\sqrt{D})^{r}.

Lower Bound:

Let
A_{t}=\sum_{j=1}^{t}\varepsilon_{j}
with
\varepsilon_{j}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle iid}}{{\sim}}\text{Rad}(\pm 1)
.
Fix
c_{1}>0
as in the statement. Using Lemma
10.1
we know that there exist constants
c^{\prime}>0
and
t_{0}\geq 2

(depending only on
c_{1}
) such that for all
t\geq t_{0}
and all
D
with
c_{1}\log t\leq D\leq t,
we have

\mathbb{P}\big(|A_{t}|\geq c^{\prime}\sqrt{tD}\big)\ \geq\ e^{-D}.

(10.5)

Write
X_{i}=\varepsilon_{i}Z_{i}
where
\varepsilon_{i}\sim\text{Rad}(\pm 1)
and

Z_{i}:=\mathbf{1}_{\{|X_{i}|=1\}}\sim\text{Ber}(\rho)
are independent. Then

T=\sum_{i}Z_{i}\sim\text{Bin}(n,\rho)
with
\mathbb{E}T=\mu
, and conditionally on
T=t
,
we have
S\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}A_{t}
. By Chernoff inequality,

\mathbb{P}(T\leq\mu/2)\leq e^{-\mu/8}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{P}(T\geq 2\mu)\leq e^{-\mu/3},

hence for
\mu
large

\mathbb{P}(\mu/2\leq T\leq 2\mu)\geq 3/4
.
On this event, which we denote by
E
,
D\leq\mu\leq 2T
, so
D/2\leq T
and
e^{-D/2}\geq e^{-D}
. Also,
T\leq 2\mu
implies

\log T\leq\log(2\mu)\leq 2\log\mu
for
\mu
large enough, so
D/2\geq(c_{1}/4)\log T
.
Thus Lemma
10.1
(with constant
c_{1}/4
) applies for
t=T
and
D/2
.
Thus we may apply (
10.5
) with
t=T
to get

\mathbb{P}\Big(|S|\geq c^{\prime}\sqrt{TD}\ \Big|\ T,E\Big)\geq\mathbb{P}\Big(|A_{t}|\geq c^{\prime}\sqrt{TD}\Big)\geq\ e^{-D},

where we used again that
S
conditionally on
T=t
has the same distribution as
A_{t}
.
Using
T\geq\mu/2
gives
\sqrt{TD}\geq\sqrt{\mu D/2}
, hence

\mathbb{P}\big(|S|\geq c\sqrt{\mu D}\big)\ \geq\ \mathbb{P}\Big(2\mu\geq T\geq\frac{\mu}{2}\Big)e^{-D}\ \geq\ \frac{3}{4}e^{-D}

after adjusting
c
(and absorbing constants into
\mu_{0}
).
Therefore the
\frac{3}{4}e^{-D}
upper quantile of
|S|
is at least
c\sqrt{\mu D}
. Raising to the
r
-th power yields
q(D+3/4)\geq(c\sqrt{\mu D})^{r}=(c\,\sigma\sqrt{D})^{r}
. Equivalently, adjusting some constants,
q(D)\geq(c\sqrt{\mu(D-3/4)})^{r}=(c\,\sigma\sqrt{D-3/4})^{r}\geq(C\,\sigma\sqrt{D})^{r},
for
C=c/2
since
D=\omega(1)
. Combining with Step 1 the proof is complete.

10.1.3
Proof of Lemma
10.3

We have denoted
S=\langle v,v^{\prime}\rangle
and therefore the quantity
\langle X,X^{\prime}\rangle
from two i.i.d. draws
X,X^{\prime}\sim P
is
S^{r}
. Using that, the quantity

-\Delta_{a_{n}}\log\mathbb{P}(\langle X,X^{\prime}\rangle=q(D_{n}))

is equal to, substituting
S^{r}=\langle X,X^{\prime}\rangle
and
a_{n}(s)=(s+2)^{r}-s^{r}
for integers
s=q(D_{n}),

D_{n}\leq C\log n
where
C>0
,

\Lambda_{r}(s):=\frac{\log\mathbb{P}(S=s+2)-\log\mathbb{P}(S=s)}{(s+2)^{r}-s^{r}},

We proceed with some notation and some Auxiliary lemmas.

As we said, we will evaluate
\Lambda_{r}(s)
at
s=q(D_{n})
or using Lemma
10.2
this is equivalent to evaluating at
s=\Theta(D_{n}\sigma_{S})
. Absorbing the leading constant of
s
into
D_{n}
we only need to evaluate at
s=D_{n}\sigma_{S}
. Fix any deterministic sequence
D_{n}\geq 1
and define

s_{n}:=2\left\lfloor\frac{D_{n}\sigma_{S}}{2}\right\rceil\quad\text{so that}\quad|s_{n}-D_{n}\sigma_{S}|\leq 1\ \text{ and }\ s_{n}\equiv 0\pmod{2}.

Let
\varepsilon_{1},\dots,\varepsilon_{t}
be i.i.d. Rademacher,
\mathbb{P}(\varepsilon_{i}=\pm 1)=1/2
, and define

A_{t}:=\sum_{i=1}^{t}\varepsilon_{i}.

Notice
\mathbb{P}(A_{t}=s)=0
unless
s\equiv t\mod 2
.

We first compute the step-
2
ratio for a fixed simple random walk
A_{t}
. This will later serve as the main term for an expansion of
S
once we show that the relevant values of
t
are concentrated near
\mu
.

Lemma 10.6

(Expansion of
A_{t}
)
.

Uniformly over integers
t\geq 1
and
s
with
s\equiv t\pmod{2}
and
s\leq t-2
,

\log\frac{\mathbb{P}(A_{t}=s+2)}{\mathbb{P}(A_{t}=s)}=-\frac{2s+2}{t}+O\left(\frac{(s+1)^{2}}{t^{2}}\right).

Proof.

If
s\equiv t\mod 2
for
j:=(t+s)/2\in\{0,\dots,t\}
, we have

\mathbb{P}(A_{t}=s)=2^{-t}\binom{t}{j}.

Otherwise
\mathbb{P}(A_{t}=s)=0
. Indeed, if
B_{t}:=\#\{i:\varepsilon_{i}=+1\}\sim\mathrm{Bin}(t,1/2)
. Then
A_{t}=2B_{t}-t
and the event
\{A_{t}=s\}

is equivalent to
\{B_{t}=(t+s)/2\}
.

Now for
s\in\mathbb{Z}
that satisfies
s\equiv t\mod 2
and
s\leq t-2
as above
j=(t+s)/2
. Then
(t+s+2)/2=j+1
and

\frac{\mathbb{P}(A_{t}=s+2)}{\mathbb{P}(A_{t}=s)}=\frac{\binom{t}{j+1}}{\binom{t}{j}}=\frac{t-j}{j+1}=\frac{t-\frac{t+s}{2}}{\frac{t+s}{2}+1}=\frac{t-s}{t+s+2}.

By taking logarithms,

\log\frac{\mathbb{P}(A_{t}=s+2)}{\mathbb{P}(A_{t}=s)}=\log\Big(\frac{t-s}{t+s+2}\Big)=\log(1-u),\qquad u:=\frac{2s+2}{t+s+2}.

If
s\leq t-2
then
0<u\leq 1/2
. For
u\in[0,1/2]
we have
|\log(1-u)+u|\leq 2u^{2}
,
so
\log(1-u)=-u+O(u^{2})
. Also

u=\frac{2s+2}{t}\cdot\frac{1}{1+\frac{s+2}{t}}=\frac{2s+2}{t}+O\left(\frac{(s+1)^{2}}{t^{2}}\right),\qquad u^{2}=O\left(\frac{(s+1)^{2}}{t^{2}}\right).

Combining yields the claim.
∎

The next lemma rewrites the law of
S
as a mixture of the laws of the random walks
A_{t}
, where the mixing variable is the random support overlap
T
. This decomposition is the key reduction that allows us to transfer estimates for
A_{t}
to estimates for
S
.

Lemma 10.7

(Binomial-mixture representation)
.

We have
T=\sum_{i=1}^{n}1\{|X_{i}|=1\}\sim\mathrm{Bin}(n,q)
with
q=(k/n)^{2}
and
\mathbb{E}[T]=\mu=k^{2}/n
. Moreover, conditional on
T=t
,

S\mid(T=t)\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\ A_{t}.

Consequently, for every
s\in\mathbb{Z}
,

\mathbb{P}(S=s)=\sum_{t=0}^{n}\mathbb{P}(T=t)\mathbb{P}(A_{t}=s).

Proof.

Each
X_{i}
is nonzero with probability
q
, so
T\sim\mathrm{Bin}(n,q)
. Given the size of the support of set (hence given
T=t
),
the signs are i.i.d. symmetric, so the sum is a length-
t
Rademacher walk
A_{t}
. Averaging over
T
gives the formula.
∎

For fixed
s
, define weights

w_{t}(s):=\mathbb{P}(T=t)\mathbb{P}(A_{t}=s),\qquad\text{for all}\quad t=0,1,\dots,n.

Then
\mathbb{P}(S=s)=\sum_{t}w_{t}(s)
, and note that
w_{t}(s)=0
automatically unless
t\equiv s\mod 2
.

Next, assume
\mathbb{P}(S=s)>0
for some fixed value
s\in\mathbb{R}
and define

r_{t}(s):=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{P}(A_{t}=s+2)}{\mathbb{P}(A_{t}=s)}&\text{if }\mathbb{P}(A_{t}=s)>0,\\[8.00003pt]
0&\text{if }\mathbb{P}(A_{t}=s)=0.\end{cases}

(10.6)

Using the mixture representation, we now express the ratio
\mathbb{P}(S=s+2)/\mathbb{P}(S=s)
as a weighted average of the corresponding conditional ratios for
A_{t}
. Thus, the problem reduces to understanding which values of
t
carry most of the mass and how much
r_{t}(s)
varies across those values.

Lemma 10.8

(Step-
2
ratio as a weighted average)
.

Assume
\mathbb{P}(S=s)>0
for some fixed value
s\in\mathbb{R}
. Then,

\frac{\mathbb{P}(S=s+2)}{\mathbb{P}(S=s)}=\frac{\sum_{t=0}^{n}w_{t}(s)r_{t}(s)}{\sum_{t=0}^{n}w_{t}(s)}.

Equivalently, if
u_{t}(s):=w_{t}(s)/\sum_{u}w_{u}(s)
, for all
t\geq 0
(so
u_{t}(s)\geq 0
and
\sum_{t}u_{t}(s)=1
), then
