\frac{\mathbb{P}(S=s+2)}{\mathbb{P}(S=s)}=\sum_{t=0}^{n}u_{t}(s)r_{t}(s),\quad\text{for all}\quad t=0,1,\dots,n.

Proof.

By Lemma
10.7
,

\mathbb{P}(S=s+2)=\sum_{t}\mathbb{P}(T=t)\mathbb{P}(A_{t}=s+2).

If
\mathbb{P}(A_{t}=s)=0
then also
\mathbb{P}(A_{t}=s+2)=0
, so the identity

\mathbb{P}(A_{t}=s+2)=r_{t}(s)\mathbb{P}(A_{t}=s)
holds for all
t
.
Hence

\mathbb{P}(S=s+2)=\sum_{t}\mathbb{P}(T=t)r_{t}(s)\mathbb{P}(A_{t}=s)=\sum_{t}w_{t}(s)r_{t}(s).

Divide by
\mathbb{P}(S=s)=\sum_{t}w_{t}(s)
.
∎

To use the weighted-average representation effectively, we need to know that the conditional ratio
r_{t}(s)
does not change much when
t
stays in the typical window around
\mu
. The following lemma provides precisely this stability estimate.

Lemma 10.9
.

Assume
k=n^{\beta+o(1)}
,
\beta\in(1/2,1)
and in particular
\mu=k^{2}/n=\omega(1)
. Let
s\leq D_{n}\sqrt{\mu}
and define

W:=C^{\prime}\sqrt{\mu}\log\mu,\qquad\mathcal{I}:=\{t\in\{0,\dots,n\}:\ |t-\mu|\leq W\},

where
C^{\prime}>0
is a fixed constant.
Then for all
t,t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}
with
t,t^{\prime}\geq s+2
,

|r_{t}(s)-r_{t^{\prime}}(s)|=O\left(\frac{D_{n}\log\mu}{\mu}\right),

where
r_{t}(s),s\in\mathbb{R}
was defined in (
10.6
).

Proof.

For real
t>s
set
f(t):=(t-s)/(t+s+2)
. Then

f^{\prime}(t)=\frac{2s+2}{(t+s+2)^{2}}.

On
\mathcal{I}
we have
t\geq\mu-W=\Theta(\mu)
and
s\leq D_{n}\sqrt{\mu}=o(\mu)
, since
D_{n}=O(\log n)
and
\mu=k^{2}/n
. Hence,
(t+s+2)^{2}\geq c\mu^{2}

for large
\mu
, so for some universal constant
C>0

|f^{\prime}(t)|\leq\frac{C(s+1)}{\mu^{2}}\leq\frac{CD_{n}\sqrt{\mu}}{\mu^{2}}=\frac{CD_{n}}{\mu^{3/2}}.

By the mean value theorem and
|t-t^{\prime}|\leq 2W
,

|r_{t}(s)-r_{t^{\prime}}(s)|\leq\sup_{\xi\in\mathcal{I}}|f^{\prime}(\xi)||t-t^{\prime}|\leq\frac{CD_{n}}{\mu^{3/2}}\cdot(2W)=O\left(\frac{D_{n}\log\mu}{\mu}\right).

∎

In order to show that the atypical values
t\notin\mathcal{I}
contribute negligibly, we also need a lower bound on the denominator
\mathbb{P}(S=s_{n})
. The next lemma gives such a bound at the relevant scale
s_{n}=D_{n}\sqrt{\mu}
.

Lemma 10.10
.

Assume
k=n^{\beta+o(1)},\beta\in(1/2,1)
and
s_{n}=\Theta\left(D_{n}\sqrt{\mu}\right)
with
D_{n}\leq C\log n,C>0
and
\mu=\omega(\log^{6}n)
.
Then for all sufficiently large
n
,

\mathbb{P}(S=s_{n})\geq\frac{c_{1}}{\mu}\exp\Big(-\frac{s_{n}^{2}(1+o(1))}{2\mu}\Big)\geq\frac{c_{1}}{\mu}\exp\Big(-\Theta(D_{n}^{2})\Big),

for a universal constant
c_{1}>0
.

Proof.

Set
J:=\{t:\ |t-\mu|\leq 4\sqrt{\mu}\}
. Since
\mathrm{Var}(T)=\mu(1-q)\leq\mu
,
Chebyshev inequality implies
\mathbb{P}(T\in J)\geq 15/16
. Also
\mathbb{E}[(-1)^{T}]=(1-2q)^{n}=(1-2\mu/n)^{n}\leq e^{-2\mu}
,
so for either parity,

\mathbb{P}(T\equiv s_{n}\!\!\!\!\pmod{2})=\frac{1}{2}\Big(1+(-1)^{s_{n}}\mathbb{E}[(-1)^{T}]\Big)\geq\frac{1}{2}-\frac{e^{-2\mu}}{2}.

Hence, by a union bound,

\mathbb{P}(T\in J,\ T\equiv s_{n}\!\!\!\!\pmod{2})\geq 1-\frac{1}{16}-\Big(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{e^{-2\mu}}{2}\Big)\geq\frac{1}{3}

for all large
\mu
. Set
J^{\prime}:=\{t\in J:t\equiv s_{n}\pmod{2}\}
and notice that among integers in
J
, at most
4\sqrt{\mu}+1
have parity
s_{n}
, so
there exists
t_{s_{n}}\in J^{\prime}
with

\mathbb{P}(T=t_{s_{n}})\geq\frac{1/3}{|J^{\prime}|}\geq\frac{1/3}{4\sqrt{\mu}+1}=\Omega(\mu^{-1/2}).

Now apply Lemma
10.7
and keep only the term
t=t_{s_{n}}
:

\mathbb{P}(S=s_{n})\geq\mathbb{P}(T=t_{s_{n}})\mathbb{P}(A_{t_{s_{n}}}=s_{n}).

Since
t_{s_{n}}=\mu+O(\sqrt{\mu})
, we have
s_{n}\leq D_{n}\sqrt{\mu}\leq 2D_{n}\sqrt{t_{s_{n}}}
for large
\mu
.

Recall now that
A_{t}=\sum_{i=1}^{t}\varepsilon_{i}
with
\varepsilon_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle iid}}{{\sim}}\mathrm{Rad}(\pm 1)
, so Lemma
10.1
applies with
n=t
,
T=D_{n}
and
|s|\leq D_{n}\sqrt{t}
since
D_{n}=o(t^{1/6})
. Indeed, we have
|s|=o(t^{2/3})
, hence in particular
|s|\leq t/2
for all sufficiently large
t
.
Therefore, for all large
t
,

\mathbb{P}(A_{t}=s)\geq\frac{c_{0}}{\sqrt{t}}\exp\Big(-\frac{s^{2}}{2t}\Big)

(10.7)

for a universal constant
c_{0}>0
. Equation (
10.7
) yields

\mathbb{P}(A_{t_{s_{n}}}=s_{n})\geq\frac{c_{0}}{\sqrt{t_{s_{n}}}}\exp\Big(-\frac{s_{n}^{2}}{2t_{s_{n}}}\Big)\geq\frac{c_{0}^{\prime}}{\sqrt{\mu}}\exp\Big(-\frac{s_{n}^{2}}{2\mu}(1+o(1))\Big).

Multiplying with
\mathbb{P}(T=t_{s_{n}})\geq c/\sqrt{\mu}
gives the result.
∎

For a fixed integer
s\in\mathbb{N}
define an integer
t_{\star}=t_{\star}(s)
to be any integer satisfying

t_{\star}\equiv s\mod 2,\qquad|t_{\star}-\mu|\leq 1.

(10.8)

We can now combine concentration of the mixing variable
T
, the stability of
r_{t}(s)
on the typical window, and the lower bound on
\mathbb{P}(S=s_{n})
. This allows us to approximate the logarithmic step-
2
ratio for
S
.

Lemma 10.11
.

Assume
k=n^{\beta+o(1)},\beta\in(1/2,1)
and
D_{n}\leq C\log n
,
C>0
.
Let
s=\Theta(D_{n}\sqrt{\mu})
.
Then

\displaystyle\log\mathbb{P}(S=s_{n}+2)-\log\mathbb{P}(S=s_{n})=\log\Big(\frac{t_{\star}-s_{n}}{t_{\star}+s_{n}+2}\Big)+O\left(\frac{D_{n}\log n}{\mu}\right).

Proof.

By Lemma
10.8
,

\frac{\mathbb{P}(S=s_{n}+2)}{\mathbb{P}(S=s_{n})}=\sum_{t=0}^{n}u_{t}(s_{n})r_{t}(s_{n}),\qquad u_{t}(s_{n}):=\frac{w_{t}(s_{n})}{\sum_{u}w_{u}(s_{n})}.

Split the sum into
t\in\mathcal{I}
and
t\notin\mathcal{I}
, where
\mathcal{I}=\{|t-\mu|\leq W\}
with
W=C^{\prime}\sqrt{\mu}\log\mu
for some fixed
C^{\prime}>0
.

Step 1 (outside
\mathcal{I}
):

Since
0\leq\mathbb{P}(A_{t}=s_{n})\leq 1
,

\sum_{t\notin\mathcal{I}}w_{t}(s_{n})\leq\mathbb{P}(T\notin\mathcal{I})\quad\text{and therefore}\quad\sum_{t\notin\mathcal{I}}u_{t}(s_{n})\leq\frac{\mathbb{P}(T\notin\mathcal{I})}{\mathbb{P}(S=s_{n})}.

By a standard Chernoff bound, for
u\in(0,\mu)
,

\mathbb{P}(|T-\mu|>u)\leq 2\exp(-u^{2}/(3\mu))
. Plugging
u=C^{\prime}\sqrt{\mu}\log\mu
gives

u^{2}/(3\mu)=\frac{C^{\prime 2}}{3}\log^{2}\mu
. Therefore,
\mathbb{P}(T\notin\mathcal{I})\leq 2e^{-c\log^{2}\mu}
, with
c=C^{\prime 2}/3
, and by Lemma
10.10
,

\mathbb{P}(S=s_{n})\geq(c_{1}/\mu)\exp(-s_{n}^{2}/(2\mu))
. Hence

\sum_{t\notin\mathcal{I}}u_{t}(s_{n})\leq\mu\cdot 2e^{-c\log^{2}\mu}\cdot\exp\Big(\frac{s_{n}^{2}}{2\mu}\Big)\cdot\frac{1}{c_{1}}=\exp\Big(-c\log^{2}\mu+\frac{s_{n}^{2}}{2\mu}+O(\log n)\Big).

Since
s_{n}^{2}/\mu=\Theta(D_{n}^{2})
,
\mu=n^{\Theta(1)}
and
D_{n}\leq C\log n
, choosing
C^{\prime}
to be large enough:

\sum_{t\notin\mathcal{I}}u_{t}(s_{n})=O(e^{-\log^{2}n}).

(10.9)

Step 2 (inside
\mathcal{I}
):

By Lemma
10.9
,

\sup_{t\in\mathcal{I}}|r_{t}(s_{n})-r_{t_{\star}}(s_{n})|=O\left(\frac{CD_{n}\log\mu}{\mu}\right).

Step 3 (conclude):

For every integer
s\geq 0
and every
t
with
\mathbb{P}(A_{t}=s)>0
(equivalently
s\equiv t\pmod{2}
and
|s|\leq t
),

r_{t}(s)=\frac{\mathbb{P}(A_{t}=s+2)}{\mathbb{P}(A_{t}=s)}=\frac{\binom{t}{(t+s+2)/2}}{\binom{t}{(t+s)/2}}=\frac{t-s}{t+s+2}.

Therefore, for
s\geq 0
it holds that
0\leq r_{t}(s_{n})\leq 1
, and thus

\displaystyle\left|\sum_{t}u_{t}(s_{n})r_{t}(s_{n})-r_{t_{\star}}(s_{n})\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{t\in\mathcal{I}}|r_{t}(s_{n})-r_{t_{\star}}(s_{n})|+2\sum_{t\notin\mathcal{I}}u_{t}(s_{n})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{CD_{n}\log n}{\mu}+o\left(\frac{D_{n}\log n}{\mu}\right),

where for the last inequality we used Step
2
and (
10.9
). Thus,

\frac{\mathbb{P}(S=s_{n}+2)}{\mathbb{P}(S=s_{n})}=r_{t_{\star}}(s_{n})+O\left(\frac{D_{n}\log n}{\mu}\right)=\frac{t_{\star}-s_{n}}{t_{\star}+s_{n}+2}+O\left(\frac{D_{n}\log n}{\mu}\right).

Write
R:=\frac{t_{\star}-s_{n}}{t_{\star}+s_{n}+2}
and
\delta=O\left(\frac{D_{n}\log n}{\mu}\right)
.
Since
t_{\star}=\mu+O(1)
and
s_{n}=o(\mu)
, we have
R=\Theta(1)
, so

\log\frac{\mathbb{P}(S=s_{n}+2)}{\mathbb{P}(S=s_{n})}=\log(R+\delta)=\log R+\log\Big(1+\frac{\delta}{R}\Big)=\log R+O\Big(\frac{\delta}{R}\Big)=\log R+O\left(\frac{D_{n}\log n}{\mu}\right),

proving our desired result.
Taking logarithms (using that the main ratio is bounded away from
0
and
\infty
since
s_{n}=o(\mu)
)
gives the claimed form.
∎

With the numerator now identified up to a controlled error, it remains to divide by the increment
(s_{n}+2)^{r}-s_{n}^{r}
. The proposition below performs this final expansion and yields the claimed order of
\Lambda_{r}(s_{n})
.

Proposition 10.12
.

Fix an integer
r\geq 2
. Assume
k=n^{\beta+o(1)}
,
\beta\in(1/2,1)
and
D_{n}\leq C\log n
.
Let
s_{n}=\Theta(D_{n}\sqrt{\mu})
.
Then,

\Lambda_{r}(s_{n})=-\Theta\left(\frac{1}{D_{n}^{r-2}\mu^{r/2}}\right).

Proof.

By Lemma
10.11
,

\log\mathbb{P}(S=s+2)-\log\mathbb{P}(S=s)=\log\Big(\frac{t_{\star}-s_{n}}{t_{\star}+s_{n}+2}\Big)+O\Big(\frac{D_{n}\log n}{\mu}\Big).

Apply Lemma
10.6
with
t=t_{\star}
, defined in (
10.8
), and
s=s_{n}
:

\log\Big(\frac{t_{\star}-s_{n}}{t_{\star}+s_{n}+2}\Big)=-\frac{2s_{n}+2}{t_{\star}}+O\left(\frac{(s_{n}+1)^{2}}{t_{\star}^{2}}\right).

Combining with
t_{\star}=\mu+O(1)
,

\log\mathbb{P}(S=s+2)-\log\mathbb{P}(S=s)=-\frac{2s_{n}+2}{\mu}+O\left(\frac{(s_{n}+1)^{2}}{\mu^{2}}\right)+O\left(\frac{D_{n}\log n}{\mu}\right).

(10.10)

Now divide by
(s_{n}+2)^{r}-s_{n}^{r}
. Since
s_{n}\to\infty
, as
D_{n}\sqrt{\mu}\to\infty
, using the binomial theorem:

(s+2)^{r}-s^{r}=\sum_{j=1}^{r}\binom{r}{j}2^{j}s^{r-j}=2rs^{r-1}+\sum_{j=2}^{r}\binom{r}{j}2^{j}s^{r-j}=2rs^{r-1}+O_{r}(s^{r-2}).

Therefore,

(s_{n}+2)^{r}-s_{n}^{r}=2rs_{n}^{r-1}\Big(1+O_{r}\big(\tfrac{1}{s_{n}}\big)\Big)

which implies

\frac{1}{(s_{n}+2)^{r}-s_{n}^{r}}=\frac{1}{2rs_{n}^{r-1}}\Big(1+O_{r}\big(\tfrac{1}{s_{n}}\big)\Big).

(10.11)

Multiplying (
10.10
), (
10.11
) implies

\Lambda_{r}(s_{n})=-\frac{1}{r\mu}s_{n}^{-(r-2)}\Big(1+O_{r}\big(\tfrac{1}{s_{n}}\big)\Big)+O\left(\frac{D_{n}\log n}{\mu s_{n}^{r-1}}\right)+O\left(\frac{(s_{n}+1)^{2}}{\mu^{2}s_{n}^{r-1}}\right).

(10.12)

In particular, since
s_{n}=\Theta(D_{n}\sqrt{\mu})
this implies the desired inequality and the proof is complete.
∎

10.1.4
Proof of Lemma
10.4

Let
X_{i}:=v_{i}v_{i}^{\prime}\in\{-1,0,+1\},i\in[n]
and
T:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{1}\{|X_{i}|=1\}
.
Then
T\sim\mathrm{Bin}(n,q)
with
q=(k/n)^{2}
and
\mathbb{E}[T]=\mu=nq
.
Conditionally on
\{T=t\}
, the
t
nonzero
X_{i}
’s are i.i.d. uniform on
\{\pm 1\}
, hence

S_{n}\mid(T=t)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\sum_{j=1}^{t}\xi_{j}
with
\xi_{j}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle iid}}{{\sim}}\mathrm{Rad}(\pm 1)
.
In particular
S_{n}\leq T
always, and on
\{T=m\}
we have

\mathbb{P}(S_{n}\geq m\mid T=m)=\mathbb{P}(|\sum_{j=1}^{m}\xi_{j}|=m)=2^{1-m}
.
Therefore, for every integer
m\geq 1
,

2^{1-m}\,\mathbb{P}(T=m)\ \leq\ \mathbb{P}(S_{n}\geq m)\ \leq\ \mathbb{P}(T\geq m).

(10.13)

Notice that

\mathbb{P}(T\geq m)\leq\binom{n}{m}\,q^{m}\leq\frac{n^{m}}{m!}\,q^{m}=\frac{(nq)^{m}}{m!}=\frac{\mu^{m}}{m!}.

(10.14)

Next, for
m=n^{o(1)}
and since
\mu=o(1)
,

\mathbb{P}(T=m)=\binom{n}{m}q^{m}(1-q)^{n-m}=\frac{\mu^{m}}{m!}\,(1+o(1)),

since
\binom{n}{m}=n^{m}/m!\cdot(1+o(1))
and
(1-q)^{n-m}=\exp(-(n-m)q+o(1))=1+o(1)
. Combining this with (
10.13
) and (
10.14
) yields, uniformly for
m=n^{o(1)}
,

\frac{\mu^{m}}{m!}\,2^{-m}\ \leq\ \mathbb{P}(S_{n}\geq m)\ \leq\ \frac{\mu^{m}}{m!}.

Taking logs and using
\log m!=m\log m-m+O(\log m)
gives

-\log\mathbb{P}(S_{n}\geq m)=m\log\frac{1}{\mu}\ +\ O(m\log m).

Since
\log(1/\mu)=(1-2a)\log n
, the term
m\log m
is of lower order
relative to
m\log(1/\mu)=\Theta(m\log n)
and therefore there exist constants
c,C>0
such that for all integers
c_{0}\log n\leq D_{n}\leq n^{o(1)}
,

-\log\mathbb{P}(S_{n}\geq m)\geq D_{n}\ \ \text{for all}\ \ m\geq C\,\frac{D_{n}}{\log n},\qquad-\log\mathbb{P}(S_{n}\geq m)\leq D_{n}\ \ \text{for all}\ \ m\leq c\,\frac{D_{n}}{\log n}.

By the definition of
q(D_{n})
and monotonicity of
m\mapsto\mathbb{P}(S_{n}\geq m)
, this implies

c\,\frac{D_{n}}{\log n}\ \leq\ q(D_{n})\ \leq\ C\,\frac{D_{n}}{\log n},

as claimed.

10.1.5
Proof of Lemma
10.5

Let
S=\langle v,v^{\prime}\rangle
, so that
\langle X,X^{\prime}\rangle=S^{r}
.

We first claim that uniformly for all integers
m\geq 1
with
m=n^{o(1)}
,

\mathbb{P}(S=m)=\frac{\mu^{m}}{2^{m}m!}\,(1+o(1)),

(10.15)

where, as before,
\mu=k^{2}/n\to 0
. Indeed, by Lemma
10.7
,

\mathbb{P}(S=m)=\sum_{j\geq 0}\mathbb{P}(T=m+2j)\,\mathbb{P}(A_{m+2j}=m),

where
T\sim\mathrm{Bin}(n,q)
and
A_{t}=\sum_{i=1}^{t}\xi_{i}
with

\xi_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle iid}}{{\sim}}\mathrm{Rad}(\pm 1)
.
For the
j=0
term,

\mathbb{P}(T=m)\,\mathbb{P}(A_{m}=m)=\mathbb{P}(T=m)\,2^{-m}.

Since
m=n^{o(1)}
, we have uniformly

\mathbb{P}(T=m)=\binom{n}{m}q^{m}(1-q)^{n-m}=\frac{\mu^{m}}{m!}\,(1+o(1)).

Hence

\mathbb{P}(T=m)\,\mathbb{P}(A_{m}=m)=\frac{\mu^{m}}{2^{m}m!}\,(1+o(1)).

It remains to show that the contribution of
j\geq 1
is negligible.
Using
\mathbb{P}(T=t)\leq\mu^{t}/t!
for all
t\geq 0
, we get

\displaystyle\sum_{j\geq 1}\mathbb{P}(T=m+2j)\,\mathbb{P}(A_{m+2j}=m)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j\geq 1}\frac{\mu^{m+2j}}{(m+2j)!}\binom{m+2j}{j}2^{-(m+2j)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mu^{m}}{2^{m}}\sum_{j\geq 1}\frac{\mu^{2j}}{4^{j}\,j!\,(m+j)!}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mu^{m}}{2^{m}m!}\sum_{j\geq 1}\frac{\mu^{2j}}{4^{j}\,j!}=O\!\left(\frac{\mu^{m+2}}{2^{m}m!}\right).

Since
\mu\to 0
, this is
o\!\left(\mu^{m}/(2^{m}m!)\right)
uniformly in
m=n^{o(1)}
.
This proves (
10.15
).

Applying (
10.15
) with
m=s_{n}
and
m=s_{n}+2
, we obtain

\frac{\mathbb{P}(S=s_{n}+2)}{\mathbb{P}(S=s_{n})}=\frac{\mu}{2(s_{n}+2)}\,(1+o(1)),

and therefore

\log\mathbb{P}(S=s_{n})-\log\mathbb{P}(S=s_{n}+1)=\log\frac{1}{\mu}+\log(2(s_{n}+1))+o(1).

(10.16)

Now

\mu=n^{-(1-2\beta)+o(1)},\ \text{so}\ \log\mu=(2\beta-1+o(1))\log n.

Also, since
s_{n}=n^{o(1)}
,

\log(s_{n}+2)=o(\log n).

Thus (
10.16
) gives

\log\mathbb{P}(S=s_{n})-\log\mathbb{P}(S=s_{n}+2)=\Theta(\log n).

(10.17)

Next, since
s_{n}\geq 1
and
r\geq 2
, we have
(s_{n}+1)^{r}-s_{n}^{r}=\Theta(s_{n}^{\,r-1}).

Therefore,

-\Delta_{a_{n}}\log\mathbb{P}(\langle X,X^{\prime}\rangle=s_{n}^{r})=\frac{\log\mathbb{P}(S=s_{n})-\log\mathbb{P}(S=s_{n}+2)}{(s_{n}+2)^{r}-s_{n}^{r}}=\Theta\left(\left(\frac{\log n}{(s_{n})^{r-1}}\right)\right),

proving the first part of the lemma.

Set now

s_{n}:=q_{n}(D_{n}).
By Lemma
10.4
,
s_{n}=\Theta\left(D_{n}/\log n\right).
Since
D_{n}=n^{o(1)}
and
D_{n}\geq c_{0}\log n
, it follows that
1\leq s_{n}=n^{o(1)}
for all sufficiently large
n
.
Moreover,

(s_{n}+1)^{r}-s_{n}^{r}=\Theta\left(\left(\frac{D_{n}}{\log n}\right)^{r-1}\right).

(10.18)

Therefore, combining the above for this choice of
s_{n},

-\Delta_{a_{n}}\log\mathbb{P}(\langle X,X^{\prime}\rangle=s_{n}^{r})=\Theta\!\left(\frac{\log n}{(D_{n}/\log n)^{r-1}}\right)=\Theta\!\left(\frac{(\log n)^{r}}{D_{n}^{\,r-1}}\right).

This proves the lemma.

10.2
Proofs for Tensor PCA

Suppose
v,v^{\prime}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{n})
independently drawn and let
S=\langle v,v^{\prime}\rangle
. Then,
X=v^{\otimes r},X^{\prime}=(v^{\prime})^{\otimes r}
are two i.i.d. draws from Gaussian Tensor PCA and
S^{r}=\langle X,X^{\prime}\rangle
. Also, denote
f_{S}
the density of
S
. We state the two Lemmas we are going to use for the proof of Theorem
3.1
.

As we mentioned before these Lemmas are doing the following for each model: evaluate the leading order of the quantile
q(D_{n})
and then we find the order of the derivative that appears in (
2.6
) evaluated at order
q(D_{n})
. These steps allow us to apply Theorem
2.7
for Tensor PCA.

Lemma 10.13
.

Fix an integer
r\geq 2
. Then there exist absolute constants
0<c<C<\infty
and an absolute
n_{0}\geq 1
such that
for all
n\geq n_{0}
and all
\Omega(1)=D\leq n
,

\big(c(\sqrt{nD}+D)\big)^{r}\leq q(D)\leq\big(C(\sqrt{nD}+D)\big)^{r}.

The proof of this Lemma is deferred to Section
10.2.2
.

Lemma 10.14
.

For the Tensor PCA model with the Gaussian prior and for any
1\leq D_{n}=o(\sqrt{n})
the following derivative is of order:

-\left.\frac{d}{ds}\log f_{S}(s)\right|_{s=(D_{n}n)^{r/2}}=\Theta(n^{-r/2}D_{n}^{(3-r)/2})=\Theta(\lambda_{\mathrm{ALG}}\cdot D_{n}^{(3-r)/2}).

The proof of this Lemma is deferred to Section
10.2.2
.

10.2.1
Proof of Theorem
3.1

We prove this by directly applying Theorem
2.7
. To do this we first prove that the prior satisfies the main assumption
2.4
. Specifically, we verify the four conditions.

Condition 1:
Since the standard Gaussian distribution has non-negative cumulants of all order and Tensor PCA is of the form (
8.1
), Lemma
8.5
implies that for all multi-indices
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
it holds that

\kappa_{\alpha}(X_{i})\geq 0.

Condition 2:
This follows directly form the fact that each
X_{i}
is a products of
r
Gaussian random variables.

Condition 3:
This condition is straightforward since, for
D_{n}^{\prime}=\Theta(D_{n}\log^{2}n)
and using Lemma
10.13

q(D_{n}^{\prime})\geq(cD_{n}^{\prime})^{r}=\omega(\max\{\log^{-D_{n}}n,n^{-C}\})

for any
C>0
.

Condition 4:
Using Lemma
10.13
we know that for
D_{n}=o(\sqrt{n})
the derivative at
q(D_{n})
is of order

(nD_{n})^{r/2}.

Therefore Item 4 from Assumption
2.4
is equivalent to proving that there exists a constant
C>0
such that:

\frac{D_{n}^{(3-r)/2}n^{-r/2}}{A_{n}}\leq\frac{1}{q(D_{n}^{\prime})\log n}.

Using Lemma
10.13
we know that for
D_{n}^{\prime}\leq\sqrt{n},
it holds that

q(D_{n}^{\prime})\leq C_{1}(D_{n}^{\prime}n)^{r/2}.

Plugging this inequality to the one above we get that Assumption 4 holds for all
D_{n}^{\prime}=o(\sqrt{n})
if:

\frac{D_{n}^{(3-r)/2}n^{-r/2}}{A_{n}}\leq\frac{1}{C_{1}^{\prime}n^{r/2}(D_{n}^{\prime})^{r/2}\log n}\quad\text{or equivalently}\quad C_{1}^{\prime}D_{n}^{3/2}\log^{r+1}n\leq A_{n}

where we used
D_{n}^{\prime}=D_{n}\log^{2}n
to get the last inequality. Therefore, for
A_{n}=C_{1}^{\prime}(D_{n})^{3/2}\log^{r+1}n
the inequality holds.

Now combining the above with (
2.6
) we conclude for all
\lambda>0

\mathrm{Corr}^{\leq D_{n}}_{P_{0}}\left(\frac{1}{C_{1}^{\prime}D_{n}^{3/2}\log^{r+1}n}\cdot\big(\lambda+\frac{\textup{d}}{\textup{d}q}\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ann},\lambda}\bigg|_{q=q(D_{n})}\big)\right)^{2}\leq q(D_{n}^{\prime})\leq C_{1}(D_{n}^{\prime}n)^{r/2}.

Now notice that

\displaystyle\mathrm{MMSE}^{\mathrm{trivial}}_{X}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\|X\|^{2}-(\mathbb{E}\|X\|)^{2}=n^{r}.

(10.19)

Also, from Lemma
10.14
and (
2.6
) for all
\lambda>0
,
\lambda+\frac{\textup{d}}{\textup{d}q}\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ann},\lambda}\bigg|_{q=q(D_{n})}=\Theta(D_{n}^{(3-r)/2}\lambda_{\mathrm{ALG}})
. C
Choosing
\lambda=D_{n}^{(3-r)/2}n^{-r/2}/\log n
now, implies
\frac{\textup{d}}{\textup{d}q}\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ann},\lambda}\bigg|_{q=q(D_{n})}\geq 0
. Therefore, using Theorem
2.7
,
\mathrm{Corr}(\lambda/A_{n})^{2}\leq 2q(D_{n}^{\prime}),
where
A_{n}=C_{1}^{\prime}(D_{n})^{3/2}\log^{r+1}n
. Equivalently,

\mathrm{MMSE}^{\leq D_{n}}_{X}\left(\frac{\lambda}{C_{1}^{\prime}D_{n}^{3/2}\log^{r+1}n}\right)\geq n^{r}-C_{1}(D_{n}^{\prime}n)^{r/2}.

Finally, substituting
\lambda_{\mathrm{ALG}}=n^{-r/2}

\mathrm{MMSE}^{\leq D_{n}}_{X}\left(\frac{\lambda_{\mathrm{ALG}}}{C_{1}^{\prime}D_{n}^{r/2}\log^{r+2}n}\right)\geq n^{r}-C_{1}(D_{n}^{\prime}n)^{r/2}.

Combining this with (
10.19
) we have proven that for any
D_{n}^{\prime}=o(\sqrt{n})
and
D_{n}=\frac{D_{n}^{\prime}}{\log^{2}n}=o(\sqrt{n}/\log^{2}n)

\mathrm{MMSE}^{\leq D_{n}}_{X}\left(\frac{\lambda_{\mathrm{ALG}}}{C_{1}^{\prime}D_{n}^{r/2}\log^{r+2}n}\right)\geq\mathrm{MMSE}^{\mathrm{trivial}}_{X}(1+o(1)),

completing the proof.

10.2.2
Proof of Lemma
10.13

Upper bound:

X_{i}:=v_{i}v_{i}^{\prime}
,
i\in[n]
are i.i.d. mean-zero sub-exponential, hence for some
absolute
c_{0}>0
,

\mathbb{P}(|S|\geq t)\leq 2\exp\Big(-c_{0}\min\Big\{\frac{t^{2}}{n},\,t\Big\}\Big)\qquad t\geq 0,

which implies
\mathbb{P}(|S|\geq C(\sqrt{nD}+D))\leq e^{-D}
for large enough absolute
C
.
Therefore
q(D)\leq(C(\sqrt{nD}+D))^{r}
.

Lower bound:

Condition on
v
. Since
v^{\prime}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{n})

is independent of
v
,

S=\langle v,v^{\prime}\rangle\mid v\sim\mathcal{N}\big(0,\|v\|_{2}^{2}\big).

Let
U:=\|v\|_{2}^{2}\sim\chi^{2}_{n}
and let
Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
independent of
U
. Then

S\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\sqrt{U}Z
.
Let
E:=\{U\geq n/2\}
. Since
\mathbb{E}U=n
and
\mathrm{Var}(U)=2n
, Chebyshev inequality gives

\mathbb{P}(E)\geq 1-\frac{\mathrm{Var}(U)}{n^{2}}=1-\frac{8}{n}\geq\frac{1}{2},

for all
n\geq 8
.
On
E
, we have
\sqrt{U}\geq\sqrt{n/2}
, hence for any
t>0
,

\mathbb{P}(|S|\geq t)\geq\mathbb{P}(E)\mathbb{P}\left(|Z|\geq\frac{t}{\sqrt{n/2}}\right)\geq\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{P}\left(|Z|\geq\frac{t}{\sqrt{n/2}}\right).

Choose
t=c\sqrt{nD}
with
c>0
small, so
t/\sqrt{n/2}=c\sqrt{D/2}
.
Using the standard Gaussian tail lower bound, there exists a constant
c_{1}>0
, such that for
x\geq c_{1}
,

\mathbb{P}(|Z|\geq x)\geq\frac{c_{1}^{2}}{3(c_{1}^{2}+1)x}e^{-x^{2}/2},

Since
D\geq 1
, we have
x=\Theta(\sqrt{D})\geq c_{1}
.
Thus for an absolute constant
c_{2}
only depends on
c_{1}
,

\mathbb{P}(|S|\geq c\sqrt{nD})\geq\frac{c_{2}}{\sqrt{D}}\exp\Big(-\frac{c^{2}}{4}D\Big)\geq e^{-D},

for all
\Omega(1)=D\leq n
by taking
0<c<2
and
D\geq c_{2}^{2}
sufficiently large.
Hence the
e^{-D}
quantile of
|S|
is at least
c\sqrt{nD}
in this regime and therefore
q(D)\geq(c(\sqrt{nD}+D))^{r}
.

10.2.3
Proof of Lemma
10.14

Suppose
v,v^{\prime}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{n})
independent. Then, as proved in Lemma
9.5
,
the density of
T=\langle v,v^{\prime}\rangle
can be written explicitly in terms of a modified Bessel function of the second kind
K_{\nu}
:

f_{T}(t)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}\,\Gamma\!\big(\tfrac{n}{2}\big)}\left(\frac{|t|}{2}\right)^{\frac{n}{2}-\frac{1}{2}}K_{\frac{n}{2}-\frac{1}{2}}(|t|),\qquad t\in\mathbb{R}.

Therefore,

\log f_{T}(t)=C+\Big(\frac{n}{2}-\frac{1}{2}\Big)\log t+\log K_{\frac{n}{2}-\frac{1}{2}}(t),

for a constant
C
independent of
t
, Lemma
9.1
gives

\frac{\textup{d}}{\textup{d}t}\log\mathbb{P}(\langle v,v^{\prime}\rangle=t)=\frac{\textup{d}}{\textup{d}t}\log f_{T}(t)=\frac{n-1}{t}-\frac{K_{(n+1)/2}(t)}{K_{(n-1)/2}(t)}.

Let
\nu=\frac{n-1}{2}
. We recall the identity for the modified Bessel function of the second kind, stated in Lemma
9.1
and see also
[
1
, Chap 9]
,

K_{\nu+1}(t)=\frac{2\nu}{t}K_{\nu}(t)+K_{\nu-1}(t),\qquad t>0.

Therefore,

\frac{\textup{d}}{\textup{d}t}\log\mathbb{P}(\langle v,v^{\prime}\rangle=t)=\frac{2\nu}{t}-\frac{K_{\nu+1}(t)}{K_{\nu}(t)}=-\frac{K_{\nu-1}(t)}{K_{\nu}(t)},

for all
t>0
.
According to Corollary
9.4
, which is derived from
[
35
, (1.10)]
, for

t=\sqrt{nD_{n}}
and
D_{n}=o(\sqrt{n})
,

\frac{K_{\nu-1}(t)}{K_{\nu}(t)}=\frac{t}{2\nu}(1+o(1))=\sqrt{\frac{D_{n}}{n}}(1+o(1)).

Then, by the chain rule, if
t=s^{1/r}
,

\frac{\textup{d}}{\textup{d}s}\log\mathbb{P}(\langle X,X^{\prime}\rangle=s)=\frac{\textup{d}}{\textup{d}s}\log\mathbb{P}(\langle v,v^{\prime}\rangle^{r}=s)=\frac{\textup{d}}{\textup{d}t}\log\mathbb{P}(\langle v,v^{\prime}\rangle=t)\cdot\frac{t^{1-r}}{r},

so plugging in
t=\sqrt{nD_{n}}
(that is,
s=(nD_{n})^{r/2}
) gives

-\frac{\textup{d}}{\textup{d}s}\log\mathbb{P}(\langle X,X^{\prime}\rangle=s)\Bigg|_{s=(nD_{n})^{r/2}}=\Theta\!\left(\sqrt{\frac{D_{n}}{n}}\right)\cdot\frac{(nD_{n})^{(1-r)/2}}{r}=\Theta\!\left(n^{-r/2}D_{n}^{\,1-r/2}\right).

10.3
Proofs for Sparse Clustering

Let
\xi=(\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n})^{\top}
have i.i.d.
\mathrm{Rad}(1/2)
entries and note that the signal matrix in this model can be written as
X:=\xi\mu^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times p}
, i.e.,

X_{ij}=\xi_{i}\mu_{j}
. As we have discussed, the SNR
\lambda
when we write it as a Gaussian Additive Model of the form (
1.1
), will be
\lambda=\Delta/s
.
Clustering amounts to recovering the labels
(\xi_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
(up to a global sign flip) from
Y
.

10.3.1
Notation

We will start with some notation. Let
n,p\geq 1
where
p=n^{c+o(1)}
, for some constant
c>0
, and
s=s_{n,p}
satisfy
\widetilde{\omega}(\sqrt{p})=s=o(p)
.
Let also
\xi_{i},\xi_{i}^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle iid}}{{\sim}}\mathrm{Rad}(\pm 1)
for
i\in[n]
.
Define

A:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\xi_{i}\xi_{i}^{\prime}.

Then
\mathbb{E}[A]=0
and
\mathrm{Var}(A)=n
.
Let
b_{j},b_{j}^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle iid}}{{\sim}}\mathrm{Ber}(s/p)
and
g_{j},g_{j}^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle iid}}{{\sim}}N(0,1)
, all independent.
Then, we can define
\mu,\mu^{\prime}
to be two independent draws from the priors

\mu_{j}:=b_{j}g_{j},\ \mu_{j}^{\prime}:=b_{j}^{\prime}g_{j}^{\prime}\

and we denote

Z_{j}:=\mu_{j}\mu_{j}^{\prime}=(b_{j}b_{j}^{\prime})(g_{j}g_{j}^{\prime})
,

B:=\sum_{j=1}^{p}Z_{j}
.
Let also
\delta_{j}:=b_{j}b_{j}^{\prime}\sim\mathrm{Ber}((s/p)^{2})
and
Y_{j}:=g_{j}g_{j}^{\prime}
. Then,
Z_{j}=\delta_{j}Y_{j}
and
U:=\sum_{j=1}^{p}\delta_{j}\sim\mathrm{Bin}(p,(s/p)^{2}).

With this notation, if we set
S=\langle X,X^{\prime}\rangle
then

S=A\cdot B.

In particular,
\mathrm{Var}(S)=n\sigma_{B}=\sigma_{S}^{2}
. Since
B
is continuous on
\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
,
S
has an absolutely continuous density on
\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
.
We write
f_{S}
for this density on
\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
.
Finally, denote
\phi(x):=(2\pi)^{-1/2}e^{-x^{2}/2},x\in\mathbb{R}.

10.3.2
Auxilary lemmas

Before proving Theorem
3.3
we state two Lemmas that we are going to use.

Once again, these lemmas will be doing two things. Find the order of the derivative that appears in (
2.6
) evaluated at order
q(D_{n})
and calculate the leading order of the quantile
q(D_{n})
.

Lemma 10.15
.

There exist absolute constants
0<c<C<\infty
and absolute
n_{0},\sigma_{0}\geq 1
such that
whenever
n\geq n_{0}
and
\sigma_{B}\geq\sigma_{0}
, for all
1\leq D\leq c\,\min\{n,\sigma_{B}\}
it holds

c\sigma_{S}D\leq q(D)\leq C\sigma_{S}D,

where
\sigma_{S}^{2}=\mathrm{Var}(S)
.

The proof of this Lemma is deferred to Section
10.3.4
.

Lemma 10.16
.

Assume
p\geq s=\widetilde{\omega}(\sqrt{p})
. The log-derivative of the density of
S

satisfies for all
D_{n}=o(\max\{n^{1/3},\sigma_{B}^{1/6}\})
:

\left.\frac{d}{ds}\log f_{S}(s)\right|_{s=D_{n}\sigma_{S}}=-\frac{1}{s}\sqrt{\frac{p}{n}}\cdot\big(1+o(1)\big).

The proof of this Lemma is deferred to Section
10.3.5
.

10.3.3
Proof of Theorem
3.3

Once again we prove this by directly applying Theorem
2.7
. To do this we first prove that the prior we chose satisfies the main assumption
2.4
. Specifically, we verify the four conditions.

Condition 1:
Since this model is of the form (
8.1
) (here
Z_{j}=\xi_{j}
,
t=n
and
a(i)=
the row that
X_{i}
belongs to) and the prior
P_{0}
we have assigned satisfies Assumption
8.1
(it’s a product of a standard Gaussian, so centered, with a sparse Bernoulli) using Lemma
8.2
we know that for all multi-indices
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
with
|\alpha|\leq D_{n}
for
D_{n}=\lfloor\log_{2}(p/s)-1\rfloor
it holds that:

\kappa_{\alpha}(X_{i})\geq 0.

Condition 2:
This follows directly from the fact that
X_{i}
are subGaussian.

Condition 3:

The third condition of the Assumption is straightforward since, for
D_{n}^{\prime}=D_{n}\log^{2}n
and using Lemma
10.15

q(D_{n}^{\prime})\geq cD_{n}^{\prime}s\sqrt{n/p}=\omega(\max\{\log^{-D_{n}}n,n^{-C}\}),

for some universal positive constant
C>0
, since
p=n^{c+o(1)}
, for some
c>0
.

Condition 4:
Using Lemma
10.15
we know that the derivative at
q(D_{n})
is of order

\frac{D_{n}}{s}\sqrt{\frac{p}{n}}.
Therefore, Item 4 from Assumption
2.4
is equivalent to the following condition:

\frac{\frac{1}{s}\sqrt{\frac{p}{n}}}{A_{n}}\leq\frac{1}{q(\log^{2}n)\log n}.

Using Lemma
10.15
we know that for
D_{n}^{\prime}=\Theta(\log^{3}n)\leq\mu,
it holds that

q(D_{n}^{\prime})\leq C_{1}D_{n}^{\prime}s\sqrt{\frac{n}{p}}.

Plugging this inequality to the one above we get that Assumption 4 holds for
A_{n}=C_{1}^{\prime}\log^{4}n
, where
C_{1}^{\prime}>0
is an absolute constant.

Now combining the above with (
2.6
) concludes for all
\lambda>0
:

\mathrm{Corr}^{\leq D_{n}}_{P_{0}}\left(\frac{1}{C_{1}^{\prime}\log^{4}n}\cdot\big(\lambda+\frac{\textup{d}}{\textup{d}q}\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ann},\lambda}\bigg|_{q=q(D_{n})}\big)\right)^{2}\leq q(D_{n}^{\prime})\leq C_{1}\frac{D_{n}^{\prime}}{s}\sqrt{\frac{p}{n}}.

where for the last inequality we used again the inequality
q(D_{n}^{\prime})\leq C_{1}D_{n}^{\prime}s\sqrt{\frac{n}{p}}
. Now notice that

\displaystyle\mathrm{MMSE}^{\mathrm{trivial}}_{X}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\|X\|^{2}-(\mathbb{E}\|X\|)^{2}=ns.

Also, from Lemma
10.16
and (
2.6
), for all
\lambda>0
,
\lambda+\frac{\textup{d}}{\textup{d}q}\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ann},\lambda}\bigg|_{q=q(D_{n})}=\Theta(\frac{1}{s}\sqrt{p/n})
. Choosing now
\lambda=\frac{1}{s\log n}\sqrt{p/n}
implies,
\frac{\textup{d}}{\textup{d}q}\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ann},\lambda}\bigg|_{q=q(D_{n})}\geq 0
. Therefore, using Theorem
2.7
,
\mathrm{Corr}(\lambda/A_{n})^{2}\leq 2q(D_{n}^{\prime}),
where
A_{n}=C_{1}^{\prime}\log^{4}n
. Equivalently,

\mathrm{MMSE}^{\leq D_{n}}_{X}\left(\frac{\lambda}{C_{1}^{\prime}\log^{4}n}\right)\geq ns-C_{1}s\log^{4}n\sqrt{\frac{n}{p}}.

Using that
\lambda_{\mathrm{ALG}}=\sqrt{p/(s^{2}n)}
(or equivalently
\Delta_{\mathrm{ALG}}=\sqrt{p/n}
) implies

\mathrm{MMSE}^{\leq D_{n}}_{X}\left(\frac{\lambda_{\mathrm{ALG}}}{C_{1}^{\prime}\log^{5}n}\right)\geq ns-C_{1}s\log^{4}n\sqrt{\frac{n}{p}},

Combining the last two inequalities with the fact that
n=\omega(\log^{8}n)
and
p\geq 1
we have proven

\mathrm{MMSE}^{\leq D_{n}}_{X}\left(\frac{\lambda_{\mathrm{ALG}}}{C_{1}^{\prime}\log^{5}n}\right)\geq(1+o(1))\mathrm{MMSE}^{\mathrm{trivial}}_{X},

completing the proof.

10.3.4
Proof of Lemma
10.15

We prove matching upper and lower bounds on
q(D)
in the range
1\leq D\leq c\min\{n,\sigma_{B}\}
.

Step 1:

By Hoeffding for all
t\geq 0
,

\mathbb{P}(|A|\geq t)\leq 2\exp\Big(-\frac{t^{2}}{2n}\Big).

Conversely, by Lemma
10.1
there exists a universal constant
c_{A}>0
such that for all

n
large enough and all
1\leq D\leq cn
,

\mathbb{P}\big(|A|\geq c_{A}\sqrt{nD}\big)\geq e^{-D},

and similarly there exists a universal constant
C_{A}>0
such that

\mathbb{P}\big(|A|\geq C_{A}\sqrt{nD}\big)\leq e^{-D}.

We absorb any fixed constant in the exponent since we can adjust
c_{A},C_{A}
.

Step 2:

Now we condition on
(b,b^{\prime},g^{\prime})
.

B=\sum_{j=1}^{p}(b_{j}b_{j}^{\prime}g_{j}^{\prime})\,g_{j}

is a linear combination of independent
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
variables, hence

B\big|(b,b^{\prime},g^{\prime})\sim\mathcal{N}(0,V),\qquad V:=\sum_{j=1}^{p}(b_{j}b_{j}^{\prime}g_{j}^{\prime})^{2}=\sum_{j=1}^{p}b_{j}b_{j}^{\prime}(g_{j}^{\prime})^{2}.

In particular, with
Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
independent of
V
, we have the exact representation

B\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\sqrt{V}\,Z.

Also
\mathbb{E}[V]=\sigma_{B}
since
\mathbb{E}[b_{j}b_{j}^{\prime}]=(s/p)^{2}
and
\mathbb{E}[(g_{j}^{\prime})^{2}]=1
.

Step 3:

Let
Y_{j}:=b_{j}b_{j}^{\prime}(g_{j}^{\prime})^{2}\geq 0
, so
V=\sum_{j=1}^{p}Y_{j}
with i.i.d. terms.
Since
(g_{j}^{\prime})^{2}
has MGF
\mathbb{E}e^{\theta(g_{j}^{\prime})^{2}}=(1-2\theta)^{-1/2}
for
\theta<1/2
, we get for
\theta\in(0,1/4)

\mathbb{E}e^{\theta Y_{j}}=(1-(s/p)^{2})+(s/p)^{2}(1-2\theta)^{-1/2}\leq\exp\!\Big((s/p)^{2}\big((1-2\theta)^{-1/2}-1\big)\Big),

which is finite and bounded uniformly in
p
for fixed
\theta<1/2
.
For any
\theta\in(0,1/2)
, by Chernoff inequality,

\mathbb{P}(V\geq 2\sigma_{B})\leq e^{-2\theta\sigma_{B}}\big(\mathbb{E}[e^{\theta Y_{1}}]\big)^{p}.

Taking
\theta=\tfrac{1}{4}
gives

\mathbb{P}(V\geq 2\sigma_{B})\leq e^{-c_{+}\sigma_{B}}

for some universal
c_{+}>0
.
Similarly, for any
\lambda>0
,

\mathbb{P}\Big(V\leq\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{B}\Big)=\mathbb{P}\Big(e^{-\lambda V}\geq e^{-\lambda\sigma_{B}/2}\Big)\leq e^{\lambda\sigma_{B}/2}\big(\mathbb{E}[e^{-\lambda Y_{1}}]\big)^{p},

hence

\mathbb{P}\Big(V\leq\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{B}\Big)\leq\exp\!\left(-\sigma_{B}\Big[1-(1+2\lambda)^{-1/2}-\lambda/2\Big]\right).

Taking
\lambda=\tfrac{1}{2}
gives

\mathbb{P}\Big(V\leq\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{B}\Big)\leq e^{-c_{-}\sigma_{B}}

for some absolute
c_{-}>0
.

Therefore, for some universal
c_{V}>0
,

\mathbb{P}(V\geq 2\sigma_{B})\leq e^{-c_{V}\sigma_{B}},\ \mathbb{P}\Big(V\leq\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{B}\Big)\leq e^{-c_{V}\sigma_{B}},

and so

\mathbb{P}\Big(\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{B}\leq V\leq 2\sigma_{B}\Big)\geq 1-2e^{-c_{V}\sigma_{B}}.

(10.20)

Step 4:

Using
B\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\sqrt{V}\,Z
and (
10.20
), we compare to Gaussian tails. For the upper tail, for any
t>0
,

\mathbb{P}(|B|\geq t)=\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{P}(|Z|\geq t/\sqrt{V}\mid V)\big]\leq\mathbb{P}(V>2\sigma_{B})+\mathbb{P}\big(|Z|\geq t/\sqrt{2\sigma_{B}}\big).

Choose
t:=C_{B}\sqrt{\sigma_{B}D}
. Then
t/\sqrt{2\sigma_{B}}=C_{B}\sqrt{D/2}
, and the Gaussian tail bound

\mathbb{P}(|Z|\geq x)\leq 2e^{-x^{2}/2}
gives

\mathbb{P}(|B|\geq C_{B}\sqrt{\sigma_{B}D})\leq e^{-c_{V}\sigma_{B}}+2\exp\Big(-\frac{C_{B}^{2}}{4}D\Big)\leq e^{-D}

for all
D\leq c\sigma_{B}
after taking
C_{B}
large enough and
c
small enough.
Similarly, for the lower tail

\mathbb{P}(|B|\geq t)\geq\mathbb{P}\big(V\geq\tfrac{1}{2}\sigma_{B}\big)\,\mathbb{P}\big(|Z|\geq t/\sqrt{\sigma_{B}/2}\big)\geq\frac{9}{10}\,\mathbb{P}\big(|Z|\geq\sqrt{2}\,t/\sqrt{\sigma_{B}}\big).

Take
t:=c_{B}\sqrt{\sigma_{B}D}
. Then
\sqrt{2}\,t/\sqrt{\sigma_{B}}=\sqrt{2}\,c_{B}\sqrt{D}
.
By the standard Gaussian lower tail, for all
x\geq 1
,

\mathbb{P}(|Z|\geq x)\geq(c_{0}/x)e^{-x^{2}/2}
.
Thus for
D\geq 1
and
c_{B}
small enough,

\mathbb{P}(|B|\geq c_{B}\sqrt{\sigma_{B}D})\geq\ \frac{c_{1}}{\sqrt{D}}\exp\Big(-c_{2}D\Big)\geq\ e^{-D},

again after adjusting constants. So in the regime
1\leq D\leq c\sigma_{B}
we have constants
0<c_{B}<C_{B}
with

\mathbb{P}\big(|B|\geq c_{B}\sqrt{\sigma_{B}D}\big)\geq e^{-D},\qquad\mathbb{P}\big(|B|\geq C_{B}\sqrt{\sigma_{B}D}\big)\leq e^{-D}.

Step 5:

We are now ready to prove the two bounds. Fix
D
with
1\leq D\leq c\min\{n,\sigma_{B}\}
.

Upper Bound:
Let
a:=C_{A}\sqrt{nD}
and
b:=C_{B}\sqrt{\sigma_{B}D}
. Then

\mathbb{P}(|S|\geq ab)\leq\mathbb{P}(|A|\geq a)+\mathbb{P}(|B|\geq b)\leq e^{-D}+e^{-D}\leq 2e^{-D},

so (absorbing the factor
2
into constants) we get

q(D)\leq ab=C_{A}C_{B}\sqrt{n\sigma_{B}}D=C\sigma_{S}D.

Lower Bound:
For a lower bound on
q(D)
,
let
a:=c_{A}\sqrt{nD}
and
b:=c_{B}\sqrt{\sigma_{B}D}
. By independence,

\mathbb{P}(|S|\geq ab)\geq\mathbb{P}(|A|\geq a)\,\mathbb{P}(|B|\geq b)\geq e^{-D}\cdot e^{-D}=e^{-2D}.

Now replace
D
by
D/2
in the construction (which stays within the same regime up to constants) to obtain

\mathbb{P}\big(|S|\geq c\sqrt{n\sigma_{B}}D\big)\geq e^{-D}.

Hence,
q(D)\geq c\sqrt{n\sigma_{B}}\,D=c\sigma_{S}D.
Combining the two bounds yields
c\,\sigma_{S}D\leq q(D)\leq C\,\sigma_{S}D
for all

1\leq D\leq c\min\{n,\sigma_{B}\}
, as claimed.

10.3.5
Proof of Lemma
10.16

The proof of this Lemma is quite technical. We break it down by stating and proving some Lemmas that lead us to the result.

We first prove a local central limit theorem (CLT) for the
\chi^{2}
distribution.

Lemma 10.17

(Density CLT for
\chi^{2}_{u}
at the
\sqrt{u}
scale)
.

Let
u\in\mathbb{N}
such that
u=\omega(1)
and consider
g_{u}
to be the density of a
\chi^{2}_{u}
distribution. Assume
D_{n}=o(u^{1/6})
. Then, uniformly for
|x|\leq D_{n}
, as
n\to\infty

\displaystyle\sup_{|x|\leq D_{n}}\left|\sqrt{2u}g_{u}\big(u+x\sqrt{2u}\big)-\phi(x)\right|

\displaystyle\rightarrow 0,

(10.21)

\displaystyle\sup_{|x|\leq D_{n}}\left|(2u)g_{u}^{\prime}\big(u+x\sqrt{2u}\big)-\phi^{\prime}(x)\right|

\displaystyle\rightarrow 0.

(10.22)

Proof.

Write
u=2\nu
so
\nu=u/2=\omega(1)
. The
\chi^{2}_{u}
density is, for
t>0
,

g_{u}(t)=\frac{1}{2^{\nu}\Gamma(\nu)}t^{\nu-1}e^{-t/2}.

Fix
|x|\leq D_{n}
and set

t=u+x\sqrt{2u}=2\nu+2x\sqrt{\nu}\ =2\nu\Big(1+\frac{x}{\sqrt{\nu}}\Big).

Consider the log-density

\log g_{u}(t)=-(\nu\log 2+\log\Gamma(\nu))+(\nu-1)\log t-\frac{t}{2}.

Using Stirling’s formula,

\Gamma(\nu)=\sqrt{2\pi}\,\nu^{\nu-\frac{1}{2}}e^{-\nu}\big(1+o(1)\big),\ \nu\to\infty,

which implies

\log\Gamma(\nu)=\Big(\nu-\tfrac{1}{2}\Big)\log\nu-\nu+\tfrac{1}{2}\log(2\pi)+o(1),

uniformly as
\nu\to\infty
. Also,

\log t=\log(2\nu)+\log\Big(1+\frac{x}{\sqrt{\nu}}\Big)

and uniformly for
|x|\leq D_{n}
,

\log\Big(1+\frac{x}{\sqrt{\nu}}\Big)=\frac{x}{\sqrt{\nu}}-\frac{x^{2}}{2\nu}+O\Big(\frac{|x|^{3}}{\nu^{3/2}}\Big).

Since
D_{n}=o(u^{1/6})
and
u=2\nu
, we have

\sup_{|x|\leq D_{n}}\frac{|x|^{3}}{\nu^{3/2}}=o\Big(\frac{1}{\nu}\Big)\qquad\text{and in particular}\qquad\sup_{|x|\leq D_{n}}\frac{|x|^{3}}{\sqrt{\nu}}\to 0.

Now plug into
\log g_{u}(t)
and note that
\frac{t}{2}=\nu+x\sqrt{\nu}.
, to get

\displaystyle\log g_{u}(t)

\displaystyle=-\nu\log 2-\Big[\Big(\nu-\tfrac{1}{2}\Big)\log\nu-\nu+\tfrac{1}{2}\log(2\pi)\Big]

\displaystyle\quad+(\nu-1)\Big[\log(2\nu)+\frac{x}{\sqrt{\nu}}-\frac{x^{2}}{2\nu}+O\Big(\frac{|x|^{3}}{\nu^{3/2}}\Big)\Big]-\nu-x\sqrt{\nu}+o(1)

\displaystyle=-\log 2-\tfrac{1}{2}\log\nu-\tfrac{1}{2}\log(2\pi)+(\nu-1)\frac{x}{\sqrt{\nu}}-x\sqrt{\nu}-(\nu-1)\frac{x^{2}}{2\nu}

\displaystyle\quad+O\Big(\frac{|x|^{3}}{\sqrt{\nu}}\Big)+O\Big(\frac{1+x^{2}}{\nu}\Big)+o(1)

\displaystyle=-\log 2-\tfrac{1}{2}\log\nu-\tfrac{1}{2}\log(2\pi)-\frac{x^{2}}{2}+r_{u}(x),

where

r_{u}(x)=-\frac{x}{\sqrt{\nu}}+O\Big(\frac{1+x^{2}}{\nu}\Big)+O\Big(\frac{|x|^{3}}{\sqrt{\nu}}\Big)+o(1).

Now add the normalizing factor
\sqrt{2u}=\sqrt{4\nu}
:

\displaystyle\log\Big(\sqrt{2u}\,g_{u}(t)\Big)

\displaystyle=\log g_{u}(t)+\tfrac{1}{2}\log(4\nu)

\displaystyle=\Big(-\log 2-\tfrac{1}{2}\log\nu-\tfrac{1}{2}\log(2\pi)\Big)+\Big(\log 2+\tfrac{1}{2}\log\nu\Big)-\frac{x^{2}}{2}+r_{u}(x)

\displaystyle=-\tfrac{1}{2}\log(2\pi)-\frac{x^{2}}{2}+r_{u}(x)=\log\phi(x)+r_{u}(x).

Therefore,

\sqrt{2u}\,g_{u}(u+x\sqrt{2u})=\phi(x)e^{r_{u}(x)}.
Thus,

\big|\sqrt{2u}\,g_{u}(u+x\sqrt{2u})-\phi(x)\big|=\phi(x)\,|e^{r_{u}(x)}-1|\leq\phi(0)\,|e^{r_{u}(x)}-1|,

and taking
\sup_{|x|\leq D_{n}}
proves the first claim.

For the derivative statement, define

h_{u}(x):=\sqrt{2u}\,g_{u}(u+x\sqrt{2u})=\phi(x)e^{r_{u}(x)}.

We differentiate
g_{u}
to get

g_{u}^{\prime}(t)=g_{u}(t)\left(\frac{\nu-1}{t}-\frac{1}{2}\right).

By the chain rule,

h_{u}^{\prime}(x)=(2u)\,g_{u}^{\prime}(u+x\sqrt{2u}).
Hence

h_{u}^{\prime}(x)=h_{u}(x)\,A_{u}(x),\qquad A_{u}(x):=\sqrt{2u}\left(\frac{\nu-1}{t}-\frac{1}{2}\right).

Using
u=2\nu
and
t=2\nu(1+y)
,

\displaystyle A_{u}(x)=2\sqrt{\nu}\left(\frac{\nu-1}{2\nu(1+y)}-\frac{1}{2}\right)=\sqrt{\nu}\left(\frac{1-1/\nu}{1+y}-1\right)=-\frac{x+\nu^{-1/2}}{1+x/\sqrt{\nu}}.

Therefore

A_{u}(x)+x=\frac{x^{2}-1}{\sqrt{\nu}\,(1+x/\sqrt{\nu})}.

Since
D_{n}/\sqrt{\nu}\to 0
, for all sufficiently large
\nu
we have

1+x/\nu\geq\frac{1}{2},\ \text{for all}\ |x|\leq D_{n},

and hence

\sup_{|x|\leq D_{n}}|A_{u}(x)+x|\leq\frac{2(D_{n}^{2}+1)}{\sqrt{\nu}}\to 0.

Now recall that
h_{u}(x)=\phi(x)e^{r_{u}(x)}
and
\phi^{\prime}(x)=-x\phi(x)
. Thus

\displaystyle h_{u}^{\prime}(x)-\phi^{\prime}(x)

\displaystyle=h_{u}(x)A_{u}(x)+x\phi(x)

\displaystyle=\phi(x)e^{r_{u}(x)}A_{u}(x)+x\phi(x).

Add and subtract
x\phi(x)e^{r_{u}(x)}
to get

h_{u}^{\prime}(x)-\phi^{\prime}(x)=\phi(x)e^{r_{u}(x)}(A_{u}(x)+x)+x\phi(x)\big(1-e^{r_{u}(x)}\big).

Hence

|h_{u}^{\prime}(x)-\phi^{\prime}(x)|\leq\phi(x)e^{|r_{u}(x)|}\,|A_{u}(x)+x|+|x|\phi(x)\,|1-e^{r_{u}(x)}|.

Taking the supremum over
|x|\leq D_{n}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\sup_{|x|\leq D_{n}}|h_{u}^{\prime}(x)-\phi^{\prime}(x)|

\displaystyle\leq\Big(\sup_{|x|\leq D_{n}}\phi(x)\Big)\Big(\sup_{|x|\leq D_{n}}e^{|r_{u}(x)|}\Big)\Big(\sup_{|x|\leq D_{n}}|A_{u}(x)+x|\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad+\Big(\sup_{|x|\leq D_{n}}|x|\phi(x)\Big)\Big(\sup_{|x|\leq D_{n}}|1-e^{r_{u}(x)}|\Big).

Now

\sup_{|x|\leq D_{n}}\phi(x)\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}.

Also,

|x|\phi(x)=\frac{|x|}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-x^{2}/2}.

For
x\geq 0
, the function
x\mapsto xe^{-x^{2}/2}
has derivative

\frac{d}{dx}\big(xe^{-x^{2}/2}\big)=e^{-x^{2}/2}(1-x^{2}),

so its maximum is attained at
x=1
. Hence

\sup_{|x|\leq D_{n}}|x|\phi(x)\leq\frac{e^{-1/2}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}.

Since
\sup_{|x|\leq D_{n}}|r_{u}(x)|\to 0
, we have

\sup_{|x|\leq D_{n}}e^{|r_{u}(x)|}\to 1\qquad\text{and}\qquad\sup_{|x|\leq D_{n}}|1-e^{r_{u}(x)}|\to 0.

Moreover, we already proved that

\sup_{|x|\leq D_{n}}|A_{u}(x)+x|\to 0.

Combining these bounds, we conclude that

\sup_{|x|\leq D_{n}}|h_{u}^{\prime}(x)-\phi^{\prime}(x)|\to 0.

Since
h_{u}^{\prime}(x)=(2u)\,g_{u}^{\prime}(u+x\sqrt{2u})
, this proves

\sup_{|x|\leq D_{n}}\left|(2u)\,g_{u}^{\prime}(u+x\sqrt{2u})-\phi^{\prime}(x)\right|\to 0.

This proves (
10.22
).
∎

Let
G,H\sim N(0,1)
independent and
Y:=GH
. For an integer
u\geq 1
, let
Y_{1},Y_{2},\dots
be i.i.d. copies of
Y
and define

B_{u}:=\sum_{k=1}^{u}Y_{k}.

(10.23)

Let
f_{u}
be the density of
B_{u}
for
u\geq 1
. We now prove a Local CLT for
B_{u}
. Recall that by Lemma
9.5
,
B_{u}=(X_{u}-Y_{u})/2
with
X_{u},Y_{u}
i.i.d.
\chi^{2}_{u}
. We state the Lemma here and we defer it’s proof to Section
10.4
.

Lemma 10.18

(Density CLT for
f_{u}
and
f_{u}^{\prime}
)
.

Assume
D_{n}=o(u^{1/6})
. Uniformly for
|x|\leq D_{n}
,

\displaystyle\sup_{|x|\leq D_{n}}\left|\sqrt{u}f_{u}(x\sqrt{u})-\phi(x)\right|

\displaystyle\rightarrow 0,

(10.24)

\displaystyle\sup_{|x|\leq D_{n}}\left|uf_{u}^{\prime}(x\sqrt{u})-\phi^{\prime}(x)\right|

\displaystyle\rightarrow 0.

(10.25)

We also make use on the following bounds for the density and the derivative of the density of
B_{u}
. The proof of this is also deferred to Section
10.4
.

Lemma 10.19

(Uniform bounds for
f_{u}
and
f_{u}^{\prime}
away from zero)
.

For every
u\geq 1
, the density
f_{u}
of
B_{u}
is differentiable on
\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
.
Moreover, there exists an absolute constant
C>0
such that for all
u\geq 1
and all
x\neq 0
,

f_{u}(x)\leq\frac{C}{|x|},\qquad|f_{u}^{\prime}(x)|\leq\frac{C}{x^{2}}.

Now we are ready to move on to the density of the random variable
B
that appears in the overlap of the sparse clustering model.

Lemma 10.20

(Exact density for
B
conditioning)
.

Let
f_{B}
denote the density of
B
on
\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
. Then for all
x\neq 0
,

f_{B}(x)=\sum_{u=1}^{p}\mathbb{P}(U=u)f_{u}(x),\qquad f_{B}^{\prime}(x)=\sum_{u=1}^{p}\mathbb{P}(U=u)f_{u}^{\prime}(x).

Proof.

By definition,
B\mid(U=u)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}B_{u}
.
For any Borel set
E\subset\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
,

\mathbb{P}(B\in E)=\sum_{u=1}^{p}\mathbb{P}(U=u)\mathbb{P}(B_{u}\in E),

hence by writing the equation with integrals and by the uniqueness of the PDF it should hold that
f_{B}(x)=\sum_{u=1}^{p}\mathbb{P}(U=u)f_{u}(x)
for
x\neq 0
.
Since the sum is finite we differentiate with respect to
x\neq 0
term by term to get the formula for
f_{B}^{\prime}(x)
.
∎

Lemma 10.21

(Density CLT for
B
and
f_{B}^{\prime}
on the
\sqrt{\sigma_{B}}
scale)
.

Fix
0<\varepsilon<1
. Assume
D_{n}=o(\sigma_{B}^{1/6})
. Uniformly for
\varepsilon\leq|x|\leq D_{n}
,

\displaystyle\sup_{\varepsilon\leq|x|\leq D_{n}}\left|\sqrt{\sigma_{B}}f_{B}(x\sqrt{\sigma_{B}})-\phi(x)\right|

\displaystyle\rightarrow 0,

(10.26)

\displaystyle\sup_{\varepsilon\leq|x|\leq D_{n}}\left|\sigma_{B}f_{B}^{\prime}(x\sqrt{\sigma_{B}})-\phi^{\prime}(x)\right|

\displaystyle\rightarrow 0.

(10.27)

Proof.

Let
L=\log\sigma_{B}
and
\mathcal{I}:=\{u:\ |u-\sigma_{B}|\leq\sqrt{\sigma_{B}}L\}
.
We split the mixture from Lemma
10.20
into
u\in\mathcal{I}
and
u\notin\mathcal{I}
.

Step 1:

By Chernoff inequality for the Binomial distribution,
\mathbb{P}(U\notin\mathcal{I})\leq 2e^{-cL^{2}}=o(1)
.
By Lemma
10.19
, for every
u\geq 1
and every
z\neq 0
,

f_{u}(z)\leq\frac{C}{|z|},\qquad|f_{u}^{\prime}(z)|\leq\frac{C}{z^{2}}.

Therefore for
\varepsilon\leq|x|\leq D_{n}
,

|f_{u}(x\sqrt{\sigma_{B}})|\leq\frac{C}{\varepsilon\sqrt{\sigma_{B}}},\qquad|f_{u}^{\prime}(x\sqrt{\sigma_{B}})|\leq\frac{C}{\varepsilon^{2}\sigma_{B}}.

Define

C_{\varepsilon}:=C/\varepsilon^{2}.

Then using Lemma
10.20
we obtain, uniformly over
\varepsilon\leq|x|\leq D_{n}
,

\sqrt{\sigma_{B}}\cdot\sum_{u\notin\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{P}(U=u)\,|f_{u}(x\sqrt{\sigma_{B}})|\leq C_{\varepsilon}\,\mathbb{P}(U\notin\mathcal{I})=o(1),

and similarly

\sigma_{B}\cdot\sum_{u\notin\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{P}(U=u)\,|f_{u}^{\prime}(x\sqrt{\sigma_{B}})|\leq C_{\varepsilon}\,\mathbb{P}(U\notin\mathcal{I})=o(1).

Step 2:

Fix
\varepsilon\leq|x|\leq D_{n}
and
u\in\mathcal{I}
. Write

z_{u}(x):=x\sqrt{\sigma_{B}/u}.

Since
u=\sigma_{B}+O(\sqrt{\sigma_{B}}L)
, we have
z_{u}(x)=x+O(|x|L/\sqrt{\sigma_{B}})
, uniformly over
\varepsilon\leq|x|\leq D_{n}
.
Because
|x|\leq D_{n}
and
D_{n}L/\sqrt{\sigma_{B}}=o(1)
, we have
|z_{u}(x)|\leq 2D_{n}
for all large
(n,p)
.
Also, since
|x|\geq\varepsilon
, for all large
(n,p)
we have
|z_{u}(x)|\geq\varepsilon/2
.
Apply Lemma
10.18
uniformly for
\varepsilon/2\leq|z|\leq 2D_{n}
:

f_{u}(x\sqrt{\sigma_{B}})=f_{u}(z_{u}(x)\sqrt{u})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{u}}\phi(z_{u}(x))+o\Big(\frac{1}{\sqrt{u}}\Big),

uniformly over
\varepsilon\leq|x|\leq D_{n}
and
u\in\mathcal{I}
. Next, since
\sup_{\varepsilon\leq|x|\leq D_{n}}\sup_{u\in\mathcal{I}}|z_{u}(x)-x|=o(1)
and
\phi
is
O(1)
Lipschitz, we have

\sup_{\varepsilon\leq|x|\leq D_{n}}\sup_{u\in\mathcal{I}}|\phi(z_{u}(x))-\phi(x)|=o(1).

Also, uniformly for
u\in\mathcal{I}
, we have
1/\sqrt{u}=1/\sqrt{\sigma_{B}}+O(L/\sigma_{B})
. Combining these two we get

f_{u}(x\sqrt{\sigma_{B}})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\sigma_{B}}}\phi(x)+o\Big(\frac{1}{\sqrt{\sigma_{B}}}\Big),

uniformly over
u\in\mathcal{I}
and
\varepsilon\leq|x|\leq D_{n}
.
Averaging with weights
\mathbb{P}(U=u)
over
u\in\mathcal{I}
and using Step 1 (note that almost all the mass of
U
lies inside
\mathcal{I}
) yields (
10.26
).
The derivative statement (
10.27
) is identical, using Lemma
10.18
and specifically (
10.25
):

f_{u}^{\prime}(x\sqrt{\sigma_{B}})=f_{u}^{\prime}(z_{u}(x)\sqrt{u})=\frac{1}{u}\phi^{\prime}(z_{u}(x))+o\Big(\frac{1}{u}\Big),

then
1/u=1/\sigma_{B}+O(L/\sigma_{B}^{3/2})
and
\phi^{\prime}(z_{u}(x))=\phi^{\prime}(x)+o(1)
again by using the fact that
\phi^{\prime}
is
O(1)
Lipschitz.
∎

Lemma 10.22

(Local CLT for
A
and tail)
.

Let
T:=\sqrt{D_{n}}\,\log(n\vee p)
. Uniformly for integers
a\equiv n\pmod{2}
with
|a|\leq\sqrt{n}T
,

\mathbb{P}(A=a)=\frac{2}{\sqrt{n}}\phi\Big(\frac{a}{\sqrt{n}}\Big)\Big(1+o(1)\Big).

Also
\mathbb{P}(|A|\geq\sqrt{n}T)\leq 2e^{-T^{2}/2}
.

Proof.

The first part is a standard application of Lemma
10.1
. For the tail, Hoeffding’s inequality for sums of i.i.d.
\pm 1
gives

\mathbb{P}(|A|\geq\sqrt{n}T)\leq 2e^{-T^{2}/2}.

∎

Lemma 10.23

(Mixture density and derivative)
.

Let
f_{B}
be the density of
B
. Then for every
t\neq 0
,

f_{S}(t)=\sum_{a\in\{-n,-n+2,\dots,n\}\setminus\{0\}}\mathbb{P}(A=a)\frac{1}{|a|}f_{B}(t/a),

and
f_{S}
is differentiable for
t\neq 0
with

f_{S}^{\prime}(t)=\sum_{a\neq 0}\mathbb{P}(A=a)\frac{1}{a|a|}f_{B}^{\prime}(t/a).

Proof.

Since
S=A\cdot B
, we prove this by conditioning on
A
. By the law of total probability and independence of
A
and
B
, for any
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

\mathbb{P}(S\leq x)=\sum_{a\in\{-n,-n+2,\dots,n\}}\mathbb{P}(A=a)\,\mathbb{P}(aB\leq x).

The sum is finite, so we may differentiate term by term at any
t\neq 0
to get

f_{S}(t)=\frac{d}{dt}\,\mathbb{P}(S\leq t)=\sum_{a\neq 0}\mathbb{P}(A=a)\,\frac{1}{|a|}\,f_{B}(t/a),

which is the claimed formula for
f_{S}(t)
for
t\neq 0
.
For
f_{S}^{\prime}
, the result follow directly by differentiating once more term by term with respect to
t\neq 0
.
∎

Let
G,H\sim N(0,1)
independent and set
W:=GH
.
Its density is

f_{\infty}(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{|y|}\phi(y)\phi(x/y)dy,\qquad x\neq 0.

(10.28)

Lemma 10.24

(Density Local Limit Theorem (LLT) for
S
at
t=D_{n}\sigma_{S}
)
.

Assume
D_{n}=o(\sigma_{B}^{1/6})
. Set
t:=D_{n}\sigma_{S}=D_{n}\sqrt{n\sigma_{B}}
, where the random variables
B,S
where defined in
10.3.1
. Then,

\sigma_{S}f_{S}(t)=f_{\infty}(D_{n})\big(1+o(1)\big),\qquad\sigma_{S}^{2}f_{S}^{\prime}(t)=f_{\infty}^{\prime}(D_{n})\big(1+o(1)\big).

(10.29)

Proof.

Fix
t=D_{n}\sigma_{S}=D_{n}\sqrt{n\sigma_{B}}
and let
T=\sqrt{D_{n}}\log(n\vee p)
as in Lemma
10.22
.
We will repeatedly use the elementary lower bound (valid for all
x\geq 1
)

f_{\infty}(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{|u|}\phi(u)\phi(x/u)\,du\ \geq\ \int_{\sqrt{x}}^{\sqrt{x}+1}\frac{1}{u}\phi(u)\phi(x/u)\,du\geq\frac{c}{\sqrt{x}}\,e^{-Cx},

(10.30)

for universal constants
c,C>0
(since on
[\sqrt{x},\sqrt{x}+1]
we have
u=\Theta(\sqrt{x})
,

x/u=\sqrt{x}+O(1)
, hence
\phi(u)\phi(x/u)=\Theta(e^{-x})
).

Finally, note that for every fixed
x\neq 0
, one may differentiate (
10.28
) under the integral sign
(because as
u\to 0
the factor
\phi(x/u)
decays super-exponentially, and as
|u|\to\infty
the factor
\phi(u)
decays),
obtaining for
x\neq 0
,

f_{\infty}^{\prime}(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{u|u|}\phi(u)\phi^{\prime}(x/u)\,du.

(10.31)

Furthermore, by integrating (
10.31
) over
u\in[\sqrt{x},\sqrt{x}+1]
noting
\phi^{\prime}(x/u)=\Theta(-\sqrt{x}\,\phi(\sqrt{x}))
and then using once more (
10.30
))

f_{\infty}^{\prime}(x)\geq C_{1}\int_{\sqrt{x}}^{\sqrt{x}+1}\frac{1}{u}\phi(u)\phi(x/u)\,du\geq\frac{c^{\prime}}{\sqrt{x}}\,e^{-Cx},

(10.32)

for some universal constants
C_{1},c^{\prime}>0
.

Step 1:

First we want to show that the tail contribution
|a|>\sqrt{n}\,T
is very small. To do this, we will apply Lemma
10.21

at
t/a
. First, notice that by Lemma
10.22
,

\mathbb{P}(|A|\geq\sqrt{n}\,T)\leq 2e^{-T^{2}/2}=2\exp\Big(-\frac{1}{2}D_{n}\log^{2}(n\vee p)\Big).

For
|a|>\sqrt{n}\,T
, define

x_{a}:=\frac{t/a}{\sqrt{\sigma_{B}}}=\frac{D_{n}\sqrt{n\sigma_{B}}/a}{\sqrt{\sigma_{B}}}=\frac{D_{n}}{a/\sqrt{n}},\qquad\text{so}\qquad|x_{a}|\leq\frac{D_{n}}{T}=\frac{\sqrt{D_{n}}}{\log(n\vee p)}.

In particular,
|x_{a}|\leq D_{n}
, so Lemma
10.21
applies uniformly over the choice of
x_{\alpha}
and yields

|f_{B}(t/a)|\leq\frac{C}{\sqrt{\sigma_{B}}},\qquad|f_{B}^{\prime}(t/a)|\leq\frac{C}{\sigma_{B}}\,|x_{a}|\leq\frac{C}{\sigma_{B}}\cdot\frac{D_{n}}{T},

for an absolute constant
C>0
, using that
|\phi(x)|\leq(2\pi)^{-1/2}
and
|\phi^{\prime}(x)|=|x|\phi(x)\leq C|x|
.
Therefore, using Lemma
10.23
and
|a|>\sqrt{n}\,T\Rightarrow|a|^{-1}\leq(\sqrt{n}\,T)^{-1}
,

\displaystyle\sigma_{S}\sum_{|a|>\sqrt{n}\,T}\mathbb{P}(A=a)\frac{1}{|a|}\,|f_{B}(t/a)|

\displaystyle\leq\sigma_{S}\cdot\mathbb{P}(|A|>\sqrt{n}\,T)\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}\,T}\cdot\frac{C}{\sqrt{\sigma_{B}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{C}{T}\,\mathbb{P}(|A|>\sqrt{n}\,T)=o\Big(\frac{e^{-D_{n}}}{\sqrt{D_{n}}}\Big)=o\big(f_{\infty}(D_{n})\big),

(10.33)

where we used
\sigma_{S}=\sqrt{n\sigma_{B}}
and (
10.30
).
Similarly, since
|a|>\sqrt{n}\,T\Rightarrow|a|^{-2}\leq(nT^{2})^{-1}
,

\displaystyle\sigma_{S}^{2}\sum_{|a|>\sqrt{n}\,T}\mathbb{P}(A=a)\frac{1}{|a|^{2}}\,|f_{B}^{\prime}(t/a)|

\displaystyle\leq\sigma_{S}^{2}\cdot\mathbb{P}(|A|>\sqrt{n}\,T)\cdot\frac{1}{nT^{2}}\cdot\frac{C}{\sigma_{B}}\cdot\frac{D_{n}}{T}

\displaystyle=C\,\frac{D_{n}}{T^{3}}\,\mathbb{P}(|A|>\sqrt{n}\,T)=o\Big(\frac{e^{-D_{n}}}{\sqrt{D_{n}}}\Big)=o\big(|f_{\infty}^{\prime}(D_{n})|\big),

(10.34)

where the last step uses (
10.32
).
Thus, from now on we restrict the sums to
|a|\leq\sqrt{n}\,T
.

Step 2:

Write
y:=a/\sqrt{n}
so
|y|\leq T
and
|a|=\sqrt{n}|y|
.
Then

\frac{t}{a}=\frac{D_{n}\sqrt{n\sigma_{B}}}{\sqrt{n}y}=\frac{D_{n}\sqrt{\sigma_{B}}}{y}.

Lemma
10.22
gives
\mathbb{P}(A=a)=(2/\sqrt{n})\phi(y)(1+o(1))
uniformly on
|y|\leq T
. Let

\mathcal{A}:=\Big\{\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}:\tfrac{1}{2}\sqrt{nD_{n}}\leq|\alpha|\leq 2\sqrt{nD_{n}}\Big\}.

We will further truncate our sum to
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}
. We claim that the contribution of
\alpha\notin\mathcal{A}
to
\sigma_{S}f_{S}(t)
is still
o(f_{\infty}(D_{n}))
,
even after the truncation
|\alpha|\leq\sqrt{n}T
. Indeed, using again the notation
y=a/\sqrt{n},
then

g_{D_{n}}(y):=\frac{1}{|y|}\phi(y)\phi(D_{n}/y).

For
|y|\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\sqrt{D_{n}}
, we have
(D_{n}/y)^{2}\geq 4D_{n}
, hence

g_{D_{n}}(y)\leq\frac{C}{|y|}\exp\Big(-\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{D_{n}^{2}}{y^{2}}\Big)\leq\frac{C}{|y|}e^{-2D_{n}},

(10.35)

and integrating over
0<|y|\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\sqrt{D_{n}}
gives

\int_{0<|y|\leq\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{D_{n}}}g_{D_{n}}(y)\,dy\leq Ce^{-2D_{n}}\log D_{n}
.
Similarly, for
|y|\geq 2\sqrt{D_{n}}
we have
y^{2}\geq 4D_{n}
so

g_{D_{n}}(y)\leq Ce^{-2D_{n}}/|y|
, hence

\int_{|y|\geq 2\sqrt{D_{n}}}g_{D_{n}}(y)\,dy\leq Ce^{-2D_{n}}
.
Comparing with (
10.30
), both tails are
o(f_{\infty}(D_{n}))
.
Fix
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}
and the corresponding point
y=\alpha/\sqrt{n}
.
By Lemma
10.22
(using that
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\{|\alpha|\leq\sqrt{n}T\}
for all large
n
),

\mathbb{P}(A=a)=\frac{2}{\sqrt{n}}\phi(y)\big(1+o(1)\big),

(10.36)

Next, note that for
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}
we have

\frac{t}{a}=\frac{D_{n}\sqrt{n\sigma_{B}}}{\sqrt{n}\,y}=\frac{D_{n}\sqrt{\sigma_{B}}}{y},\qquad\text{so}\qquad\frac{t/a}{\sqrt{\sigma_{B}}}=\frac{D_{n}}{y}\in\Big[\tfrac{1}{2}\sqrt{D_{n}},\,2\sqrt{D_{n}}\Big].

In particular, this ratio is at most
2\sqrt{D_{n}}=o(\sigma_{B}^{1/6})

since
D_{n}=o(\sigma_{B}^{1/6})
. Therefore, we may apply Lemma
10.21
and similarly for the derivative to get:

f_{B}\Big(\frac{D_{n}\sqrt{\sigma_{B}}}{y}\Big)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\sigma_{B}}}\phi(D_{n}/y)\big(1+o(1)\big),\qquad f_{B}^{\prime}\Big(\frac{D_{n}\sqrt{\sigma_{B}}}{y}\Big)=\frac{1}{\sigma_{B}}\phi^{\prime}(D_{n}/y)\big(1+o(1)\big).

(10.37)

Plugging (
10.36
) and (
10.37
) into Lemma
10.23
, and using
|a|=\sqrt{n}\,|y|
, gives

f_{S}(t)=\frac{(1+o(1))}{\sigma_{S}}\cdot\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0<|a|\leq\sqrt{n}T\\
a\in\mathcal{A}\end{subarray}}\Big(\frac{2}{\sqrt{n}}\Big)\frac{1}{|y|}\phi(y)\phi(D_{n}/y).

The sum is a Riemann sum for (
10.28
) evaluated at
x=D_{n}
, giving (
10.29
).

Step 3:
For the derivative, we repeat this process using Lemma
10.23
for
f_{S}^{\prime}
.

We first show that the contribution of
\alpha\notin\mathcal{A}
is negligible also for the derivative.
For
y=a/\sqrt{n}
,
0<|y|\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\sqrt{D_{n}}
, using the bound in (
10.35
),

\frac{D_{n}}{|y|^{2}}g_{D_{n}}(y)\leq C\frac{D_{n}}{|y|^{3}}\exp\!\Big(-\frac{D_{n}^{2}}{2y^{2}}\Big).

Therefore, by the change of variables
z=D_{n}/|y|
,

\int_{0<|y|\leq\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{D_{n}}}\frac{D_{n}}{|y|^{2}}g_{D_{n}}(y)\,dy\leq C\int_{2\sqrt{D_{n}}}^{\infty}\frac{z}{D_{n}}\phi(z)\,dz\leq C\frac{e^{-2D_{n}}}{D_{n}}.

Similarly, for
|y|\geq 2\sqrt{D_{n}}
we have

\frac{D_{n}}{|y|^{2}}g_{D_{n}}(y)\leq C\frac{D_{n}}{|y|^{3}}e^{-y^{2}/2},

and thus

\int_{|y|\geq 2\sqrt{D_{n}}}\frac{D_{n}}{|y|^{2}}g_{D_{n}}(y)\,dy\leq CD_{n}\int_{|y|\geq 2\sqrt{D_{n}}}\frac{e^{-y^{2}/2}}{|y|^{3}}\,dy\leq C\frac{e^{-2D_{n}}}{D_{n}}.

Comparing with (
10.32
), both tails are
o(|f_{\infty}^{\prime}(D_{n})|)
.

Now, for
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}
, by (
10.36
) and (
10.37
),

\mathbb{P}(A=a)=\frac{2}{\sqrt{n}}\phi(y)\big(1+o(1)\big),\qquad f_{B}^{\prime}\Big(\frac{D_{n}\sqrt{\sigma_{B}}}{y}\Big)=\frac{1}{\sigma_{B}}\phi^{\prime}(D_{n}/y)\big(1+o(1)\big),

uniformly over
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}
.
Plugging this into Lemma
10.23
for
f_{S}^{\prime}
and using
y=\alpha/\sqrt{n}

gives

f_{S}^{\prime}(t)=\frac{(1+o(1))}{\sigma_{S}^{2}}\cdot\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0<|a|\leq\sqrt{n}T\\
a\in\mathcal{A}\end{subarray}}\Big(\frac{2}{\sqrt{n}}\Big)\frac{1}{y|y|}\phi(y)\phi^{\prime}(D_{n}/y).

The sum is a Riemann sum for (
10.31
) evaluated at
x=D_{n}
, and by the tail bounds just proved the contribution of
y\notin[\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{D_{n}},2\sqrt{D_{n}}]
is negligible. Therefore,

f_{S}^{\prime}(t)=\frac{(1+o(1))}{\sigma_{S}^{2}}f_{\infty}^{\prime}(D_{n}),

which is the desired conclusion.

∎

We are now are ready to prove our main Lemma.

Proof of Lemma
10.16
.

By Lemma
10.24
,

f_{S}(D_{n}\sigma_{S})=\frac{1}{\sigma_{S}}f_{\infty}(D_{n})(1+o(1)),\qquad f_{S}^{\prime}(D_{n}\sigma_{S})=\frac{1}{\sigma_{S}^{2}}f_{\infty}^{\prime}(D_{n})(1+o(1)).

Therefore

\frac{d}{ds}\log f_{S}(s)\Big|_{s=D_{n}\sigma_{S}}=\frac{f_{S}^{\prime}(D_{n}\sigma_{S})}{f_{S}(D_{n}\sigma_{S})}=\frac{1}{\sigma_{S}}\frac{f_{\infty}^{\prime}(D_{n})}{f_{\infty}(D_{n})}+o\Big(\frac{1}{\sigma_{S}}\Big)=\frac{1}{\sigma_{S}}(\log f_{\infty})^{\prime}(D_{n})+o\Big(\frac{1}{\sigma_{S}}\Big).

Finally, by (
9.10
), we have

f_{\infty}(x)=\frac{1}{\pi}K_{0}(|x|),

and for
x>0
,

(\log f_{\infty})^{\prime}(x)=-\frac{K_{1}(x)}{K_{0}(x)}.

Moreover, by Lemma
9.2
,

\frac{K_{1}(x)}{K_{0}(x)}\to 1\qquad\text{as }x\to\infty.

Hence

(\log f_{\infty})^{\prime}(x)\to-1\ \text{as }\ x\to\infty,

so after rescaling by
\sigma_{S}
, the leading order is
-1/\sigma_{S}
.
∎

10.4
Auxiliary lemmas

In this section we present the proofs of Lemmas
9.5
,
10.1
,
10.18
and
10.19
.

Proof of Lemma
9.5
.

Set

U=\frac{G+H}{\sqrt{2}},\qquad V=\frac{G-H}{\sqrt{2}}.

Then
U,V
are independent
N(0,I_{d})
vectors, and

4\langle G,H\rangle=\|G+H\|^{2}-\|G-H\|^{2}=2\|U\|^{2}-2\|V\|^{2}.

Thus
W=(X-Y)/2
, where
X=\|U\|^{2}
and
Y=\|V\|^{2}
are i.i.d.
\chi^{2}_{d}
.

Next, for a single coordinate product
Z=G_{1}H_{1}
,

\mathbb{E}[e^{itZ}\mid G_{1}]=\exp\!\left(-\frac{t^{2}G_{1}^{2}}{2}\right),

so after averaging over
G_{1}\sim N(0,1)
,

\phi_{Z}(t):=\mathbb{E}[e^{itZ}]=(1+t^{2})^{-1/2}.

Since
W=\sum_{i=1}^{d}G_{i}H_{i}
is a sum of
d
independent copies of
Z
,

\phi_{W}(t)=\mathbb{E}[e^{itW}]=(1+t^{2})^{-d/2}.

Now use the standard cosine-transform identity

\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\cos(tx)}{(1+t^{2})^{\mu}}\,dt=\frac{\sqrt{\pi}}{\Gamma(\mu)}\left(\frac{|x|}{2}\right)^{\mu-\frac{1}{2}}K_{\mu-\frac{1}{2}}(|x|),\qquad\mu>0,\ x\neq 0,

(10.38)

see
[
1
, Chap. 9]
. Since
\phi_{W}
is even,

f_{d}(x)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-itx}(1+t^{2})^{-d/2}\,dt=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\cos(tx)}{(1+t^{2})^{d/2}}\,dt.

Applying (
10.38
) with
\mu=d/2
gives (
9.10
).
The continuity statement at
0
follows from (
9.6
).
∎

Proof of Lemma
10.1
.

For
s\equiv n\pmod{2}
we have

\mathbb{P}(S_{n}=s)=2^{-n}\binom{n}{j},\qquad j=\frac{n+s}{2},\quad p=\frac{j}{n}.

(10.39)

Assume
|s|\leq n/2
, so
p\in[1/4,3/4]
and
j,n-j\geq n/4
.
Using Stirling’s formula with remainder,

\log(m!)=m\log m-m+\tfrac{1}{2}\log(2\pi m)+O(1/m),

one obtains uniformly over
p\in[1/4,3/4]
,

\log\binom{n}{j}=nH(p)-\tfrac{1}{2}\log\big(2\pi np(1-p)\big)+O(1/n),

(10.40)

where
H(p)=-p\log p-(1-p)\log(1-p)
.
Combining (
10.39
)-(
10.40
) gives the form

\mathbb{P}(S_{n}=s)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi np(1-p)}}\,\exp\big(n(H(p)-\log 2)\big)\,\exp\big(O(1/n)\big),\qquad(|s|\leq n/2).

(10.41)

Write
p=\tfrac{1}{2}+x
with
x:=\tfrac{s}{2n}
(so
|x|\leq 1/4
when
|s|\leq n/2
).
A Taylor expansion of
H
around
1/2
yields

n(H(p)-\log 2)=-\frac{s^{2}}{2n}+O\Big(\frac{|s|^{3}}{n^{2}}\Big),\qquad(|s|\leq n/2),

(10.42)

uniformly in
s
in that range.
In particular, this also implies that

n(H(p)-\log 2)\geq-\frac{s^{2}}{2n}-C\frac{|s|^{3}}{n^{2}},\qquad(|s|\leq n/2),

(10.43)

for an absolute constant
C>0
.

Proof of first claim:

Assume
|s|\leq\sqrt{n}\,T
with
T^{3}=o(\sqrt{n})
. Then
|s|=o(n^{2/3})
and in particular
|s|\leq n/2

for all large
n
, so (
10.41
) applies.
Also
p(1-p)=\tfrac{1}{4}+O(s^{2}/n^{2})
, hence

\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi np(1-p)}}=\frac{2}{\sqrt{2\pi n}}\exp\Big(O\Big(\frac{s^{2}}{n^{2}}\Big)\Big)=\frac{2}{\sqrt{2\pi n}}\big(1+o(1)\big),

uniformly on
|s|\leq\sqrt{n}\,T
, since
s^{2}/n^{2}\leq T^{2}/n=o(1)
.
By (
10.42
),

\frac{|s|^{3}}{n^{2}}\leq\frac{(\sqrt{n}\,T)^{3}}{n^{2}}=\frac{T^{3}}{\sqrt{n}}=o(1),

so the exponential remainders in (
10.41
) are
1+o(1)
uniformly on the same window.
Therefore,

\mathbb{P}(S_{n}=s)=\frac{2}{\sqrt{2\pi n}}e^{-s^{2}/(2n)}\big(1+o(1)\big)=\frac{2}{\sqrt{n}}\phi\Big(\frac{s}{\sqrt{n}}\Big)\big(1+o(1)\big),

uniformly for
|s|\leq\sqrt{n}\,T
, proving (
10.1
).

Proof of second claim:

Fix
n
large and
0\leq s\leq n/2
. Then
p\in[1/4,3/4]
and (
10.41
) holds.
Using the crude bound
p(1-p)\leq 1/4
gives

\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi np(1-p)}}\geq\frac{2}{\sqrt{2\pi n}}.

Combine this with the one-sided entropy bound (
10.43
) and the factor

\exp(O(1/n))\geq 1/2
to conclude

\mathbb{P}(S_{n}=s)\geq\frac{c}{\sqrt{n}}\exp\Big(-\frac{s^{2}}{2n}-C\frac{s^{3}}{n^{2}}\Big),

for absolute constants
c,C>0
and all
n\geq n_{\star}
, which is (
10.2
).
∎

Before proving this we state two Auxiliary Lemmas. Their proofs are deferred to Section
10.4.1
.

Lemma 10.25

(Gaussian product integral)
.

Let
\phi(z):=(2\pi)^{-1/2}e^{-z^{2}/2}
be the standard normal density. Then for every
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\phi(z)\,\phi(z-\sqrt{2}\,x)\,dz=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\,\phi(x).

Lemma 10.26

(Sup bounds for
\chi^{2}_{u}
density and its derivative)
.

Let
g_{u}
be the density of a
\chi^{2}_{u}
random variable:

g_{u}(t)=\frac{1}{2^{u/2}\Gamma(u/2)}\,t^{u/2-1}e^{-t/2},\qquad t>0.

Then there exists an absolute constant
C<\infty
such that for all
u
,

\sup_{t>0}g_{u}(t)\ \leq\ \frac{C}{\sqrt{u}},\qquad\sup_{t>0}|g_{u}^{\prime}(t)|\ \leq\ \frac{C}{u}.

Proof of Lemma
10.18
.

By Lemma
9.5
,
B_{u}=(X_{u}-Y_{u})/2
with
X_{u},Y_{u}
i.i.d.
\chi^{2}_{u}
and density
g_{u}
.
Hence for any
b\in\mathbb{R}
,

f_{u}(b)=2\int_{0}^{\infty}g_{u}(t)\,g_{u}(t-2b)\,\mathbf{1}\{t-2b>0\}\,dt.

(10.44)

Fix
|x|\leq D_{n}
and set
b=x\sqrt{u}
. Write
u=2\nu
. Let
t=u+z\sqrt{2u},dt=\sqrt{2u}\,dz.

The constraint
t>0
is equivalent to
z>-\sqrt{u/2}
.
The constraint
t-2b>0
is equivalent to

u+z\sqrt{2u}-2x\sqrt{u}>0\Leftrightarrow\ z>-\sqrt{u/2}+\sqrt{2}\,x.

We denote from now on
z_{u,x}:=-\sqrt{u/2}+\sqrt{2}\,x
.
Since
|x|\leq D_{n}=o(u^{1/6})
, we have
|x|=o(\sqrt{u})
, hence

\sup_{|x|\leq D_{n}}z_{u,x}=-\sqrt{u/2}+o(\sqrt{u})\to-\infty.

Hence, with this change of variables, (
10.44
) becomes

f_{u}(x\sqrt{u})=2\int_{z>z_{u,x}}g_{u}(u+z\sqrt{2u})\,g_{u}\big(u+(z-\sqrt{2}x)\sqrt{2u}\big)\,\sqrt{2u}\,dz.

(10.45)

Fix
M\geq 1
. Split the integral in (
10.45
) into

|z|\leq M
and
|z|>M
.

For the tail
|z|>M
, we bound one factor by a uniform sup bound on
g_{u}
and the other by its tail mass.
Using Lemma
10.26

\sup_{t>0}g_{u}(t)\leq\frac{C}{\sqrt{u}}\qquad\text{for all large }u,

(10.46)

for an absolute constant
C>0
.
Also,

\int_{|z|>M}g_{u}(u+z\sqrt{2u})\,dz=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2u}}\mathbb{P}\!\left(|X_{u}-u|>M\sqrt{2u}\right)\leq\frac{2}{\sqrt{2u}}e^{-cM^{2}},

where we used a chi-square concentration bound and the last inequality holds for all
u
large
and
M
.
Hence, uniformly over
|x|\leq D_{n}
,

\displaystyle 2\sqrt{2u}\int_{|z|>M}g_{u}(u+z\sqrt{2u})\,g_{u}(u+(z-\sqrt{2}x)\sqrt{2u})\,dz

\displaystyle\leq 2\sqrt{2u}\cdot\sup_{t>0}g_{u}(t)\cdot\int_{|z|>M}g_{u}(u+z\sqrt{2u})\,dz\leq\frac{C}{\sqrt{u}}\,e^{-cM^{2}}.

(10.47)

After multiplying by
\sqrt{u}
, this tail is
\leq Ce^{-cM^{2}}
, which can be made arbitrarily small by choosing
M
large. So it suffices to analyze (
10.45
) on
|z|\leq M
.

On
|z|\leq M
and
|x|\leq D_{n}
, we have
|z-\sqrt{2}x|\leq M+\sqrt{2}D_{n}
.
Since
D_{n}=o(u^{1/6})
, also
M+\sqrt{2}D_{n}=o(u^{1/6})
for every fixed
M
, so Lemma
10.17
applies uniformly:

g_{u}(u+z\sqrt{2u})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2u}}\phi(z)\big(1+o(1)\big),\qquad g_{u}(u+(z-\sqrt{2}x)\sqrt{2u})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2u}}\phi(z-\sqrt{2}x)\big(1+o(1)\big),

uniformly over
|z|\leq M
and
|x|\leq D_{n}
. Plugging into (
10.45
) (restricted to
|z|\leq M
) yields

\displaystyle f_{u}(x\sqrt{u})

\displaystyle=2\sqrt{2u}\int_{|z|\leq M}\frac{1}{2u}\phi(z)\phi(z-\sqrt{2}x)\,dz\cdot(1+o(1))+O\!\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{u}}e^{-cM^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{2}}{\sqrt{u}}\int_{|z|\leq M}\phi(z)\phi(z-\sqrt{2}x)\,dz\cdot(1+o(1))+O\!\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{u}}e^{-cM^{2}}\right),

(10.48)

uniformly for
|x|\leq D_{n}
.

Now let
u\to\infty
first (with
M
fixed), then let
M\to\infty
.
Using Lemma
10.25
,
\int_{\mathbb{R}}\phi(z)\phi(z-\sqrt{2}x)\,dz=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\phi(x)
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
,
and
\int_{|z|>M}\phi(z)\phi(z-\sqrt{2}x)\,dz\leq\int_{|z|>M}\phi(z)\,dz\to 0
uniformly in
x
,
from (
10.48
) we obtain

f_{u}(x\sqrt{u})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{u}}\phi(x)\big(1+o(1)\big),

uniformly for
|x|\leq D_{n}
, proving (
10.24
).

For the second equation we want to prove for all
b\in\mathbb{R}
,
differentiate (
10.44
) with respect to
b
to get

f_{u}^{\prime}(b)=-4\int_{0}^{\infty}g_{u}(t)\,g_{u}^{\prime}(t-2b)\,\mathbf{1}\{t-2b>0\}\,dt.

(10.49)

Fix
b=x\sqrt{u}
with
|x|\leq D_{n}
and apply the same change of variables
t=u+z\sqrt{2u}
to obtain

f_{u}^{\prime}(x\sqrt{u})=-4\sqrt{2u}\int_{z>z_{u,x}}g_{u}(u+z\sqrt{2u})\,g_{u}^{\prime}\big(u+(z-\sqrt{2}x)\sqrt{2u}\big)\,dz.

Truncate to
|z|\leq M
exactly as before: using (
10.46
) and

\sup_{t>0}|g_{u}^{\prime}(t)|\leq C/u
from Lemma
10.26
,
together with the chi-square tail bound, the contribution of
|z|>M
is

O\big(u^{-1}e^{-cM^{2}}\big)
uniformly in
|x|\leq D_{n}
. On
|z|\leq M
, Lemma
10.17
gives

g_{u}(u+z\sqrt{2u})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2u}}\phi(z)\big(1+o(1)\big),\qquad g_{u}^{\prime}\big(u+(z-\sqrt{2}x)\sqrt{2u}\big)=\frac{1}{2u}\phi^{\prime}(z-\sqrt{2}x)\big(1+o(1)\big),

uniformly for
|z|\leq M
and
|x|\leq D_{n}
.
Therefore

f_{u}^{\prime}(x\sqrt{u})=-\frac{2}{u}\int_{|z|\leq M}\phi(z)\phi^{\prime}(z-\sqrt{2}x)\,dz\cdot(1+o(1))+O\!\left(\frac{1}{u}e^{-cM^{2}}\right),

uniformly in
|x|\leq D_{n}
.
Letting
u\to\infty
then
M\to\infty
, and using

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\phi(z)\phi^{\prime}(z-\sqrt{2}x)\,dz=-\frac{1}{2}\phi^{\prime}(x),

(which follows by differentiating
\int\phi(z)\phi(z-\sqrt{2}x)\,dz=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\phi(x)
with respect to
x
),
we conclude

f_{u}^{\prime}(x\sqrt{u})=\frac{1}{u}\phi^{\prime}(x)\big(1+o(1)\big),

uniformly for
|x|\leq D_{n}
, proving (
10.25
).
∎

Proof of Lemma
10.19
.

Recall that

B_{u}=\langle G_{u},H_{u}\rangle=\sum_{i=1}^{u}G_{i}H_{i},

where
G_{u},H_{u}\sim N(0,I_{u})
are independent. Conditioning on
G_{u}
, we have

B_{u}\mid G_{u}\sim N(0,\|G_{u}\|^{2}).

Writing
V:=\|G_{u}\|^{2}\sim\chi^{2}_{u}
, it follows that

f_{u}(x)=\mathbb{E}[\varphi_{V}(x)]=\mathbb{E}\!\left[\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi V}}e^{-x^{2}/(2V)}\right],

(10.50)

where
\varphi_{v}
denotes the
N(0,v)
density.

Fix
x\neq 0
. For every
v>0
,

\varphi_{v}(x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi v}}e^{-x^{2}/(2v)}=\frac{1}{|x|\sqrt{\pi}}\left(\frac{x^{2}}{2v}\right)^{1/2}e^{-x^{2}/(2v)}.

Since the function
t\mapsto t^{1/2}e^{-t}
is bounded on
[0,\infty)
, we get

\varphi_{v}(x)\leq\frac{C}{|x|}\qquad\text{for all }v>0.

Taking expectation in (
10.50
) gives

f_{u}(x)\leq\frac{C}{|x|},

uniformly over
u\geq 1
.

Next, for
x\neq 0
and
v>0
,

\varphi_{v}^{\prime}(x)=-\frac{x}{v}\varphi_{v}(x),

so

|\varphi_{v}^{\prime}(x)|=\frac{|x|}{\sqrt{2\pi}}v^{-3/2}e^{-x^{2}/(2v)}=\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}\,x^{2}}\left(\frac{x^{2}}{2v}\right)^{3/2}e^{-x^{2}/(2v)}.

Since
t\mapsto t^{3/2}e^{-t}
is also bounded on
[0,\infty)
, it follows that

|\varphi_{v}^{\prime}(x)|\leq\frac{C}{x^{2}}\qquad\text{for all }v>0.

Thus for each fixed
x\neq 0
, the random variable
\varphi_{V}^{\prime}(x)
is dominated by the integrable constant
C/x^{2}
, so we can differentiate under the expectation and h ence
f_{u}
is differentiable on
\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
with

f_{u}^{\prime}(x)=\mathbb{E}[\varphi_{V}^{\prime}(x)],\qquad\text{therefore}\qquad|f_{u}^{\prime}(x)|\leq\frac{C}{x^{2}}.

∎

10.4.1
Proof of Lemmas
10.25
,
10.26

Proof of Lemma
10.25
.

Using
\phi(z)=(2\pi)^{-1/2}e^{-z^{2}/2}
we write

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\phi(z)\phi(z-\sqrt{2}x)\,dz=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\exp\!\Big(-\frac{z^{2}+(z-\sqrt{2}x)^{2}}{2}\Big)\,dz.

Expand and complete the square:

-\frac{z^{2}+(z-\sqrt{2}x)^{2}}{2}=-(z^{2}-\sqrt{2}x\,z+x^{2})=-\Big(z-\frac{x}{\sqrt{2}}\Big)^{2}-\frac{x^{2}}{2}.

With the change of variables
u=z-\frac{x}{\sqrt{2}}
we have

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\exp\!\Big(-\Big(z-\frac{x}{\sqrt{2}}\Big)^{2}\Big)\,dz=\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-u^{2}}\,du=\sqrt{\pi}.

Thus

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\phi(z)\phi(z-\sqrt{2}x)\,dz=\frac{e^{-x^{2}/2}}{2\pi}\cdot\sqrt{\pi}=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\pi}}e^{-x^{2}/2}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-x^{2}/2}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\phi(x),

as claimed.
∎

Proof of Lemma
10.26
.

First,

\frac{d}{dt}\log g_{u}(t)=\frac{u/2-1}{t}-\frac{1}{2},

so this derivative is positive for
t<u-2
and negative otherwise, implying:

\sup_{t>0}g_{u}(t)=g_{u}(u-2)=\frac{(u-2)^{u/2-1}e^{-(u-2)/2}}{2^{u/2}\Gamma(u/2)}.

Using the Stirling’s approximation lower bound
\Gamma(u/2)\geq\sqrt{2\pi}\,(u/2)^{u/2-1/2}e^{-u/2}
gives

\sup_{t>0}g_{u}(t)\leq\frac{e}{2\sqrt{\pi}}\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{u}}\Big(1-\frac{2}{u}\Big)^{u/2-1}\leq\frac{e}{2\sqrt{\pi}}\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{u}}.

Next,

g_{u}^{\prime}(t)=g_{u}(t)\Big(\frac{u/2-1}{t}-\frac{1}{2}\Big)=g_{u}(t)\cdot\frac{u-2-t}{2t},

hence

|g_{u}^{\prime}(t)|=g_{u}(t)\cdot\frac{|u-2-t|}{2t}.

For
t\neq u-2
, maximizing
|g_{u}^{\prime}(t)|
is equivalent to maximizing

h(t):=t^{u/2-2}e^{-t/2}|u-2-t|,\qquad t>0.
On each of the intervals
(0,u-2)
and
(u-2,\infty)
, the function
h
is
C^{1}
, and its critical points satisfy

(t-(u-2))^{2}=2(u-2).

Moreover,
h(t)\to 0
as
t\to\infty
, and for
u>2
also
h(t)\to 0
as
t\downarrow 0
. Since
h
is continuous and nonnegative on
(0,\infty)
, it follows that its global maximum is attained either at a boundary or at a critical point. In the boundary the limits are
0
, so the maximum is attained at one of the critical points

t=u-2\pm\sqrt{2(u-2)}.

At either such point,
|u-2-t|=\sqrt{2(u-2)}
, and for large
u
we have
t\geq(u-2)/2
. Hence

\sup_{t>0}|g_{u}^{\prime}(t)|\leq\sup_{t>0}g_{u}(t)\cdot\frac{\sqrt{2(u-2)}}{2\cdot(u-2)/2}\leq\sup_{t>0}g_{u}(t)\cdot\frac{2}{\sqrt{u}}.

Combining with the first bound
\sup_{t>0}g_{u}(t)\leq C/\sqrt{u}
yields

\sup_{t>0}|g_{u}^{\prime}(t)|\leq C/u
after adjusting the universal constant
C
.
∎

Appendix A
A failure of the quenched FP potential to predict Low-Degree hardness

It is natural to wonder whether the monotonicity of the original quenched FP potential,
\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}
(
1
), is equivalent to the low-degree MMSE lower bounds for a family of GAMs. In other words:

Can one deduce results for the low-degree MMSE

directly from the monotonicity behavior of the
quenched
FP potential?

While our results establish this connection for the annealed FP potential, traditional physics intuition suggests that the same should hold for the quenched potential, as the annealed potential is typically defined merely as a tractable proxy for the quenched landscape. It remains possible that many of the results presented in this work transfer to the quenched setting—for instance, by directly establishing an agreement between the monotonicity of the annealed and quenched potentials. However, the purpose of this appendix is to present a thought-provoking counterexample, for which we show that while the monotonicity of the annealed FP potential aligns with the low-degree MMSE lower bounds, the monotonicity of the quenched FP potential does not. This discrepancy between the potentials is notable, and we leave the question of why the annealed potential can ”outperform” the quenched potential in predicting algorithmic hardness as a compelling topic for future work.

We explicitly construct and analyze this counterexample. In this setting, the annealed FP is increasing ”around
0
” (i.e., annealed physics-hard) precisely when the low-degree MMSE is trivial and it begins to decrease around
0
(i.e., annealed physics-easy) exactly when the low-degree MMSE improves upon the trivial MSE. In contrast, the quenched FP remains non-decreasing (i.e., not quenched physics-easy) around
0
well into the regime where the low-degree MMSE already strictly outperforms the trivial MSE.

The Model-Counterexample

The counterexample holds for the following
“truncated” Rademacher” 3-tensor sparse PCA model
. For
k=n^{\beta+o(1)}
where
\beta\in(0,1/2)
, we choose
X=\mathrm{vec}(v^{\otimes 3}),
for
v\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
generated as follows. Let
u\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
with i.i.d. entries
\mathrm{Rad}(k/n),
meaning that for all
i=1,\ldots,N
,
u_{i}=1
with probability
k/(2n)
,
u_{i}=-1
with probability
k/(2n)
and
u_{i}=0
, otherwise. If
\|u\|_{0}\in[k/2,2k]
, we set
v=u.
Otherwise we set
v=1_{[k]}
(the indicator of the first
k
-elements).

Remark A.1

(Explaining the “truncation”, and a roadmap)
.

The model is almost identical to the Rademacher sparse tensor PCA model discussed in Section
3.1.2
, with the only difference being that we truncate the signal
v
to
1_{[k]}
whenever
\|v\|_{0}\notin[k/2,2k]
. A standard application of Bernstein’s inequality shows that the event
\|v\|_{0}\in[k/2,2k]
occurs with probability
1-e^{-\Theta(k)}
. For this reason, the truncated and original models share the same “algorithmic” thresholds (see Section
A.1
), the same “low-degree” thresholds (see Section
A.2
), and the same behavior of the annealed Franz-Parisi potential (see Section
A.2
). In particular, combining our equivalence theorems in Section
3.1.2
, the monotonicity of the annealed FP potential remains equivalent to the low-degree MMSE bounds for the truncated model as well.

The truncation is introduced purely for technical convenience, as it simplifies the analysis of now the quenched Franz-Parisi potential. To complete the counterexample, we show that the monotonicity of the quenched FP potential is not in agreement with the low-degree MMSE lower bounds (see Section
A
). Specifically, the quenched FP potential remains non-monotonic over a large portion of the low-degree “easy” regime.

A.1
The algorithmic threshold

In this section, we prove that for
\lambda=\widetilde{\Theta}(1)
one can achieve exact recovery with high probability (and hence MMSE that is of smaller order to the trivial MMSE) with a (simple) diagonal thresholding polynomial-time method (see Algorithm
1
). In particular, the “truncation” in the prior doesn’t affect the success of diagonal thresholding for the sparse Rademacher 3-tensor PCA model.

Algorithm 1

Diagonal Thresholding for support recovery

1:

Tensor
Y\in\mathbb{R}^{n^{3}}
, threshold
\tau>0

2:

Compute diagonal terms
d_{i}\leftarrow Y_{iii}
for
i=1,\dots,n

3:

Output
\widehat{v}\leftarrow\mathrm{sign}(d_{i})1\{|d_{i}|\geq\tau\}

Our main result for this subsection is as follows.

Lemma A.2

(Diagonal thresholding recovers
v
(including signs))
.

Let
Y
be generated according to the truncated sparse 3-tensor PCA model. For each
i\in[n]
, let
d_{i}:=Y_{iii}
.
Define the diagonal-thresholding estimator
\widehat{v}\in\{-1,0,1\}^{n}
by

\widehat{v}_{i}:=\mathrm{sign}(d_{i})1\{|d_{i}|\geq\tau\},\qquad i=1,\dots,n.

(A.1)

Set
\tau:=\sqrt{6\log n}
and assume
\lambda\geq 2\tau
. Then

\mathbb{P}(\widehat{v}\neq v)\leq 2n^{-2}+4k\,n^{-3}+4k\,n^{-27}.

(A.2)

In particular, if
k=n^{\beta+o(1)}
for any
\beta\in(0,1/2)
, then

\mathbb{P}(\widehat{v}\neq v)=o(1)
.

The proof of this Lemma is deferred to Section
B
.

A.2
The equivalence between the annealead FP potential and the low-degree MMSE

Our next claim is that the monotonicity of the annealed FP potential is equivalent to low-degree MMSE lower bounds for the
truncated
sparse 3-tensor PCA model. We remind the reader that for the original (“untruncated”) sparse 3-tensor PCA model from Section
3.1.2
the equivalence holds are already discussed in Theorem
3.2
); specifically it follows from Theorems
2.13
and
2.11
as the original model satisfies the Assumption
2.10
.

Due to the technical complications of introducing the truncation to the prior, we prove the equivalence for the truncated sparse 3-tensor PCA model not by directly verifying Assumption
2.10
but proving that both (a) the monotonicity of the annealed FP potential and (b) the low-degree MMSE are up to
1+o(1)
-factors identical between the original and the truncated sparse 3-tensor PCA model. Then as the equivalence holds for the original model, the equivalence transfers to the truncated models as well.

A.2.1
Equivalence of low-degree MMSE between the original and truncated models

We start with the almost equivalence between the low-degree MMSE of the truncated 3-sparse tensor PCA model and the original model.
Let
P
be the untruncated prior on signals

\mathrm{vec}(u^{\otimes 3})\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
for the 3-sparse tensor case,
N=n^{3}
, and
\widetilde{P}
denote the truncated prior.

Proposition A.3
.

There exists an absolute constant
C>0
, such that for any
D
satisfying
D=O((\log n)^{h})
with some constant
0\leq h<2
and
\lambda=O(1)
,

\big|\mathrm{MMSE}^{\leq D}_{\widetilde{P}}(\lambda)-\mathrm{MMSE}^{\leq D}_{P}(\lambda)\big|\leq e^{-\Theta(k)}.

Proof.

We proceed as follows to complete the proof.

Step 1: Coupling and reduction to the bad event.

We employ the obvious coupling between the two priors. First, we draw
\widetilde{X}=\mathrm{vec}(u^{\otimes 3})\sim\widetilde{P}
.
Denote

E_{1}(\widetilde{X})=\{\|u\|_{0}\in[k/2,2k]\}
as the measurable event depending on
\widetilde{X}
.
Fix the deterministic vector
x_{0}=\mathrm{vec}(1_{[k]}^{\otimes 3})\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
. Then, for the truncated signal

X:=\widetilde{X}\mathbf{1}_{E_{1}(\widetilde{X})}+x_{0}\mathbf{1}_{E_{1}(\widetilde{X})^{c}},

we have
X\sim P
.
Let
Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{N})
be independent, and define

\widetilde{Y}:=\sqrt{\lambda}\widetilde{X}+Z,\qquad Y:=\sqrt{\lambda}X+Z.

By construction,
(X,Y)=(\widetilde{X},\widetilde{Y})
on
E_{1}(\widetilde{X})
. Hence for any

f\in(\mathbb{R}[\cdot]_{\leq D})^{N}
,

\displaystyle\Big|\mathbb{E}\|f(Y)-X\|^{2}-\mathbb{E}\|f(\widetilde{Y})-\widetilde{X}\|^{2}\Big|

\displaystyle=\Big|\mathbb{E}\Big[\big(\|f(Y)-X\|^{2}-\|f(\widetilde{Y})-\widetilde{X}\|^{2}\big)\mathbf{1}_{E_{1}(\widetilde{X})^{c}}\Big]\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\Big[\big(\|f(Y)-X\|^{2}+\|f(\widetilde{Y})-\widetilde{X}\|^{2}\big)\mathbf{1}_{E_{1}(\widetilde{X})^{c}}\Big]

\displaystyle\leq 2\mathbb{E}\Big[\big(\|f(Y)\|^{2}+\|f(\widetilde{Y})\|^{2}+\|X\|^{2}+\|\widetilde{X}\|^{2}\big)\mathbf{1}_{E_{1}(\widetilde{X})^{c}}\Big].

(A.3)

We now proceed with bounding the four terms:

\mathbb{E}[\|f(Y)\|^{2}\mathbf{1}_{E_{1}^{c}}],\quad\mathbb{E}[\|f(\widetilde{Y})\|^{2}\mathbf{1}_{E_{1}^{c}}],\quad\mathbb{E}[\|X\|^{2}\mathbf{1}_{E_{1}^{c}}],\quad\mathbb{E}[\|\widetilde{X}\|^{2}\mathbf{1}_{E_{1}^{c}}].

Step 2: Restricting the class of estimators
f
.

First, we record the following elementary fact. For any random vectors
U,V
and estimator
f(V)
, if

\mathbb{E}\|f(V)-U\|^{2}\leq\mathbb{E}\|U\|^{2}
, then by triangle inequality,

\|f(V)\|\leq\|f(V)-U\|+\|U\|,

and hence

\|f(V)\|^{2}\leq 2\|f(V)-U\|^{2}+2\|U\|^{2}.

Taking expectations gives

\mathbb{E}\|f(V)\|^{2}\leq 4\mathbb{E}\|U\|^{2}.

When bounding the low-degree MMSE, since the optimal estimator is always better than the zero estimator, we may restrict to estimators satisfying

\mathbb{E}\|f(Y)-X\|^{2}\leq\mathbb{E}\|X\|^{2}.

Thus,

\mathbb{E}\|f(Y)\|^{2}\leq 4\mathbb{E}\|X\|^{2}\leq Ck^{3}.

Similarly, under
\widetilde{P}
, we also have
\mathbb{E}\|f(\widetilde{Y})\|^{2}\leq Ck^{3}.

Step 3: Probability of the bad event.

Let
\delta:=\mathbb{P}_{\widetilde{P}}(E_{1}(\widetilde{X})^{c})
. Since
\|u\|_{0}\sim\mathrm{Bin}(n,k/n)
is subgaussian with mean
k
and variance at most
k
, Bernstein’s inequality implies that there exists an absolute constant
C>0
, such that

\delta\leq e^{-2ck}
.

Step 4: Bounds for the signal terms.

On
E_{1}^{c}
, we have
X=x_{0}
, hence

\mathbb{E}[\|X\|^{2}\mathbf{1}_{E_{1}^{c}}]=\delta k^{3}.

(A.4)

Also, since
\|\widetilde{X}\|^{2}=\|u\|_{0}^{3}
and
\mathbb{E}[\|\widetilde{X}\|^{4}]\leq C^{2}k^{6}
(see e.g.
[
33
, Prop 2.5.2]
), by Cauchy–Schwarz inequality,

\mathbb{E}[\|\widetilde{X}\|^{2}\mathbf{1}_{E_{1}^{c}}]\leq\sqrt{\mathbb{E}[\|\widetilde{X}\|^{4}]\mathbb{P}(E_{1}^{c})}\leq Ck^{3}e^{-ck}.

(A.5)

Step 5: Bound for
\mathbb{E}[\|f(Y)\|^{2}\mathbf{1}_{E_{1}^{c}}]
.

On
E_{1}^{c}
,
Y=\sqrt{\lambda}x_{0}+Z
, hence

\mathbb{E}[\|f(Y)\|^{2}\mathbf{1}_{E_{1}^{c}}]=\delta\,\mathbb{E}\|f(Y)\|^{2}\leq Ck^{3}e^{-2ck}.

(A.6)

Step 6: Bound for
\mathbb{E}[\|f(\widetilde{Y})\|^{2}\mathbf{1}_{E_{1}^{c}}]
.

It remains to control the only nontrivial term

\mathbb{E}[\|f(\widetilde{Y})\|^{2}\mathbf{1}_{E_{1}^{c}}]
.

Step 6a: Upper bound via Hermite expansion.

By conditioning on
u
, we first argue that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\|f(\widetilde{Y})\|^{2}\mathbf{1}_{E_{1}^{c}}]=\mathbb{E}_{u}\left[\mathbf{1}_{E_{1}(\widetilde{X})^{c}}\mathbb{E}_{Z}[\|f(Z+\sqrt{\lambda}\widetilde{X})\|^{2}]\right]\leq\mathbb{E}_{Z}[\|f(Z)\|^{2}]\sum_{j=0}^{D}\binom{D}{j}\frac{1}{j!}\lambda^{j}\mathbb{E}[\|u\|_{0}^{3j}\mathbf{1}_{E_{1}^{c}}].

(A.7)

We conduct the proof of this identity by expanding
f
in the multivariate Hermite basis as

f(z)=\sum_{|\alpha|\leq D}\widehat{f}_{\alpha}H_{\alpha}(z)
, and as a consequence,

\mathbb{E}_{Z}[\|f(Z)\|^{2}]=\sum_{|\alpha|\leq D}\widehat{f}_{\alpha}^{2}\alpha!
.

Let
\mu=\sqrt{\lambda}\widetilde{X}
. Applying Proposition
7.3
, we have
