f(Z+\mu)=\sum_{|\alpha|\leq D}\widehat{f}_{\alpha}\sum_{\gamma\leq\alpha}\binom{\alpha}{\gamma}\mu^{\alpha-\gamma}H_{\gamma}(Z);

and by Lemma
7.5
,

\mathbb{E}_{Z}[\|f(Z+\mu)\|^{2}]=\sum_{|\gamma|\leq D}\gamma!\left\|\sum_{\beta}\widehat{f}_{\gamma+\beta}\binom{\gamma+\beta}{\beta}\mu^{\beta}\right\|^{2}.

We apply the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality to the inner sum over
\beta
:

\left\|\sum_{\beta}\widehat{f}_{\gamma+\beta}\sqrt{(\gamma+\beta)!}\frac{\binom{\gamma+\beta}{\beta}}{\sqrt{(\gamma+\beta)!}}\mu^{\beta}\right\|^{2}\leq\left(\sum_{\beta}\|\widehat{f}_{\gamma+\beta}\|^{2}(\gamma+\beta)!\right)\left(\sum_{\beta}\frac{\binom{\gamma+\beta}{\beta}^{2}}{(\gamma+\beta)!}\mu^{2\beta}\right).

Multiplying both sides by
\gamma!
and using the factorial identity
\gamma!\frac{\binom{\gamma+\beta}{\beta}^{2}}{(\gamma+\beta)!}=\binom{\gamma+\beta}{\beta}\frac{1}{\beta!}
yields

\mathbb{E}_{Z}[\|f(Z+\mu)\|^{2}]\leq\sum_{|\gamma|\leq D}\left(\sum_{\beta}\|\widehat{f}_{\gamma+\beta}\|^{2}(\gamma+\beta)!\right)\left(\sum_{\beta}\binom{\gamma+\beta}{\beta}\frac{\mu^{2\beta}}{\beta!}\right),

changing variables back to
\alpha=\gamma+\beta
, summing over
\gamma
and
\beta
with
|\gamma+\beta|\leq D
is equivalent to summing over
|\alpha|\leq D
and
\beta\leq\alpha
:

\mathbb{E}_{Z}[\|f(Z+\mu)\|^{2}]\leq\sum_{|\alpha|\leq D}\|\widehat{f}_{\alpha}\|^{2}\alpha!\sum_{\beta\leq\alpha}\binom{\alpha}{\beta}\frac{\mu^{2\beta}}{\beta!}.

Combinatorially,
\binom{\alpha}{\beta}=\prod_{i=1}^{N}\binom{\alpha_{i}}{\beta_{i}}\leq\binom{|\alpha|}{|\beta|}\leq\binom{D}{|\beta|}
. Let
j=|\beta|
. Grouping the inner sum by the degree
j
, we get

\sum_{\beta\leq\alpha}\binom{\alpha}{\beta}\frac{\mu^{2\beta}}{\beta!}\leq\sum_{j=0}^{|\alpha|}\binom{D}{j}\sum_{|\beta|=j}\frac{\mu^{2\beta}}{\beta!}

By the multinomial theorem,
\sum_{|\beta|=j}\frac{j!}{\beta!}\mu^{2\beta}=(\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mu_{i}^{2})^{j}=\|\mu\|^{2j}
. Therefore, the inner sum is uniformly bounded by
\sum_{j=0}^{D}\binom{D}{j}\frac{1}{j!}\|\mu\|^{2j}
for any
\alpha
. Factoring this out, we extract the baseline norm:

\mathbb{E}_{Z}[\|f(Z+\mu)\|^{2}]\leq\left(\sum_{|\alpha|\leq D}\|\widehat{f}_{\alpha}\|^{2}\alpha!\right)\sum_{j=0}^{D}\binom{D}{j}\frac{1}{j!}\|\mu\|^{2j}=\mathbb{E}_{Z}[\|f(Z)\|^{2}]\sum_{j=0}^{D}C_{j}(D)\|\mu\|^{2j}

(A.8)

where
C_{j}(D)=\binom{D}{j}\frac{1}{j!}
, which proves (
A.7
).

Recall
\|\mu\|^{2}=\lambda\|\widetilde{X}\|^{2}=\lambda\|u\|_{0}^{3}
, where
\|u\|_{0}\sim\mathrm{Binomial}(n,k/n)
. Similarly according to
[
33
, Prop 2.5.2]
, for some absolute constant
C>0
that for every
j\leq D
,
\mathbb{E}[\|u\|_{0}^{6j}]\leq(Ck)^{6j}
. Then by using Cauchy–Schwarz inequality,

\mathbb{E}[\|u\|_{0}^{3j}\mathbf{1}_{E_{1}(\widetilde{X})^{c}}]\leq\sqrt{\mathbb{E}[\|u\|_{0}^{6j}]\mathbb{P}(E_{1}(\widetilde{X})^{c})}=\sqrt{\mathbb{E}[\|u\|_{0}^{6j}]}\sqrt{\delta}\leq(Ck)^{3j}e^{-ck},

(
A.7
) leads to

\mathbb{E}[\|f(\widetilde{Y})\|^{2}\mathbf{1}_{E_{1}^{c}}]\leq\mathbb{E}_{Z}[\|f(Z)\|^{2}]\cdot\frac{1}{(D-1)!}(C\lambda k)^{3D}e^{-ck}.

Step 6b: Lower bound relating
\mathbb{E}\|f(\widetilde{Y})\|^{2}
and
\mathbb{E}_{Z}[\|f(Z)\|^{2}]
.

To conclude, we need to upper bound
\mathbb{E}_{Z}[\|f(Z)\|^{2}]
in terms of
\mathbb{E}\|f(\widetilde{Y})\|^{2}
and use Step 1.

For each multi-index
\gamma
, let
A_{\gamma}(\mu)=\sum_{\beta>0}\widehat{f}_{\gamma+\beta}\binom{\gamma+\beta}{\beta}\mu^{\beta}
represent the strictly higher-degree shift components. Then by Jensen’s inequality,

\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\left[\|\widehat{f}_{\gamma}+A_{\gamma}(\mu)\|^{2}\right]\geq\|\widehat{f}_{\gamma}+\mathbb{E}[A_{\gamma}(\mu)]\|^{2}.

(A.9)

Using Cauchy–Schwarz inequality, for any
\eta>0
,
(a+b)^{2}\geq(1+\eta)^{-1}a^{2}-\eta^{-1}b^{2}
. Therefore, we obtain

\mathbb{E}[\|f(Z+\mu)\|^{2}]\geq(1+\eta)^{-1}\sum_{|\gamma|\leq D}\gamma!\|\widehat{f}_{\gamma}\|^{2}-\eta^{-1}\sum_{|\gamma|\leq D}\gamma!\|\mathbb{E}[A_{\gamma}(\mu)]\|^{2}.

Now define the moments
m_{\beta}=\mathbb{E}[\mu^{\beta}]
. By using Cauchy–Schwarz inequality in the
\beta
-sum, we have

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{E}[A_{\gamma}(\mu)]\|^{2}=\left\|\sum_{\beta>0}\widehat{f}_{\gamma+\beta}\binom{\gamma+\beta}{\beta}m_{\beta}\right\|^{2}\leq\left(\sum_{\beta>0}(\gamma+\beta)!\|\widehat{f}_{\gamma+\beta}\|^{2}\right)\left(\sum_{\beta>0}\frac{\binom{\gamma+\beta}{\beta}^{2}m_{\beta}^{2}}{(\gamma+\beta)!}\right).

Multiplying by
\gamma!
and using

\gamma!\frac{\binom{\gamma+\beta}{\beta}^{2}}{(\gamma+\beta)!}=\binom{\gamma+\beta}{\beta}\frac{1}{\beta!},

we have

\displaystyle\sum_{|\gamma|\leq D}\gamma!\|\mathbb{E}[A_{\gamma}(\mu)]\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\sum_{\beta>0}(\gamma+\beta)!\|\widehat{f}_{\gamma+\beta}\|^{2}\right)\left(\sum_{\beta>0}\binom{\gamma+\beta}{\beta}\frac{m_{\beta}^{2}}{\beta!}\right)\leq\Bigg(\sup_{|\alpha|\leq D}\sum_{0<\beta\leq\alpha}\binom{\alpha}{\beta}\frac{m_{\beta}^{2}}{\beta!}\Bigg)\sum_{|\alpha|\leq D}\alpha!\|\widehat{f}_{\alpha}\|^{2}

\displaystyle:=S_{D}\sum_{|\alpha|\leq D}\alpha!\|\widehat{f}_{\alpha}\|^{2}=S_{D}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{Z}[\|f(Z)\|^{2}],

where we denote

S_{D}=\sup_{|\alpha|\leq D}\sum_{0<\beta\leq\alpha}\binom{\alpha}{\beta}\frac{m_{\beta}^{2}}{\beta!}.

Combining this with the previous inequality, we get

\mathbb{E}[\|f(Z+\mu)\|^{2}]\geq((1+\eta)^{-1}-\eta^{-1}S_{D})\mathbb{E}_{Z}[\|f(Z)\|^{2}].

(A.10)

Write
p=k/n
. The coordinates of
\mu
are

\mu_{abc}=\sqrt{\lambda}u_{a}u_{b}u_{c}
, for every
a,b,c\in[n]
.
Let
e_{i}(\beta)
be the exponent of
u_{i}
in
\mu^{\beta}
, and define

r(\beta)=|\{i:e_{i}(\beta)>0\}|.

Since

\mathbb{E}[u_{i}^{r}]=\begin{cases}1,&r=0,\\
0,&r\text{ odd},\\
p,&r\geq 2\text{ even},\end{cases}

we obtain

m_{\beta}=\lambda^{|\beta|/2}p^{r(\beta)}\mathbf{1}_{\{e_{i}(\beta)\text{ all even}\}}.

We split the sum defining
S_{D}
according to whether
r(\beta)=1
or

r(\beta)\geq 2
:

S_{D}\leq S_{D}^{(1)}+S_{D}^{(\geq 2)},

where

S_{D}^{(1)}:=\sup_{|\alpha|\leq D}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0<\beta\leq\alpha\\
r(\beta)=1\end{subarray}}\binom{\alpha}{\beta}\frac{m_{\beta}^{2}}{\beta!},\qquad S_{D}^{(\geq 2)}:=\sup_{|\alpha|\leq D}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0<\beta\leq\alpha\\
r(\beta)\geq 2\end{subarray}}\binom{\alpha}{\beta}\frac{m_{\beta}^{2}}{\beta!}.

Suppose
r(\beta)=1
and
m_{\beta}\neq 0
.
Then there exists a unique
i\in[n]
such that
e_{i}(\beta)>0
and

e_{j}(\beta)=0
for all
j\neq i
.
This means that every tensor coordinate
(a,b,c)
in the support of
\beta

must satisfy
a=b=c=i
.
Therefore
\beta
must be supported on a single pure diagonal coordinate

(i,i,i)
, that is,

\beta=be_{(i,i,i)}

for some integer
b\geq 1
, where
e_{(i,i,i)}
denotes the corresponding
coordinate basis vector.
Moreover, for
m_{\beta}
to be nonzero, all exponents
e_{j}(\beta)
must be even;
here
e_{i}(\beta)=3b
, so necessarily
b
is even.
Hence the nonzero terms with
r(\beta)=1
are exactly the multi-indices

\beta=2qe_{(i,i,i)}
,
q\geq 1
.
For such
\beta
,

m_{\beta}^{2}=\lambda^{2q}p^{2}
.
Fix
\alpha
with
|\alpha|\leq D
.
Then

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0<\beta\leq\alpha\\
r(\beta)=1\end{subarray}}\binom{\alpha}{\beta}\frac{m_{\beta}^{2}}{\beta!}

\displaystyle=p^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}2q\leq\alpha_{(i,i,i)}\\
q\geq 1\end{subarray}}\binom{\alpha_{(i,i,i)}}{2q}\frac{\lambda^{2q}}{(2q)!}.

For each fixed
q\geq 1
, using

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\binom{\alpha_{(i,i,i)}}{2q}\leq\binom{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{(i,i,i)}}{2q}\leq\binom{|\alpha|}{2q}\leq\binom{D}{2q},

we get

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0<\beta\leq\alpha\\
r(\beta)=1\end{subarray}}\binom{\alpha}{\beta}\frac{m_{\beta}^{2}}{\beta!}

\displaystyle\leq p^{2}\sum_{q=1}^{\lfloor D/2\rfloor}\binom{D}{2q}\frac{\lambda^{2q}}{(2q)!}.

Taking the supremum over
|\alpha|\leq D
gives

S_{D}^{(1)}\leq p^{2}\sum_{q=1}^{\lfloor D/2\rfloor}\binom{D}{2q}\frac{\lambda^{2q}}{(2q)!}\leq p^{2}\sum_{q=1}^{\lfloor D/2\rfloor}\frac{(D\lambda)^{2q}}{((2q)!)^{2}}\leq p^{2}I_{0}(2\sqrt{D\lambda}),

where
I_{0}(z)
is the modified Bessel function of the first kind defined in Appendix
9
.
Use the integral representation of the modified Bessel function,
I_{0}(z)=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\pi}\exp(z\cos\theta)d\theta
. Since
\cos\theta\leq 1
over the entire integration interval, the integrand is bounded by
\exp(z)
. Therefore,
I_{0}(z)\leq\exp(z)
, and
S_{D}^{(1)}\leq p^{2}\exp(2\sqrt{D\lambda})
.

Since
p=k/n
,
k=O(\sqrt{n})
,
D=O((\log n)^{h})
with
h<2
,
\lambda=O(1)
, we can see
\exp(2\sqrt{D\lambda})=n^{o(1)}
, so
S_{D}^{(1)}=o(1)
.

If
r(\beta)\geq 2
and
m_{\beta}\neq 0
, then

m_{\beta}^{2}=\lambda^{|\beta|}p^{2r(\beta)}\leq\lambda^{|\beta|}p^{4}
.
Hence for every
\alpha
with
|\alpha|\leq D
,

\displaystyle S_{D}^{(2)}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0<\beta\leq\alpha\\
r(\beta)\geq 2\end{subarray}}\binom{\alpha}{\beta}\frac{m_{\beta}^{2}}{\beta!}

\displaystyle\leq p^{4}\sum_{0<\beta\leq\alpha}\binom{\alpha}{\beta}\frac{\lambda^{|\beta|}}{\beta!}=\widetilde{O}(p^{2})=o(1).

The above argument shows
S_{D}=o(1)
, so for sufficiently large
n
,
S_{D}\leq 0.4
.
By choosing
\eta=0.99
in (
A.10
),

\mathbb{E}_{P}\|f(\widetilde{Y})\|^{2}\geq 0.1\mathbb{E}_{Z}[\|f(Z)\|^{2}].

Plugging back to (
A.7
) and combining with Step 1, we conclude the following:

\mathbb{E}[\|f(\widetilde{Y})\|^{2}\mathbf{1}_{E_{1}^{c}}]\leq\frac{10}{(D-1)!}k^{3}(C\lambda k)^{3D}e^{-ck}=e^{-\Theta(k)}.

(A.11)

Step 7: Conclusion.

According to the inequalities (
A.2.1
), (
A.4
), (
A.5
), (
A.6
) and (
A.11
), we have

\displaystyle\big|\mathrm{MMSE}^{\leq D}_{P}(\lambda)-\mathrm{MMSE}^{\leq D}_{\widetilde{P}}(\lambda)\big|=\bigg|\inf_{f\in\mathbb{R}^{N}[Y]_{\leq D}}\mathbb{E}\!\left[\|f(Y)-X\|^{2}\right]-\inf_{f\in\mathbb{R}^{N}[\widetilde{Y}]_{\leq D}}\mathbb{E}\!\left[\|f(\widetilde{Y})-\widetilde{X}\|^{2}\right]\bigg|

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\sup_{f\in\mathbb{R}^{N}_{\leq D},\mathbb{E}\|f(\widetilde{Y})\|^{2}\leq Ck^{3},\mathbb{E}\|f(Y)\|^{2}\leq Ck^{3}}\Big|\mathbb{E}\|f(Y)-X\|^{2}-\mathbb{E}\|f(\widetilde{Y})-\widetilde{X}\|^{2}\Big|

\displaystyle\qquad\leq 2\sup_{f\in\mathbb{R}^{N}_{\leq D},\mathbb{E}\|f(\widetilde{Y})\|^{2}\leq Ck^{3},\mathbb{E}\|f(Y)\|^{2}\leq Ck^{3}}\Big|\mathbb{E}\Big[\big(\|f(Y)\|^{2}+\|f(\widetilde{Y})\|^{2}+\|X\|^{2}+\|\widetilde{X}\|^{2}\big)\mathbf{1}_{E_{1}(\widetilde{X})^{c}}\Big]\Big|

\displaystyle\qquad\leq Ck^{3}(C\lambda k)^{3D}e^{-ck}=e^{-\Theta(k)}.

∎

A.2.2
Equivalence of the monotonicity of annealed FP potential between original and truncated models

Next, we prove that the truncation to the prior doesn’t change the monotonicity of the annealed FP potential.

Indeed, we prove this by showing that the log-PMF of the overlap random variable
\langle X,X^{\prime}\rangle
for two i.i.d. draws
X,X^{\prime}
from the truncated prior is up to a multiplicative
o(1)
factor, the same as the log-PMF of the original prior.

We first introduce some notation. For
v,v^{\prime}\in\{-1,0,1\}^{n}
let
S:=\langle v,v^{\prime}\rangle,
and define

E_{2}=E_{2}(v,v^{\prime}):=\Big\{\|v\|_{0}\in[k/2,2k]\Big\}\cap\Big\{\|v^{\prime}\|_{0}\in[k/2,2k]\Big\}.

Notice that under this notation the original prior on
v,v^{\prime}
is
\widetilde{P}
, where
v,v^{\prime}
are i.i.d. with i.i.d.
\text{Rad}(k/n)

coordinates, relates with the truncated
P
by,

{\mathbb{P}}_{P}(\,\cdot\,):={\mathbb{P}}(\,\cdot\mid E_{2}).

We then denote the annealed FP potentials of the truncated and original models by

\mathcal{F}^{\widetilde{P}}_{\rm ann,\lambda}(q),q\in\mathbb{R}\text{, and, }\mathcal{F}^{P}_{\rm ann,\lambda}(q),q\in\mathbb{R}.

We prove the following statement about them.

Theorem A.4
.

Fix
\beta\in(0,1/2)
, let
k=n^{\beta+o(1)}
. For any
n^{-C}\leq\lambda\leq n^{C}
, for some constant
C>0
and any integer
q=q_{n}
with
1\leq q=o(k)
, let
s=q^{1/3}
and define the sequence
a_{n}(s)=(s+2)^{3}-s^{3},n\in\mathbb{N}.
Then,

\Delta_{a_{n}(s)}\mathcal{F}^{\widetilde{P}}_{\rm ann,\lambda}(q)=(1+o(1))\Delta_{a_{n}(s)}\mathcal{F}^{P}_{\rm ann,\lambda}(q),

(A.12)

uniformly over all such
q
.

Proof.

First note that for both models

\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ann},\lambda}(q)=-\lambda q-\log\mathbb{P}(\langle X,X^{\prime}\rangle=q).

(A.13)

Notice also that for both models if
X,X^{\prime}
correspond to vectors
v,v^{\prime},

\langle X,X^{\prime}\rangle=S^{3},
where
S=\langle v,v^{\prime}\rangle
. To prove the Theorem, we will start by proving the following for
q=o(k),

\log{\mathbb{P}}_{P}(S^{3}=q)=(1+o(1))\,\log\mathbb{P}(S^{3}=q).

(A.14)

Let
L:=\|v\|_{0}
. Under the i.i.d.
\text{Rad}(k/n)
prior,
L\sim\mathrm{Bin}(n,k/n)
and
\mathbb{E}L=k
,
So by Chernoff’s inequality, there exists
c_{0}>0
such that

\mathbb{P}\big(L\notin[k/2,2k]\big)\leq 2e^{-c_{0}k}.

Since
v,v^{\prime}
are independent,

\mathbb{P}(E_{2}^{c})\leq 2\mathbb{P}\big(L\notin[k/2,2k]\big)\leq 4e^{-c_{0}k}=\exp\big(-\Omega(k)\big)=\exp\big(-\Omega(n^{\beta})\big).

(A.15)

Write
X_{i}:=v_{i}v^{\prime}_{i}\in\{-1,0,1\},i=1,2,\ldots,n
. Then
S=\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}
and

\mathbb{P}(X_{i}=1)=\mathbb{P}(X_{i}=-1)=:p,\qquad\mathbb{P}(X_{i}=0)=1-2p,

with
np=\Theta(k^{2}/n)=\Theta(n^{2\beta-1})\to 0
since
\beta<1/2
.

Let
s=q^{1/3}
. Consider the event that exactly
s
coordinates satisfy
X_{i}=1

and the remaining
n-s
satisfy
X_{i}=0
. This event implies
S=s
, hence

\mathbb{P}(S=s)\ \geq\ \binom{n}{s}p^{s}(1-2p)^{n-s}.

Since
np\to 0
, for all large
n
we have
(1-2p)^{n-s}\geq 1/2
. Also,

\binom{n}{s}\geq(n/s)^{s}.

Therefore, for all large
n
,

\mathbb{P}(S=s)\geq\frac{1}{2}\Big(\frac{n}{s}\Big)^{s}p^{s}=\frac{1}{2}\Big(\frac{np}{s}\Big)^{s}.

Since
np=n^{-(1-2\beta)+o(1)}
, it follows that

\mathbb{P}(S=s)\geq\exp\big(-C(s\log n+s\log s)\big)

(A.16)

for some constant
C<\infty
. By the assumption
q=o(k)
it follows that
s\log n+s\log s=o(k)
and
we deduce that

\mathbb{P}(S=s)\geq\exp(-o(k)).

(A.17)

For the upper bound, if
S=s
then necessarily at least
s
coordinates satisfy
X_{i}=1
, so

\mathbb{P}(S=s)\leq\sum_{j=s}^{n}\binom{n}{j}p^{j}(1-p)^{n-j}\leq\sum_{j=s}^{\infty}\frac{(np)^{j}}{j!}=O((np)^{s}),

where we used
\binom{n}{j}\leq n^{j}/j!,1-p\leq 1
and that
np\to 0.
Hence,

\log\mathbb{P}(S=s_{n})\to-\infty.

(A.18)

Now let
A_{n}:=\{S^{3}=s^{3}\}=\{S=s\}
. Then

{\mathbb{P}}_{P}(A_{n})=\frac{\mathbb{P}(A_{n}\cap E_{2})}{\mathbb{P}(E_{2})}=\mathbb{P}(A_{n})\cdot\frac{1-\mathbb{P}(A_{n}\cap E_{2}^{c})/\mathbb{P}(A_{n})}{1-\mathbb{P}(E_{2}^{c})}.

Using (
A.15
) and (
A.16
),

0\leq\frac{\mathbb{P}(A_{n}\cap E_{2}^{c})}{\mathbb{P}(A_{n})}\leq\frac{\mathbb{P}(E_{2}^{c})}{\mathbb{P}(A_{n})}\leq\exp(-\Omega(k)+o(k))=n^{-\omega(1)},

and also
\mathbb{P}(E_{2})=1-\mathbb{P}(E_{2}^{c})=1-o(1)
. Hence

{\mathbb{P}}_{P}(A_{n})=\mathbb{P}(A_{n})\,(1+n^{-\omega(1)}).

(A.19)

Taking logarithms gives, using
|\log(x+1)-x|\leq x^{2}/2
for
x\to 0
,

\log{\mathbb{P}}_{P}(A_{n})=\log\mathbb{P}(A_{n})+n^{-\omega(1)}.

(A.20)

Finally, by (
A.18
),

\log{\mathbb{P}}_{P}(A_{n})=(1+n^{-\omega(1)})\,\log\mathbb{P}(A_{n}),

(A.21)

which is exactly (
A.14
).
To obtain the discrete-derivative statement, apply Equation (
A.21
) both at
s
and at
s+2
. Since for any GAM,

\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ann},\lambda}(s^{3})=-\lambda s^{3}-\log\mathbb{P}(S=s),

we obtain, using that
s\geq 1
,

\displaystyle\Delta_{a_{n}(s)}\mathcal{F}^{\widetilde{P}}_{\rm ann,\lambda}(s^{3})

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathcal{F}^{\widetilde{P}}_{\rm ann,\lambda}((s+2)^{3})-\mathcal{F}^{\widetilde{P}}_{\rm ann,\lambda}(s^{3})}{(s+2)^{3}-s^{3}}

(A.22)

\displaystyle=-\lambda-\frac{\log\mathbb{P}_{\widetilde{P}}(S=s+2)-\log\mathbb{P}_{\widetilde{P}}(S=s)}{(s+2)^{3}-s^{3}}

(A.23)

\displaystyle=-\lambda-(1+n^{-\omega(1)})\frac{\log\mathbb{P}(S=s+2)-\log\mathbb{P}(S=s)}{(s+2)^{3}-s^{3}}

(A.24)

\displaystyle=(1+o(1))\Delta_{a_{n}(s)}\mathcal{F}^{P}_{\rm ann,\lambda}(s^{3})+\lambda n^{-\omega(1)}.

(A.25)

Next, using that
n^{-C}\leq\lambda\leq n^{C}
combined with the fact that

-\Delta_{a_{n}}\log\mathbb{P}(\langle X,X^{\prime}\rangle=q)\geq n^{C^{\prime}}

for some
C^{\prime}>0
and for all
q=o(k)
by Lemma
10.5
, we get that

\big|\Delta_{a_{n}(s)}\mathcal{F}^{P}_{\rm ann,\lambda}\big|\geq n^{-(C+C^{\prime})}.

(A.26)

Equation (
A.26
) together with (
A.25
) give (
A.12
) and the proof is complete.

∎

A.2.3
Small low-degree MMSE in the “easy” regime

In this section, we prove that the low-degree MMSE for the truncated prior does become small when
\lambda=\widetilde{\omega}(1),
a crucial step for the equivalence.

We define

\mathrm{MMSE}^{\leq D}(v):=\inf_{\widehat{v}}\mathbb{E}\|v-\widehat{v}(Y)\|_{2}^{2},\qquad\mathrm{MMSE}^{\leq D}(X):=\inf_{\widehat{X}}\mathbb{E}\|X-\widehat{X}(Y)\|_{F}^{2}.

Lemma A.5

(Reducing tensor estimation to vector estimation)
.

There exists a positive constant
C>0
such that for all
D>0,

\mathrm{MMSE}^{\leq D}(X)\leq C\,k^{2}\mathrm{MMSE}^{\leq D}(v).

Proof.

Let

\mathcal{C}:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:\ \|x\|_{\infty}\leq 1,\ \|x\|_{0}\leq 2k\}.

Since the true vector
v
belongs to
\mathcal{C}
almost surely, for any estimator
\widehat{v}(Y)
we may project it onto
\mathcal{C}
:

\widetilde{v}(Y):=\Pi_{\mathcal{C}}(\widehat{v}(Y)).

Since
C
is a closed set,

\|v-\widetilde{v}\|_{2}\leq\|v-\widehat{v}\|_{2}
almost surely. Also,

\|v\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{2k},\ \|\widetilde{v}\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{2k}.
Now define

\widetilde{X}:=\widetilde{v}^{\otimes 3}.

Using the telescoping identity

v^{\otimes 3}-\widetilde{v}^{\otimes 3}=\sum_{j=1}^{r}v^{\otimes(j-1)}\otimes(v-\widetilde{v})\otimes\widetilde{v}^{\otimes(3-j)},

together with the identity
\|v^{\otimes m}\|_{F}=\|v\|_{2}^{m},

we obtain

\|X-\widetilde{X}\|_{F}\leq\sum_{j=1}^{3}\|v\|_{2}^{j-1}\|v-\widetilde{v}\|_{2}\|\widetilde{v}\|_{2}^{3-j}\leq 6k\|v-\widetilde{v}\|_{2}.

Squaring and taking expectations gives

\mathbb{E}\|X-\widetilde{X}\|_{F}^{2}\leq 9k^{2}\mathbb{E}\|v-\widetilde{v}\|_{2}^{2}\leq 9k^{2}\mathbb{E}\|v-\widehat{v}\|_{2}^{2}.

Finally, taking infimum over all estimators
\widehat{v}
we get the desired result.
∎

Lemma A.6

(A diagonal odd-power estimator for
v
)
.

Let
d\geq 1
be an odd integer and define

\widehat{v}_{i}:=\lambda^{-d}Y_{i,\ldots,i}^{d},\ i=1,\dots,n.\quad\text{Then},\ \ \mathbb{E}\|v-\widehat{v}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{8k\,d^{3}}{\lambda^{2}}+n\Big(\frac{2d}{\lambda^{2}}\Big)^{d}

(A.27)

provided

2d^{2}\leq\lambda^{2}.

Proof.

Fix
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
.

Case 1:
v_{i}=0
.

Then
Y_{i,\ldots,i}=Z_{i,\dots,i}
, hence

\widehat{v}_{i}=\lambda^{-d}Z_{i,\dots,i}^{d},

and therefore

\mathbb{E}[\widehat{v}_{i}^{2}\mid v_{i}=0]=\lambda^{-2d}\mathbb{E}[Z_{i,\dots,i}^{2d}]=\lambda^{-2d}(2d-1)!!.

Since
(2d-1)!!=1\cdot 3\cdot 5\cdots(2d-1)\leq(2d)^{d},
we get

\mathbb{E}[\widehat{v}_{i}^{2}\mid v_{i}=0]\leq\Big(\frac{2d}{\lambda^{2}}\Big)^{d}.

Case 2:
v_{i}=1
.

Now

Y_{i,\ldots,i}=\lambda+Z_{i,\dots,i},
so

\widehat{v}_{i}-1=\lambda^{-d}(\lambda+Z_{i,\dots,i})^{d}-1=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\binom{d}{j}\lambda^{-j}Z_{i,\dots,i}^{j}.

Hence, by using Cauchy–Schwarz inequality in the form

\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{d}a_{j}\Big)^{2}\leq d\sum_{j=1}^{d}a_{j}^{2},
we obtain

\mathbb{E}[(\widehat{v}_{i}-1)^{2}\mid v_{i}=1]\leq d\sum_{j=1}^{d}\binom{d}{j}^{2}\lambda^{-2j}\mathbb{E}[Z_{i,\dots,i}^{2j}].

Using

\binom{d}{j}\leq\frac{d^{j}}{j!},\qquad\mathbb{E}[Z_{i,\dots,i}^{2j}]=(2j-1)!!\leq 2^{j}j!,

we find

\mathbb{E}[(\widehat{v}_{i}-1)^{2}\mid v_{i}=1]\leq d\sum_{j=1}^{d}\frac{d^{2j}}{(j!)^{2}}\lambda^{-2j}\cdot 2^{j}j!=d\sum_{j=1}^{d}\frac{(2d^{2}/\lambda^{2})^{j}}{j!}\leq d\big(e^{2d^{2}/\lambda^{2}}-1\big).

Under the assumption
2d^{2}\leq\lambda^{2}
, we have
2d^{2}/\lambda^{2}\leq 1
, and so using
e^{x}-1\leq 2x,x\in[0,1]
we get

\mathbb{E}[(\widehat{v}_{i}-1)^{2}\mid v_{i}=1]\leq\frac{4d^{3}}{\lambda^{2}}.

Case 3:
v_{i}=-1
.

Since
d
is odd and
Z_{i,\dots,i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}-Z_{i,\dots,i}
,

\mathbb{E}[(\widehat{v}_{i}+1)^{2}\mid v_{i}=-1]=\mathbb{E}[(\widehat{v}_{i}-1)^{2}\mid v_{i}=1]\leq\frac{4d^{3}}{\lambda^{2}}.

Now sum over all coordinates. Since
\|v\|_{0}\leq 2k
almost surely, there are at most
2k
active coordinates and at most
n
coordinates in total. Hence

\mathbb{E}\|v-\widehat{v}\|_{2}^{2}\leq 2k\cdot\frac{4d^{3}}{\lambda^{2}}+n\Big(\frac{2d}{\lambda^{2}}\Big)^{d},

which is exactly (
A.27
).
∎

Corollary A.7
.

Assume

\lambda\geq(\log n)^{2}.
Let
D
be arbitrary with
D=\Theta(\log n)
. Then,

\mathrm{MMSE}^{\leq D}(X)=o(k^{3}).

Proof.

Since
D\leq\log n
and
\lambda\geq(\log n)^{2}
, for all large
n
we have
2D^{2}\leq 2(\log n)^{2}\leq\lambda^{2},
so Lemma
A.6
applies. Thus

\mathbb{E}\|v-\widehat{v}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{8k\,D^{3}}{\lambda^{2}}+n\Big(\frac{2D}{\lambda^{2}}\Big)^{D}.

For the first term,

\frac{8k\,D^{3}}{\lambda^{2}}\leq 8k\frac{(\log n)^{3}}{(\log n)^{4}}=\frac{8k}{\log n}=o(k).

For the second term, since
D=\Theta(\log n)
and
\lambda^{2}\geq(\log n)^{4}
,

n\Big(\frac{2D}{\lambda^{2}}\Big)^{D}\leq n\Big(\frac{2\log n}{(\log n)^{4}}\Big)^{D}=n\Big(\frac{2}{(\log n)^{3}}\Big)^{D}.

Taking logarithms and using
D=\Theta(\log n)
,

\log\!\left[n\Big(\frac{2}{(\log n)^{3}}\Big)^{D}\right]=\log n+D\big(\log 2-3\log\log n\big)\to-\infty.

Hence
n(2D/\lambda^{2})^{D}=o(1),

and therefore it is certainly
o(k)
. This proves

\mathbb{E}\|v-\widehat{v}\|_{2}^{2}=o(k),

and so
\mathrm{MMSE}^{\leq D}(v)=o(k)
. The
\mathrm{MMSE}^{\leq D}(X)=o(k^{3})
conclusion then follows from Corollary
A.7
.
∎

A.2.4
Putting it all together

Theorem A.8

(Annealed FP monotonicity agreement with the low-degree MMSE for the truncated model)
.

Fix
C>0
and suppose
n^{-C}\leq\lambda\leq n^{C}
. There exists
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}=\widetilde{\Theta}(1)
with
\lambda_{1}<\lambda_{2}
such that the following holds.

If
\lambda\leq\lambda_{1}
, then

1.

the annealed Franz–Parisi potential for the truncated model
\mathcal{F}^{P}_{\rm ann,\lambda}(q)
is monotone decreasing on the interval
q=s^{3}\in[0,\Theta((\log n)^{3})].

2.

the low-degree MMSE for any
D=\Theta(\log n)
is asymptotically trivial, namely

\mathrm{MMSE}^{\leq D}_{P}(\lambda)=(1-o(1))\mathrm{MMSE}^{\mathrm{trivial}}_{P}

If
\lambda\geq\lambda_{2}
, then

1.

the annealed Franz–Parisi potential for the truncated model
\mathcal{F}^{P}_{\rm ann,\lambda}(q)
is monotone increasing on the interval
q=s^{3}\in[0,n^{o(1)}].

2.

the low-degree MMSE for
D=\Theta(\log n)
is asymptotically of lower order than the trivial, namely

\mathrm{MMSE}^{\leq D}_{P}(\lambda)=o(\mathrm{MMSE}^{\mathrm{trivial}}_{P}).

Remark A.9
.

Observe the agreement between the two different predictions for this task. When
\lambda
is smaller than the algorithmic threshold, the problem is “physics-hard” as the annealed FP is increasing and also low-degree hard as the low-degree MMSE is trivial. On the other hand, if
\lambda
is larger than the algorithmic threshold the problem is “physics-easy” as the annealed FP is decreasing, and also low-degree hard as the low-degree MMSE is beating the trivial MSE.

Proof.

We use the notations
\widetilde{P}
and
P
to denote the untruncated and truncated priors, respectively, as defined in the start of Section
A.2.1
.

First, by Theorem
3.2
for the untruncated prior there exists
\lambda_{1}=\widetilde{\Theta}(1)
such that if
\lambda\leq\lambda_{1}
it holds for some
D=\Theta(\log n)
,

\mathrm{MMSE}^{\leq D}_{\widetilde{P}}(\lambda)=(1+o(1))\mathrm{MMSE}^{\textrm{trivial}}=(1+o(1))k^{r}.

By Proposition
A.3
,

\big|\mathrm{MMSE}^{\leq D}_{P}(\lambda)-\mathrm{MMSE}^{\leq D}_{\widetilde{P}}(\lambda)\big|\leq e^{-\Theta(k)}

and therefore

\mathrm{MMSE}^{\leq D}_{P}(\lambda)=(1+o(1))\mathrm{MMSE}^{\textrm{trivial}}=(1+o(1))k^{r}.

Moreover, for any
1\leq q=s^{3}=n^{o(1)},
and the sequence
a_{n}(s)=(s+2)^{3}-s^{3},n\in\mathbb{N}
we have from Lemma
10.5
,

\Delta_{a_{n}(s)}\mathcal{F}^{P}_{\rm ann,\lambda}(q)=-\lambda+\Theta\left(\frac{\log n}{q^{2/3}}\right),

(A.28)

where the
\Theta
hides universal constants (independent of
q
). In particular, by shrinking further
\lambda_{1}
if necessary, assuming
\lambda_{1}=o(1/\log n)
, we have for any
\lambda\leq\lambda_{1},
that the annealed FP potential is decreasing in the interval
q=s^{3}\in[0,\Theta((\log n)^{3})].
Now by Theorem
A.4
,

\Delta_{a_{n}(s)}\mathcal{F}^{\widetilde{P}}_{\rm ann,\lambda}(q)=(1+o(1))\Delta_{a_{n}(s)}\mathcal{F}^{P}_{\rm ann,\lambda}(q),

(A.29)

and therefore the same monotonic behavior holds for the annealed FP potential of the truncated prior.

Now, for some
\lambda_{2}=\widetilde{\Theta}(1)
, by Corollary
A.7
it holds that if
\lambda\geq\lambda_{2}
for any
D=\Theta(\log n)
and the truncated prior that,

\mathrm{MMSE}^{\leq D}_{P}(\lambda)=o(\mathrm{MMSE}^{\textrm{trivial}}).

Moreover, fix any
1\leq q=s^{3}=n^{o(1)},
and the sequence
a_{n}(s)=(s+2)^{3}-s^{3},n\in\mathbb{N}
. We have from (
A.28
) and assuming
\lambda_{2}=\omega(\log n)
, that for
\lambda\geq\lambda_{2},
the annealed FP potential for the untruncated prior is decreasing for all
1\leq q=n^{o(1)}.
Now by Theorem
A.4
, the same monotonic behavior holds for the annealed FP potential of the truncated prior.

∎

A.3
The quenched FP potential is not decreasing in the “Low-degree easy” phase

We now state and prove the following theorem, proving that even in the “low-degree” easy regime
\lambda=k^{\Omega(1)}
where the low-degree MMSE is trivial, the quenched FP potential remains non-monotonic. In particular, the monotonocity of the quenched FP potential fails to capture the low-degree MMSE phase transition for this model. Strikingly, as we discussed in the previous section, the monotonicity of the annealed FP potential does track it accurately though.

Theorem A.10
.

There exist constants
c,C,C^{\prime}>0
such that the following holds. If
\beta>0
is a small enough constant,
k=n^{\beta+o(1)},
then for any
q\geq 1,

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}(q)-\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}(0)

\displaystyle\geq-\lambda^{2}q-Ck\log(n/k)+c\lambda kq^{1/3}/\sqrt{\log n}.

In particular if
\lambda=k^{\varepsilon+o(1)}
for any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
(i.e., for values of
\lambda\geq n^{\Omega(1)}\lambda_{\mathrm{ALG}}
) then as long as
C(\log n)^{3}\leq q\leq(\log n)^{d}
for some
d>3,
it holds

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}(q)-\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}(0)\geq k^{1+\Omega(1)}>0,

and therefore, for all
d>3,
the quenched FP potential
\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}(q)
is not a decreasing function for
q\in[0,(\log n)^{d}].

A.3.1
Key Lemmas

To prove this we first need a few lemmas. Of crucial importance is the random curve

\Gamma(q^{\prime},m):=\max_{v^{\prime}\in S_{n,m}:\langle v,v^{\prime}\rangle=q^{\prime}}\langle\mathrm{vec}(v^{\prime\otimes 3}),Z\rangle,q^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R},m\in[n]

where
S_{n,m}
is the set of
m
-sparse vectors in
\{-1,0,1\}^{n}
and as usual
Z
has i.i.d.
N(0,1)
entries.

Lemma A.11
.

Suppose
\beta>0
is a small enough constant. Then there exists a constant
C>0
such that for any
1\leq q^{\prime}=o(k),k/2\leq m\leq 2k
and for any growing sequence
A_{n}
we have with probability at least
1-Ce^{-A_{n}/2},

\displaystyle\Gamma(q^{\prime},m)\leq\sqrt{m^{3}}\sqrt{2\log\left(k\binom{k}{q^{\prime}}\binom{n-k}{m-q^{\prime}}\right)-\log(m\log(n/m))+A_{n}}.

(A.30)

In particular, for
C>0
large enough, if
A_{n}\geq C\log n,
we have that for any
1\leq q^{\prime}=o(k)
, with probability
1-e^{-\Theta(A_{n})}
, (
A.30
) holds simultaneously for all
k/2\leq m\leq 2k
.

Moreover, for any
c>0
if
\alpha>0
is small enough then for all
m
with
k/2\leq m\leq 2k,

\mathbb{E}[\Gamma(q^{\prime},m)1(\Gamma(q^{\prime},m)\geq n^{c})]=n^{-\Omega(1)}.

Proof.

For any
x\in\{-1,0,1\}^{n}
which is
m
-sparse, observe that
\langle Z,\mathrm{vec}(x^{\otimes 3})\rangle
is a mean-zero Gaussian with variance
m^{3}
.

Notice that the number of
x\in\{-1,0,1\}^{n}
which is
m
-sparse and satisfies
\langle x,v\rangle=q^{\prime},
is

\sum_{b\geq q^{\prime}}\binom{b}{q^{\prime}}\binom{k}{b}\binom{n-k}{m-b}\leq k\binom{k}{q^{\prime}}\binom{n-k}{m-q^{\prime}}.

We first simplify the summand using the identity

\binom{k}{b}\binom{b}{q^{\prime}}=\binom{k}{q^{\prime}}\binom{k-q^{\prime}}{b-q^{\prime}},

Hence, writing
t=b-q^{\prime}
,

\displaystyle\sum_{b\geq q^{\prime}}\binom{b}{q^{\prime}}\binom{k}{b}\binom{n-k}{m-b}

\displaystyle=\binom{k}{q^{\prime}}\sum_{t\geq 0}\binom{k-q^{\prime}}{t}\binom{n-k}{(m-q^{\prime})-t}.

By Vandermonde’s identity,

\sum_{t\geq 0}\binom{k-q^{\prime}}{t}\binom{n-k}{(m-q^{\prime})-t}=\binom{(k-q^{\prime})+(n-k)}{m-q^{\prime}}=\binom{n-q^{\prime}}{m-q^{\prime}},

which proves the exact equality

\sum_{b\geq q^{\prime}}\binom{b}{q^{\prime}}\binom{k}{b}\binom{n-k}{m-b}=\binom{k}{q^{\prime}}\binom{n-q^{\prime}}{m-q^{\prime}}.

For the inequality, it suffices to show

\binom{n-q^{\prime}}{m-q^{\prime}}\leq k\binom{n-k}{m-q^{\prime}}.

Let
r:=m-q^{\prime}
. Since
m\leq 2k
and
q^{\prime}\geq 1
, we have
0\leq r\leq 2k
.
Using the product representation of binomial coefficients,

\displaystyle\frac{\binom{n-q^{\prime}}{r}}{\binom{n-k}{r}}

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=0}^{r-1}\frac{n-q^{\prime}-i}{n-k-i}=\prod_{i=0}^{r-1}\Big(1+\frac{k-q^{\prime}}{n-k-i}\Big)\;\leq\;\exp\!\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{r-1}\frac{k-q^{\prime}}{n-k-i}\Big).

Since
r\leq 2k
and
i\leq r-1\leq 2k-1
, we have
n-k-i\geq n-3k
. Thus

\sum_{i=0}^{r-1}\frac{k-q^{\prime}}{n-k-i}\leq\frac{r(k-q^{\prime})}{n-3k}\leq\frac{2k^{2}}{n-3k}.

Assume
k\leq n^{\beta}
with
\beta<\frac{1}{2}
. Then
k^{2}/n=n^{2\beta-1}\to 0
, and also
3k=o(n)
,
so for all sufficiently large
n
we have
\frac{2k^{2}}{n-3k}\leq\log 2
. Therefore, for all large
n
,

\frac{\binom{n-q^{\prime}}{r}}{\binom{n-k}{r}}\leq e^{\log 2}=2\leq k,

which implies
\binom{n-q^{\prime}}{r}\leq k\binom{n-k}{r}
and completes the proof.

Hence, by a union bound and Mill’s ratio bound, the probability that (
A.30
) does not hold for some
x
which is
m
-sparse and satisfies
\langle x,v\rangle=q^{\prime},
is at most

\displaystyle\frac{1+o(1)}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\frac{\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}\log\left(m\log(n/m)\right)-\frac{1}{2}A_{n}\right)}{\sqrt{2\log\left(k\binom{k}{q^{\prime}}\binom{n-k}{m-q^{\prime}}\right)-\log\left(m\log(n/m)\right)+A_{n}}}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\frac{1+o(1)}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\sqrt{\frac{m\log(n/m)}{2\log\left(k\binom{k}{q^{\prime}}\binom{n-k}{m-q^{\prime}}\right)-\log\left(m\log(m/s)\right)+A_{n}}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}A_{n}\right)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle O\left(\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}A_{n}\right)\right).

For the second to last equality we used that since
q^{\prime}=o(k)
and hence
q^{\prime}=o(m)
, it holds

\log\left(\binom{k}{q^{\prime}}\binom{n-k}{m-q^{\prime}}\right)\geq\log\binom{n-k}{m-q^{\prime}}\geq(1-o(1))m\log(n/m)

This shows (
A.30
).

Fix
q^{\prime}=o(k)
and let

\mathcal{S}:=\{m\in\mathbb{Z}:\ k/2\leq m\leq 2k\},\qquad|\mathcal{S}|\leq 2k.

For each fixed
m\in\mathcal{S}
, (
A.30
) yields

\Pr\big(\eqref{eq:1mm}\ \text{fails for this }s\big)\leq Ce^{-A_{n}/2}.

A union bound over
m\in\mathcal{S}
gives

\Pr\Big(\exists m\in\mathcal{S}:\ \eqref{eq:1mm}\ \text{fails}\Big)\leq\sum_{m\in\mathcal{S}}Ce^{-A_{n}/2}\leq 2k\,Ce^{-A_{n}/2}.

In particular, if
A_{n}=\omega(\log n)
, with
k=n^{\beta}
for some fixed
\beta>0
, then

2kCe^{-A_{n}/2}=\exp(-\Theta(A_{n}))
, hence (
A.30
) holds
simultaneously for all
m\in[k/2,2k]
with probability
1-e^{-\Theta(A_{n})}
.

Fix any
c>0
and take
\beta>0
small enough so that
2\beta<c
.
For
m\in[k/2,2k]
define the threshold

B_{q^{\prime},m}:=\sqrt{m^{3}}\sqrt{2\log\!\Big(k\binom{k}{q^{\prime}}\binom{n-k}{m-q^{\prime}}\Big)-\log\big(m\log(p/m)+A_{n}\big)}.

Using
\log\binom{k}{q^{\prime}}\leq q^{\prime}\log(ek/q^{\prime})\leq k\log(ek)
(since
q^{\prime}=o(k)
),

\log\binom{n-k}{m-q^{\prime}}\leq m\log(en/m)
(since
m\asymp k
), and
m\asymp k
,
we obtain for some constant
C>0
, that

\log\Big(k\binom{k}{q^{\prime}}\binom{n-k}{m-q^{\prime}}\Big)\leq Ck\log n;

hence for some absolute constant
C^{\prime}>0
,

B_{q^{\prime},m}\leq C^{\prime}k^{3/2}\sqrt{k\log n}=C^{\prime}k^{2}\sqrt{\log n}.

Since
k=n^{\beta}
and
2\beta<c
, for all large
n
we have

B_{q^{\prime},m}\leq\tfrac{1}{2}n^{c}
, uniformly over
m\in[k/2,2k]
. Therefore,
for all such
n
,

\Pr\big(\Gamma(q^{\prime},m)\geq n^{c}\big)\leq\Pr\big(\Gamma(q^{\prime},m)>B_{q^{\prime},m}\big)\leq Ce^{-A_{n}/2}.

Moreover,
\Gamma(q^{\prime},m)
is the maximum of

k\binom{k}{q^{\prime}}\binom{n-k}{m-q^{\prime}}
centered subgaussian
variables with proxy variance
\leq cm^{3}
with some absolute constant
c>0
(see the proof of
(
A.30
)). By the standard maximal inequality for subgaussian
families (e.g.
[
33
, Prop. 2.5.2]
), there are constants
C,C^{\prime}>0
, such that

\mathbb{E}[\Gamma(q^{\prime},m)^{2}]\leq Cm^{3}\log\!\Big(k\binom{k}{q^{\prime}}\binom{n-k}{m-q^{\prime}}\Big)\leq C^{\prime}k^{3}\cdot(k\log n)=C^{\prime}k^{4}\log n.

Applying Cauchy–Schwarz inequality, it gives that for some absolute constant
\widetilde{C}>0
,

\mathbb{E}\big[\Gamma(q^{\prime},m)\mathbf{1}\{\Gamma(q^{\prime},m)\geq n^{c}\}\big]\leq\big(\mathbb{E}[\Gamma(q^{\prime},m)^{2}]\big)^{1/2}\,\Pr(\Gamma(q^{\prime},m)\geq n^{c})^{1/2}\leq\widetilde{C}k^{2}\sqrt{\log n}\,e^{-A_{n}/4}.

Taking, e.g.,
A_{n}=(\log n)^{2}
(or any
A_{n}\to\infty
fast enough) yields

k^{2}\sqrt{\log n}\,e^{-A_{n}/4}=n^{-\Omega(1)}
, uniformly for all

m\in[k/2,2k]
, completing the proof.
∎

We can also prove the following.

Lemma A.12
.

Suppose
0<\beta<1/5
.
Then for any
k/2\leq m\leq 2k
and for any growing sequence
A_{n}
we have with probability
1-n^{-\Omega(1)},

\Gamma(0,m)\geq\sqrt{m^{3}}\sqrt{2\log\binom{n}{m}-\log(m\log(n/m))-A_{n}}.

Proof.

This follows by applying
[
9
, Proposition 9.4]
for
t=3
, sparsity level
m
, and
\ell=1/2
, when
\beta<1/5
and directly checking the with high probability guarantee
1-n^{-\Omega(1)}
out of the second moment method (specifically, in
[
9
, Lemma 9.7]
all
o(1)
terms can be straightforwardly checked to be
n^{-\Omega(1)}
). It should be noted that while formally
[
9
]
prove
[
9
, Proposition 9.4]
only for integer
\ell
, the proof follows mutatis mutandis in the non-integer case for
\ell
. Moreover, in the case
\ell=1/2,

\Gamma_{[0,\ell]}
from
[
9
, Proposition 9.4]
becomes equal to
\Gamma(0,m)/\sqrt{m^{3}}
in our notation yielding our lemma.
∎

Based on our prior, let
p(m,q^{\prime})=\mathbb{P}_{v}(\langle v,v^{\prime}\rangle=q^{\prime},\|v^{\prime}\|_{0}=m).

Lemma A.13
.

It holds

\min_{k/2\leq m\leq 2k}p(m,0)\geq e^{-\Theta(k)}.

Proof.

Fix
m\in[k/2,2k]
and consider the event

E_{m}:=\{\|v^{\prime}\|_{0}=m\}\cap\{\mathrm{supp}(v^{\prime})\cap\mathrm{supp}(v)=\emptyset\}.

On
E_{m}
we have
\langle v,v^{\prime}\rangle=0
, hence

p(m,0)\geq\mathbb{P}(E_{m}).

Condition on
v^{\prime}
, then the support of
\{\|v^{\prime}\|_{0}=m\}
is some set

S\subset[n]
with
|S|=m
. By independence across coordinates,

\mathbb{P}\big(\mathrm{supp}(v^{\prime})\cap\mathrm{supp}(v)=\emptyset\big|\ \|v^{\prime}\|_{0}=m,\mathrm{supp}(v^{\prime})=S\big)=(1-k/n)^{m}.

Therefore

\mathbb{P}(E_{m})=\mathbb{P}(\|v^{\prime}\|_{0}=m)(1-k/n)^{m}.

We have
\|v^{\prime}\|_{0}\sim\mathrm{Bin}(n,k/n)
with mean
k
.
For any
m\in[k/2,2k]
, the standard local lower bound for the binomial
distribution (see, e.g.,
[
33
, Prop. 2.1.2]
) yields

\mathbb{P}(\|v^{\prime}\|_{0}=m)\geq\exp(-C_{1}k)

for some absolute constant
C_{1}>0
and all large
n
.

Since
m\leq 2k
and
\log(1-x)\geq-x-x^{2}
for small
x
,

(1-k/n)^{m}=\exp\big(m\log(1-k/n)\big)\geq\exp\Big(-\frac{km}{n}-O\Big(\frac{k^{2}m}{n^{2}}\Big)\Big)\geq\exp\Big(-O\Big(\frac{k^{2}}{n}\Big)\Big).

Because
\beta<1/2
, we have
k^{2}/n=n^{2\beta-1+o(1)}=o(1)
, hence

(1-k/n)^{m}=\exp(-o(k)).

Combining the bounds,

p(m,0)\geq\mathbb{P}(E_{m})\geq\exp(-C_{1}k)\cdot\exp(-o(k))=\exp(-\Theta(k)),

uniformly for all
m\in[k/2,2k]
. Taking the minimum over
m

completes the proof.
∎

A.3.2
Proof of Theorem
A.10

By the definition of the quenched FP potential, for any
q=(q^{\prime})^{3}>0
, we have:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}(q)=-\mathbb{E}_{v,Z}\left[\log\mathbb{E}_{v^{\prime}}\left[\mathbf{1}_{\{\langle v,v^{\prime}\rangle=q^{\prime}\}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\|Y-\sqrt{\lambda}\mathrm{vec}((v^{\prime})^{\otimes 3})\|_{2}^{2}\right)\right]\right]

where
Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{N})
. Expanding the squared norm and dropping terms independent of
q=(q^{\prime})^{3}
, the difference in the free energy simplifies to:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}(q)-\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}(0)

\displaystyle=-\lambda^{2}q-\mathbb{E}_{v,Z}\left[\log\frac{\mathbb{E}_{v^{\prime}}\left[\mathbf{1}_{\{\langle v,v^{\prime}\rangle=q^{\prime}\}}e^{\lambda\langle\mathrm{vec}((v^{\prime})^{\otimes 3}),Z\rangle}\right]}{\mathbb{E}_{v^{\prime}}\left[\mathbf{1}_{\{\langle v,v^{\prime}\rangle=0\}}e^{\lambda\langle\mathrm{vec}((v^{\prime})^{\otimes 3}),Z\rangle}\right]}\right].

To bound the ratio of these partition functions, we stratify the inner expectations over the sparsity levels
m=\|v^{\prime}\|_{0}\in[\lfloor k/2\rfloor,2k]
. For the numerator, we bound the sum by the maximum over
m
and the size of the support
S_{n,m}
:

\displaystyle\sum_{m=\lfloor k/2\rfloor}^{2k}\mathbb{E}_{v^{\prime}}\left[\mathbf{1}_{\{\langle v,v^{\prime}\rangle=q^{\prime},\|v^{\prime}\|_{0}=m\}}e^{\lambda\langle\mathrm{vec}((v^{\prime})^{\otimes 3}),Z\rangle}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\max_{m\in[\lfloor k/2\rfloor,2k]}\left(e^{\lambda\Gamma(q^{\prime},m)}p(m,q^{\prime})\right)\cdot\sum_{m=\lfloor k/2\rfloor}^{2k}|S_{n,m}|.

Since the total number of sparse supports is generously bounded by
\sum_{m=\lfloor k/2\rfloor}^{2k}|S_{n,m}|\leq 2k\binom{n}{2k}2^{2k}
, its logarithm is
O(k\log(n/k))
.

For the denominator, we lower bound the sum by its maximum term:

\displaystyle\sum_{m=\lfloor k/2\rfloor}^{2k}\mathbb{E}_{v^{\prime}}\left[\mathbf{1}_{\{\langle v,v^{\prime}\rangle=0,\|v^{\prime}\|_{0}=m\}}e^{\lambda\langle\mathrm{vec}((v^{\prime})^{\otimes 3}),Z\rangle}\right]

\displaystyle\geq\max_{m\in[\lfloor k/2\rfloor,2k]}\left(e^{\lambda\Gamma(0,m)}p(m,0)\right).

Taking the ratio of these bounds and applying the logarithm, the free energy difference is bounded by:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}(q)-\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}(0)

\displaystyle\geq-\lambda^{2}q-O\left(k\log\frac{n}{k}\right)-\mathbb{E}_{Z}\left[\max_{m\in[\lfloor k/2\rfloor,2k]}\lambda\big(\Gamma(q^{\prime},m)-\Gamma(0,m)\big)\right]-\max_{m\in[\lfloor k/2\rfloor,2k]}\log\frac{1}{p(m,0)}.

From Lemma
A.13
, we established that
\min_{k/2\leq m\leq 2k}p(m,0)\geq e^{-\Theta(k)}
, which absorbs into the existing
O(k\log(n/k))
term. Furthermore, with probability
1-n^{-\Omega(1)}
, the deviation between the maximums is bounded simultaneously for all
k/2\leq m\leq 2k
by Lemmas
A.11
and
A.12
:

\displaystyle\Gamma(q^{\prime},m)-\Gamma(0,m)

\displaystyle\leq-\frac{m(q^{\prime}-A_{n}-\log k)}{\Theta(\sqrt{\log n})}.

Applying Lemma
A.11
to control the tail expectation, we find that for
m>2(A_{n}+\log k)
and since
q=(q^{\prime})^{3},

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{Z}\left[\max_{m\in[\lfloor k/2\rfloor,2k]}\lambda\big(\Gamma(q,s)-\Gamma(0,s)\big)\right]

\displaystyle\leq-\Theta\left(\frac{\lambda kq^{1/3}}{\sqrt{\log n}}\right)+o(1).

Substituting this expectation bound back into the free energy inequality yields that for some constants
C,c>0
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}(q)-\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}(0)

\displaystyle\geq-\lambda^{2}q-Ck\log\frac{n}{k}+c\lambda\frac{kq^{1/3}}{\sqrt{\log n}}.

Finally, we choose
A_{n}=C\log n
for a large enough
C>0
. If we set
\lambda=k^{\varepsilon+o(1)}
for any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and evaluate at
q\geq(2C)^{3}(\log n)^{3}
, the positive term strictly dominates. Thus, we conclude:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}(q)-\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}(0)

\displaystyle\geq k^{1+\Omega(1)}>0.

Appendix B
Proof of auxiliary lemmas

In this section we prove Propositions
6.3
and
7.3
, and Lemmas
A.2
and
6.8
.

Proof of Lemma
A.2
.

We use the standard Gaussian tail bound: for
Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
and
t\geq 0
,

\mathbb{P}(|Z|\geq t)\leq 2e^{-t^{2}/2}.

(B.1)

Recall that,
d_{i}=\lambda v_{i}+Z_{i}
with
Z_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
i.i.d. If
v_{i}=0
, then
d_{i}=Z_{i}
. The event
\widehat{v}_{i}\neq v_{i}
is
\{|d_{i}|\geq\tau\}
, hence
by a union bound and (
B.1
),

\mathbb{P}\big(\exists i:\ v_{i}=0,\ \widehat{v}_{i}\neq 0\big)\leq\sum_{i:\,v_{i}=0}\mathbb{P}(|Z_{i}|\geq\tau)\leq n\cdot 2e^{-\tau^{2}/2}=2n^{-2},

(B.2)

since
\tau=\sqrt{6\log n}
.

Next, fix
i\in S={\rm supp}(v)
. Then
v_{i}\in\{\pm 1\}
and
d_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(\pm\lambda,1)
.
The event
\widehat{v}_{i}=0
is
\{|d_{i}|<\tau\}
, so

\mathbb{P}(|d_{i}|<\tau)\leq 2\exp\!\Big(-\frac{1}{2}(\lambda-\tau)_{+}^{2}\Big)\leq 2e^{-\tau^{2}/2},

where the first inequality is the same two-tail bound used in your original proof, and the
second uses
\lambda\geq 2\tau
so that
\lambda-\tau\geq\tau
.
Union bounding over
i\in S
and using
|S|\leq 2k
yields

\mathbb{P}\big(\exists i\in S:\ \widehat{v}_{i}=0\big)\leq|S|\cdot 2e^{-\tau^{2}/2}\leq 4k\,e^{-\tau^{2}/2}=4k\,n^{-3}.

(B.3)

Now, fix
i\in S
. If
v_{i}=1
then
\widehat{v}_{i}=-1
implies
d_{i}\leq-\tau
;
if
v_{i}=-1
then
\widehat{v}_{i}=1
implies
d_{i}\geq\tau
.
Hence, for each
i\in S
,

\mathbb{P}(\widehat{v}_{i}=-v_{i})\leq\mathbb{P}\big(\mathcal{N}(\lambda,1)\leq-\tau\big)+\mathbb{P}\big(\mathcal{N}(-\lambda,1)\geq\tau\big)\leq 2\exp\!\Big(-\frac{1}{2}(\lambda+\tau)^{2}\Big),

by (
B.1
). Under
\lambda\geq 2\tau
, we have
\lambda+\tau\geq 3\tau
, so

2\exp\!\Big(-\frac{1}{2}(\lambda+\tau)^{2}\Big)\leq 2\exp\!\Big(-\frac{1}{2}(3\tau)^{2}\Big)=2e^{-27\log n}=2n^{-27}.

Union bounding over
i\in S
and using
|S|\leq 2k
gives

\mathbb{P}\big(\exists i\in S:\ \widehat{v}_{i}=-v_{i}\big)\leq|S|\cdot 2n^{-27}\leq 4k\,n^{-27}.

(B.4)

Finally,

\{\widehat{v}\neq v\}\subseteq\big\{\exists i:\ v_{i}=0,\ \widehat{v}_{i}\neq 0\big\}\ \cup\ \big\{\exists i\in S:\ \widehat{v}_{i}=0\big\}\ \cup\ \big\{\exists i\in S:\ \widehat{v}_{i}=-v_{i}\big\}.

Combining the bounds from (
B.2
), (
B.3
), (
B.4
) yields (
A.2
).
∎

Proof of Proposition
6.3
.

Set
V_{i}=Z_{i}
for
i\neq t
, and
V_{t}=aX+bY
. Fix a partition
\pi\in\mathcal{P}([n])
and let

B_{\pi}
be the (unique) block of
\pi
that contains
t
. Then

\mathbb{E}\Big[\prod_{i\in B_{\pi}}V_{i}\Big]=\mathbb{E}\Big[(aX+bY)\prod_{i\in B_{\pi}\setminus\{t\}}Z_{i}\Big]=a\,\mathbb{E}\Big[X\prod_{i\in B_{\pi}\setminus\{t\}}Z_{i}\Big]+b\,\mathbb{E}\Big[Y\prod_{i\in B_{\pi}\setminus\{t\}}Z_{i}\Big],

by linearity of expectation. For every other block
B\in\pi
with
t\notin B
, we have

\mathbb{E}[\prod_{i\in B}V_{i}]=\mathbb{E}[\prod_{i\in B}Z_{i}]
, which does not depend on
X
or
Y
.

Therefore, for this fixed
\pi
,

\prod_{B\in\pi}\mathbb{E}\Big[\prod_{i\in B}V_{i}\Big]=a\Bigg(\mathbb{E}\Big[X\!\!\prod_{i\in B_{\pi}\setminus\{t\}}Z_{i}\Big]\prod_{B\in\pi:\,B\neq B_{\pi}}\mathbb{E}\Big[\prod_{i\in B}Z_{i}\Big]\Bigg)+b\Bigg(\mathbb{E}\Big[Y\!\!\prod_{i\in B_{\pi}\setminus\{t\}}Z_{i}\Big]\prod_{B\in\pi:\,B\neq B_{\pi}}\mathbb{E}\Big[\prod_{i\in B}Z_{i}\Big]\Bigg).

Multiplying by the coefficient
(|\pi|-1)!\,(-1)^{|\pi|-1}
and summing over all partitions
\pi
,
we can pull out the scalars
a
and
b
(the sum is finite), and using Proposition
6.4
, we obtain exactly the claimed linearity in the
t
-th argument.
∎

Proof of Lemma
6.8
.

By expanding the squared norm,

\mathrm{MMSE}^{\leq D}_{X}=\inf_{f_{1},\dots,f_{n}\in\mathbb{R}[Y]_{\leq D}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\big[(f_{i}(Y)-X_{i})^{2}\big]=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\inf_{f_{i}\in\mathbb{R}[Y]_{\leq D}}\mathbb{E}\big[(f_{i}(Y)-X_{i})^{2}\big].

For each
i
expand the square:

\mathbb{E}[(f_{i}(Y)-X_{i})^{2}]=\mathbb{E}[f_{i}(Y)^{2}]-2\mathbb{E}[f_{i}(Y)X_{i}]+\mathbb{E}[X_{i}^{2}].

For any fixed
f_{i}\not\equiv 0
, minimize over the scalar
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
by considering

g_{i}=\alpha f_{i}
:

\inf_{\alpha\in\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{E}[(\alpha f_{i}(Y)-X_{i})^{2}]=\mathbb{E}[X_{i}^{2}]-\frac{\mathbb{E}[f_{i}(Y)X_{i}]^{2}}{\mathbb{E}[f_{i}(Y)^{2}]}.

Indeed, the quadratic in
\alpha
is

\mathbb{E}[(\alpha f_{i}-X_{i})^{2}]=\alpha^{2}\mathbb{E}[f_{i}^{2}]-2\alpha\mathbb{E}[f_{i}X_{i}]+\mathbb{E}[X_{i}^{2}],

whose minimum occurs at
\alpha^{\star}=\mathbb{E}[f_{i}X_{i}]/\mathbb{E}[f_{i}^{2}]
. Now take the infimum over
f_{i}\in\mathbb{R}[Y]_{\leq D}
:

\mathrm{MMSE}^{\leq D}_{X_{i}}=\inf_{f_{i}\in\mathbb{R}_{\leq D}}\inf_{\alpha\in\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{E}[(\alpha f_{i}(Y)-X_{i})^{2}]=\mathbb{E}[X_{i}^{2}]-\sup_{f\in\mathbb{R}_{\leq D}}\frac{\mathbb{E}[f_{i}(Y)X_{i}]^{2}}{\mathbb{E}[f_{i}(Y)^{2}]}.

Finally, normalize
f_{i}
by setting
\tilde{f}_{i}:=f_{i}/\sqrt{\mathbb{E}[f_{i}(Y)^{2}]}
(when
\mathbb{E}[f(Y)^{2}]\neq 0
), so

\sup_{f_{i}\in\mathbb{R}_{\leq D}}\frac{\mathbb{E}[f_{i}(Y)X_{i}]^{2}}{\mathbb{E}[f_{i}(Y)^{2}]}=\Big(\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{f}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}_{\leq D}\\
\mathbb{E}[\tilde{f}_{i}(Y)^{2}]=1\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}[\tilde{f}_{i}(Y)X_{i}]\Big)^{2}=\big(\mathrm{Corr}^{\leq D}_{P_{0},i}\big)^{2}.

Substituting gives:

\inf_{f_{i}\in\mathbb{R}[Y]_{\leq D}}\mathbb{E}\big[(f_{i}(Y)-X_{i})^{2}\big]=\mathbb{E}[X_{i}^{2}]-\big(\mathrm{Corr}^{\leq D}_{P_{0},i}\big)^{2}.

Summing over
i
yields

\mathrm{MMSE}^{\leq D}_{X}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}[X_{i}^{2}]-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(\mathrm{Corr}^{\leq D}_{P_{0},i}\big)^{2}=\mathbb{E}\|X\|^{2}-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(\mathrm{Corr}^{\leq D}_{P_{0},i}\big)^{2}.

For the second claim consider arbitrary
(f_{1},\dots,f_{n})\in\mathbb{R}[Y]_{\leq D}^{n}
. By the definition of

\mathrm{Corr}^{\leq D}_{P_{0},i}
and Cauchy–Schwarz inequality, for each
i
we have

\mathbb{E}[f_{i}(Y)X_{i}]\leq\sqrt{\mathbb{E}[f_{i}(Y)^{2}]}\mathrm{Corr}^{\leq D}_{P_{0},i}.

Summing over
i
and applying Cauchy–Schwarz inequality again gives

\mathbb{E}\Big[\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{i}(Y)X_{i}\Big]\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sqrt{\mathbb{E}[f_{i}(Y)^{2}]}\mathrm{Corr}^{\leq D}_{P_{0},i}\leq\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{i}(Y)^{2}}\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(\mathrm{Corr}^{\leq D}_{P_{0},i}\big)^{2}}.

Under the constraint
\mathbb{E}\sum_{i}f_{i}(Y)^{2}=1
, we obtain the upper bound

\mathrm{Corr}^{\leq D}_{P_{0}}\leq\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(\mathrm{Corr}^{\leq D}_{P_{0},i}\big)^{2}}.

For the matching lower bound, fix
\varepsilon>0
. For each
i
choose

g_{i}\in\mathbb{R}[Y]_{\leq D}
with
\mathbb{E}[g_{i}(Y)^{2}]=1
and

\mathbb{E}[g_{i}(Y)X_{i}]\geq\mathrm{Corr}^{\leq D}_{P_{0},i}-\varepsilon

(which is possible by the definition of the supremum).
Define

f_{i}(Y):=\frac{\mathrm{Corr}^{\leq D}_{P_{0},i}}{\sqrt{\sum_{j=1}^{n}(\mathrm{Corr}^{\leq D}_{P_{0},j})^{2}}}g_{i}(Y).

Then
\mathbb{E}\sum_{i}f_{i}(Y)^{2}=1
, and hence

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{i}(Y)X_{i}\Big]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\sum_{j}(\mathrm{Corr}^{\leq D}_{P_{0},j})^{2}}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{Corr}^{\leq D}_{P_{0},i}\,\mathbb{E}[g_{i}(Y)X_{i}]

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{\sqrt{\sum_{j}(\mathrm{Corr}^{\leq D}_{P_{0},j})^{2}}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{Corr}^{\leq D}_{P_{0},i}\,(\mathrm{Corr}^{\leq D}_{P_{0},i}-\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\mathrm{Corr}^{\leq D}_{P_{0},i})^{2}}-\varepsilon\,\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{Corr}^{\leq D}_{P_{0},i}}{\sqrt{\sum_{j=1}^{n}(\mathrm{Corr}^{\leq D}_{P_{0},j})^{2}}}.

Taking the supremum over
f_{i}
and then letting
\varepsilon\downarrow 0

yields

\mathrm{Corr}^{\leq D}_{P_{0}}\geq\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(\mathrm{Corr}^{\leq D}_{P_{0},i}\big)^{2}}.

Combining with the upper bound proves the second claim.
∎

Proof of Proposition
7.3
.

The proof relies on the generating function characterization of the probabilist Hermite polynomials.
By
[
22
]
[Page 253], for any
x,t\in\mathbb{R}
and
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}
,

\sum_{\alpha=0}^{\infty}\frac{t^{\alpha}}{\alpha!}H_{\alpha}(x)=e^{tx-t^{2}/2}
, so the multivariate generating function is given by:

G(t,x)\coloneqq\exp\left(\langle t,x\rangle-\frac{1}{2}|t|^{2}\right)=\prod_{i=1}^{N}e^{t_{i}x_{i}-t_{i}^{2}/2}=\prod_{i=1}^{N}\left(\sum_{\alpha_{i}=0}^{\infty}\frac{t_{i}^{\alpha_{i}}}{\alpha_{i}!}H_{\alpha_{i}}(x_{i})\right)=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{N}}\frac{t^{\alpha}}{\alpha!}H_{\alpha}(x),\quad\forall t\in\mathbb{R}^{N}.

We evaluate the generating function at the shifted argument
x=z+\mu
. By the linearity of the inner product,
\langle t,z+\mu\rangle=\langle t,z\rangle+\langle t,\mu\rangle
, allowing us to factor the exponential term:

\displaystyle G(t,z+\mu)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(\langle t,z\rangle+\langle t,\mu\rangle-\frac{1}{2}|t|^{2}\right)=\exp\left(\langle t,z\rangle-\frac{1}{2}|t|^{2}\right)\cdot\exp\left(\langle t,\mu\rangle\right).

(B.5)

We now expand both factors on the right-hand side of (
B.5
) into their respective power series. The first factor is the generating function for
H_{\gamma}(z)
, and the second is the standard exponential series:

G(t,z+\mu)=\left(\sum_{\gamma\in\mathbb{N}^{N}}\frac{t^{\gamma}}{\gamma!}H_{\gamma}(z)\right)\left(\sum_{\beta\in\mathbb{N}^{N}}\frac{t^{\beta}}{\beta!}\mu^{\beta}\right).

(B.6)

Applying the Cauchy product formula for multivariate power series, we combine the two summations. We introduce the multi-index
\alpha=\gamma+\beta
, which implies
\beta=\alpha-\gamma
. The condition
\beta\in\mathbb{N}^{N}
necessitates that
\gamma\leq\alpha
(component-wise). Thus, we rewrite (
B.6
) as:

G(t,z+\mu)=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{N}}t^{\alpha}\left(\sum_{\gamma\leq\alpha}\frac{H_{\gamma}(z)}{\gamma!}\frac{\mu^{\alpha-\gamma}}{(\alpha-\gamma)!}\right).

(B.7)

Separately, by the definition of the generating function in (
B
) applied to the argument
z+\mu
, the left-hand side is:

G(t,z+\mu)=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{N}}t^{\alpha}\left(\frac{H_{\alpha}(z+\mu)}{\alpha!}\right).

(B.8)

Since the power series representation is unique, we equate the coefficients of
t^{\alpha}
from (
B.7
) and (
B.8
):

\frac{H_{\alpha}(z+\mu)}{\alpha!}=\sum_{\gamma\leq\alpha}\frac{H_{\gamma}(z)}{\gamma!(\alpha-\gamma)!}\mu^{\alpha-\gamma}.

(B.9)

Multiplying both sides by
\alpha!
yields:

H_{\alpha}(z+\mu)=\sum_{\gamma\leq\alpha}\frac{\alpha!}{\gamma!(\alpha-\gamma)!}H_{\gamma}(z)\mu^{\alpha-\gamma}.

(B.10)

Recognizing the multi-index binomial coefficient
\binom{\alpha}{\gamma}=\frac{\alpha!}{\gamma!(\alpha-\gamma)!}
, we obtain the stated identity.
∎
